# Chapter One: The Drowned Lantern

**MARCUS**

The bells woke him, or near enough.

They came down from the Old Hill one after another, deep bronze throats calling across the frozen roofs of the capital, and every stroke landed behind Marcus's eyes like a mallet on a tent peg. He did not open his eyes. Opening them would mean admitting it was morning, and that the jug in his arms was empty, and that the bells were ringing for a reason.

*Let them ring.* Bells were for the living. The dead could not hear them, and the living were mostly liars.

The Drowned Lantern stank of spilled wine, old tallow and the canal. Somewhere a broom scraped stone, patient as a gravedigger. The fire had burned down to a red eye in the ash. Marcus had his boots on the table and his chair tipped back against the wall, and he had found, sometime after the third jug, that if he held very still the world held still with him.

The door burst inward.

Cold came first, a knife of it, carrying snow and the sound of the bells louder and closer. Then boots. Heavy boots, unhurried, iron-shod, the tread of a man who had walked across worse floors than this one, and walked off them again with other men's blood on his heels.

Marcus knew that tread. He had known it since he was six.

"That one, General." Felix. Thin, apologetic Felix, who had been given to Marcus the way a man is given a cough. "The one with the jug."

"I know which one is the prince." The voice was gravel rolled in a barrel. "He's the only man in here who can't pay his tab."

Marcus cracked one eye.

Roderic of the Long Field stood over him like a siege tower over a ditch. He wore mourning black over his mail, the wool already crusted white at the shoulders, and the snow had caught in the grey of his beard. Thirty years of war had cut his face into something that belonged on a coin. He did not look angry. That was the worst of it. He looked at Marcus the way he looked at a bad map.

"Look at you," Roderic said. "Second in line for the throne of Aeridor, and first in line at every bar in it."

"Go away, old man. I'm grieving."

"You're *pickled.*"

The General's boot moved, almost lazily, hooked the back leg of the chair, and kicked.

The world tipped. The wall went away. The floor came up and struck Marcus across the shoulders with all the warmth of a debt collector, and the breath went out of him in a grunt. The jug, by some mercy of the gods, landed on his chest and did not break. He lay among the rushes and the spilled wine and stared at the smoke-black rafters, and the rafters stared back.

"Who ordered the earthquake?" he asked them.

"I've seen corpses with better posture," said Roderic, "and I've just come from a funeral." He crouched, slow, knees cracking like green wood in a fire, until his face hung over Marcus's own. His breath smelled of cold and cloves. "Your brother's funeral, boy. Titus. They are putting him into the hill *right now.* The whole city is standing in the snow for him, the fishwives and the guild-masters and the legion and the beggars on the temple steps. The whole city, save one. The only man in Aeridor who shares his blood."

"I can hear the bells from here."

"Aye. So can your father." Roderic's voice did not rise. It went lower, and harder, like a blade going in. "He asked me where you were. I told him you were at prayer. Felix agreed that was true, after a fashion. You've been on your knees half the night."

From the doorway, very small: "I said *technically*, General."

"I put you on your first horse," Roderic said. "I taught you to hold a sword before you could hold your water. I had higher hopes for you at six."

Marcus got up.

He did it slowly, the way weather gets up over the sea, the way a black squall stands up on the horizon before it comes for the ships. The wine was still in him, but something else had come up through it, something hot and red that had been waiting in the cellar of him since the riders brought the news. His hands were fists. He did not remember making them.

"Say his name again," he said.

Roderic stood too, and looked at him, and for a moment something moved behind the old man's eyes. Pity, perhaps. Or memory. Then it was gone, and there was only the coin-face again.

"*Titus,*" he said. "Who would have been ashamed of you. And who would have hidden it better than I am bothering to."

Marcus charged.

He was young, and he was strong, and there was murder in him like fire in dry thatch. He came across the room the way his ancestors had come up beaches, head down, shoulder low, roaring, with all his weight behind him and nothing at all in front.

Roderic took one step to the left.

It was not a dodge. It was barely a movement. It was the step a man takes around a puddle in the street, without breaking his conversation. Marcus saw it happen, saw the black wool slide past him like a closing door, and understood with perfect, sober clarity that he had been beaten before he had begun, by a man who had been doing this since before he was born.

The window did not step aside.

Glass and lead and old pine frame exploded outward around him. For one long, bright, astonishing heartbeat he was flying, the snow-light all around him, the bells suddenly enormous, the whole grey city spread wide below like a game board. Then the canal took him.

It was warm. That was the last insult. The Lower Canal ran from the hot springs under the Old Hill, and it closed over his head like bathwater, thick and mineral and stinking of sulphur. Steam boiled up around him as he came up spitting, his hair plastered to his face, his good shirt ruined, a cracked roof-tile bobbing beside his ear.

Above him, framed in the broken window, Roderic was settling his cloak on his shoulders.

"Fetch his black cloak, Felix," the General said. "And a towel. He'll want to look his best for his brother."

Felix looked down at the canal. "Should I fetch *him* first, my lord?"

Roderic considered it. Snow drifted past him into the tavern and melted on the floor.

"The water's warm," he said at last. "Give him a minute. It's the first bath he's had all week."

And from the hill, the bells went on tolling for Titus, as if they had all the time in the world.

---

# Chapter Two: The Eulogy

**MARCUS**

The canal water froze in his hair on the climb up the Old Hill.

By the time he reached the Hall of Kings it had turned to a crust of ice that cracked when he moved his head, and his good shirt clung to him like a second, colder skin. Felix had wrapped the black cloak around him at the tavern door, but the cloak was wool, and wool remembers water. It hung from his shoulders heavy as chainmail. He left a trail of wet footprints up the marble, and the footprints steamed.

The hall was full. Of course it was full. Every lord who still had an island to his name was here, and a good many who had lost theirs and were here to be seen not minding. Black silk. Black furs. Gold chains of office, polished for the occasion. The air was thick with incense and wet wool and a thousand breaths, and under it, faint and sweet, was the other smell, the one the incense was for.

Titus lay on the bier beneath the dome, under a cloth of gold. Someone had folded his hands on the hilt of his sword. Someone had combed his hair.

*He hated it combed.*

At the foot of the bier stood the king.

Aurelian of Aeridor had been a tall man once. He was still tall, in the way an old tower is still tall after the siege. Grief had pared him down to the bone and the crown, and his voice, when it carried across the hall, was thin and steady and very tired.

"...a son any father would have been proud to bury," the king said, "and no father should."

Then he saw Marcus.

It went through the hall like wind through barley. Heads turning. Silk rustling. A woman's sharp intake of breath. Marcus felt every eye in the room land on him and slide off again, the way eyes slide off a beggar, or a corpse in the street.

The king said nothing at all. That was worse than any shout.

Marcus walked up the aisle. His boots squelched. Somebody laughed and smothered it. He walked past the lords of the council in their front row, past the Lord Marshal, Gnaeus Varro, broad and grey and ribboned like a prize bull, and he climbed the three steps to the bier and looked down at his brother.

Titus looked younger dead. They always did.

"Marcus." The king, very quietly. "Step down."

"I've come to say a few words, Father." His voice came out rough, sanded by wine and canal water. "It's customary."

"You are drunk."

"I'm *wet.* The drunk is mostly gone." Mostly. Enough was left to keep his tongue loose, and the rest had burned away into something cold and very clear. He turned to face the hall. Four hundred faces. Four hundred black sleeves.

"My brother," he said, "was the best of us. You've all heard that today. It's even true. He was brave and he was patient and he could read a battlefield the way priests read omens, only he was right more often." He found Varro in the front row and held his eyes. "So you'll forgive me for asking how a man like that ends up dead in a ford at Hollowmere, holding a line he told this council, in writing, could not be held."

The silence changed. It had been embarrassed. Now it was afraid.

"He sent three letters," Marcus said. "I've read them. He asked for the Ninth Legion and was given the Fourteenth, because the Ninth was guarding the Lord Marshal's estates on Vessa. He asked for ships and was told the ships were promised elsewhere. He asked to fall back to the hills. He was ordered to hold."

"Prince Marcus—" Varro was rising, red climbing his thick neck.

"So he held." Marcus's voice cracked, and he let it. "He held, my lords. He held until the Tharsians came up out of the water with our own dead marching in front of them. Men from his own company, men he'd buried the week before, walking back across the ford with the river running out of their mouths. He held until there was no one left to hold with. And this council, which has lost us six islands in four years, sat in this hall and argued about grain tariffs."

He looked at his father.

"You didn't kill him," Marcus said. "Tharsis did. But you all handed him the knife and told him to fall on it politely, and he was too good a soldier to say no."

Nobody breathed.

The king's face did not move. Only his hand, on the edge of the bier, tightened until the knuckles stood out like stones in a riverbed.

"Guards," said King Aurelian. "Take the prince to his rooms."

---

**RODERIC**

He had known it would be the feast. Funerals were for show. Feasts were where men got drunk enough to do what they'd been thinking all day.

The guards had not taken Marcus to his rooms. The guards had taken one look at the king's face, and then at the Lord Marshal's, and decided that a prince who had just been sent to his rooms could perhaps find his own way there after the meat. Roderic had watched it happen with the weary contempt of a man who had seen better soldiers make worse decisions.

So now the boy sat at the lowest table, in the steam and the noise of the Lesser Hall, with a cup in his fist and his back to the wall. Good. Someone had taught him that much.

Roderic counted the room the way he counted a field. Long tables, benches, two fires, one great door and a servants' passage behind the screen. No steel. Mourning law: blades were left with the porters at the door, and even the Lord Marshal's knights had surrendered their swords with bad grace. Only the king's guard went armed, and the king's guard had gone with the king.

Ten men at the Lord Marshal's table. Varro's sworn swords, his household knights, big men gone soft at the belly but not at the shoulder. They had been drinking since the bells stopped. They had been looking at Marcus since the first cup.

*Here it comes.*

The biggest of them, a red-bearded slab called Ursus, stood up and swayed down the length of the hall with a cup in his hand and his friends at his back like gulls behind a fishing boat.

"Highness," Ursus said. He made the word sound like something he had stepped in. "The Lord Marshal sends his regards. He says a man who spends the war in taverns might leave the talk of fords to them as have stood in one."

Marcus looked up at him. "Did he stand in this one? I don't remember seeing his name on the list of the dead. I looked."

"Your brother—"

"Careful," said Marcus.

"—would be alive," said Ursus, "if he'd had half his little brother's talent for running."

Roderic had time to think, quite clearly, *Well, that's done it,* and then Marcus came over the table.

He went straight through it, cups and trenchers and a roasted goose flying up around him, and his shoulder took Ursus under the ribs like a ram taking a gate. They went down together in a crash of benches. The hall erupted. Women shrieked. Somebody's dog fled under the high table with the goose.

The other nine came in.

Roderic sighed, set down his cup, and stood.

His knees cracked. His back reminded him, as it did every winter, of a horse that had rolled on him at the Long Field thirty years ago. He paid it no mind. He had fought with worse backs and less reason.

The first knight to reach him was young and fast and stupid, and came in with both fists high. Roderic took the bench beside him by one end and swung it, low and flat, like a scythe through wheat. It took the young knight across both shins and he went over it face-first into the rushes. Two.

*No, one. Ursus is the boy's.*

Marcus was up again, bleeding from the mouth, grinning like a wolf. Ursus had him by the cloak. Marcus let the sodden thing go, spun out of it, and drove his forehead into the bigger man's nose with a sound like a dropped melon. Ursus sat down very suddenly and did not get up.

Two knights hit Marcus from either side. He went down under them, a thrashing heap of black silk and elbows.

Roderic picked up a candlestick. It was iron, as long as his arm, and it had been holding seven candles in memory of the dead prince. He blew them out first. It seemed only respectful. Then he waded in.

He hit the first man across the back of the knees and the second across the wrist, and heard the wrist go. He kicked the first one in the head on the way down, not hard, the way you'd nudge a sleeping dog. Then he hooked the boy by his collar and hauled him up, spitting rushes.

"On your feet, Highness."

"I was *winning.*"

"You were *underneath.*"

They went back to back without a word, the way he'd taught him in the yard when the boy was nine and had to stand on a crate to reach the pell. Five of Varro's men still standing. They had spread out now, careful, the drink burned off by blood. One had found a carving knife. One had a fire iron. They came in together.

Afterward, Roderic could never quite remember the order of it. He remembered the fire iron coming down and the candlestick going up to meet it, iron on iron, the shock of it singing up his arm into his bad shoulder. He remembered putting the man with the carving knife into the fire, briefly, just the seat of his breeches, and the howl he made. He remembered Marcus seizing a knight by the belt and the hair and running him the full length of the high table, through the salt and the bread and the funeral wine, and off the end into the screen, which fell, and showed the servants' passage behind, full of wide-eyed cooks.

He remembered one man left. The last one stood in the middle of the floor with his fists up and looked around at his nine friends groaning in the rushes, and at the old general with the bent candlestick, and at the prince with blood running down his chin and his teeth showing.

The last man lowered his fists. He bowed, quite correctly, to the prince. Then he turned and walked out of the hall.

"Ten," said Marcus, breathing hard.

"Nine. That one had sense."

"Nine and a half. I counted his bow."

The great doors opened.

The king's guard came in with drawn steel, a hedge of it, and behind them came the king. Aurelian stood in the doorway and looked at his hall. The broken tables. The scattered bread. The Lord Marshal's household knights lying among the rushes like fish on a quay. His son, soaked and bleeding, in the middle of it. And his oldest general, holding a twisted iron candlestick that had been lit, not an hour ago, for Titus.

The Lord Marshal pushed past the guards, white to the lips. "Majesty. This is treason. He has assaulted sworn men of the council in mourning hall—"

"I know what he has done, Gnaeus." The king did not look at Varro. He looked at Marcus. "I watched him do it at the bier."

Roderic set down the candlestick.

"Majesty," he said. "I brought him. I kicked him out of a chair in a tavern and dragged him up the hill. Whatever's on him is on me."

For a long moment the king looked at the two of them. The old general who had lost him the field at Hollowmere, as the council told it. The young son who had lost him everything else.

"Then you'll share it," said King Aurelian.

He turned to the hall, to the lords and the guards and the cooks in the passage, and when he spoke, his voice was the voice of the throne and not of a father.

"Cyrene fell to Tharsis eleven days ago. Its shipyards burn. Its people are under the Tharsian heel. My son has opinions about how this war should be fought. Let him fight it." His eyes came back to Marcus, and there was nothing in them at all. "You will take the penal cohort from the harbor prison, the thieves and the deserters and the debtors, and you will sail for Cyrene on the morning tide. You will take it back, or you will not come back. General Roderic will go with you, since he is so eager to share your burdens."

"Majesty," Varro began, "a penal cohort can't take a fortified—"

"No," the king agreed. "It can't."

He walked out. The guards closed around the prince and the general like a fist.

In the passage behind the fallen screen, Felix looked down at the black cloak bundled in his arms, still damp from the canal, and then up at the ceiling, and Roderic heard him say to no one at all:

"...I'll pack for three, then."

"Four."

The voice came from the servants' passage, from behind Felix, and Felix jumped like a hare in a furrow. A woman stepped past him over the fallen screen, lifting her hem clear of the broken trenchers with two fingers, the way a heron lifts its feet out of mud.

Roderic knew her before the firelight found her face. There was only one woman in Aeridor who wore violet to a funeral.

Alana of Corvus. The youngest chair the academy had ever seated, and now the eldest of whatever was left of it, which was, as far as Roderic knew, herself and the clothes she stood in. The robes were good silk gone shabby at the cuffs, stitched with sigils in thread that had once been silver. There was ink on her fingers, ink on her wrist, a smear of it under one ear where she had pushed back her hair with a wet pen. A rod of plain glass hung from her belt like a sword. She had been at the funeral. He remembered now. She had stood at the very back of the Hall of Kings among the refugees from the lost islands, the ones the council seated behind the pillars so the lords would not have to look at what the war had cost.

The king's guard looked at her, and at the prince, and decided she was not their problem. Most men decided that about Alana. Most men were wrong.

Marcus had gone very still.

"Alana," he said.

"Highness." She gave him the bow a scholar gives a lectern. "You've blood on your chin."

He wiped it with the back of his hand and only spread it. Roderic watched the boy's face and saw, with the small sour pleasure of the old, that he was remembering.

Roderic remembered too. The summer the Corvus delegation had come to court, when the war was still a rumor and the academy still stood. A girl of fifteen with ink on her hands even then, following the second prince through the gardens with a book under her arm, laughing too loudly at his jokes. The sonnet she had written him, which he had read aloud at the high table to a roar of laughter, doing her voice. The Midwinter dance, three years after, when she had crossed the whole length of the Lesser Hall to stand before him in a gown she must have saved a year to buy, and he had looked straight through her and asked the herald's daughter instead. Roderic had seen the girl walk back across that floor. He had seen soldiers walk back from worse with straighter shoulders, but not many.

It had been nothing, to Marcus. That was the thing about princes. They broke a dozen small things a day and never heard any of them land.

"Why?" said Marcus. His voice was rough. "I've done nothing for you. Less than nothing. I was..." He stopped. Started again. "I'm grateful. Gods know we need anyone who can hold a pen and a knife. But I haven't earned it. Not from you."

"No," said Alana. "You haven't."

She said it the way she might have corrected a sum on a slate. Marcus flinched as if she had slapped him, which, Roderic thought, would have been kinder.

"Let us be precise, since nobody else in this hall has bothered all day." She folded her inky hands. "You were a cruel, careless boy, and from what I've seen tonight you've grown into a cruel, careless man with a better left hand. I do not forgive you. I have not thought about you, in any useful sense, in years. This is not about *you*." She leaned on the word as if pinning it to a board. "Tharsis took Corvus two winters ago. I watched the library burn from a fishing boat. Eleven hundred years of collected learning, and the smoke smelled like any other smoke. Everyone I taught is dead, Highness. Some of them more than once. I have seen what walks out of the water at Hollowmere because I have walked behind it with a notebook, and I want to *kill* it. I want to kill the men who make it. That is the beginning and the end of my reasons."

"Cyrene's a death sentence," Marcus said quietly. "You heard my father."

"I heard him. I agree with him. A penal cohort, a disgraced general and a drunk. We will very probably all die." She shrugged, a small, academic movement. "I find I don't much mind. There is not a great deal left I'd be sorry to leave. And there is one consolation in it." For the first time something like a smile touched the corner of her mouth, thin as a paper cut. "If the odds are what I think they are, I may live long enough to watch *you* die first. I'd like a good seat."

Nobody said anything. In the hearth a log broke and settled in a burst of sparks.

Alana turned to Felix as if the prince had already been dismissed, which he had. "You. The servant. I have two trunks and a crate at the Corvus lodging-house by the Fishmarket Steps. The crate is books. If it gets wet, I'll drown you in it. The small trunk is not to be opened by anyone, for any reason, including fire. Have them on the quay before the tide turns. And find me lamp oil. A great deal of lamp oil."

"Yes, my lady," said Felix faintly.

"Not a lady. A chair." She stepped back over the screen and into the dark of the passage. Her footsteps went away briskly over the flagstones, unhurried and absolutely certain, the tread of someone who had already decided where she was going and did not care who followed.

Marcus stood where she had left him, bleeding into his collar. He did not answer. He did not argue. He only looked at the dark doorway for a long moment, and then down at his own split knuckles, the way a man looks at a bill he knows is his.

Roderic picked up the bent candlestick again, looked at it, and set it on the high table with the others, where it leaned like a drunk.

"I'm fifty-two years old," he said, to the roof. "I have been wounded nine times. I have buried two wives and most of my friends and now a prince. I had hoped to die in my bed, but I'll settle for Cyrene, so long as it's quick. Quick, mind. I'd like to be spared the rest of *this*." He waved a hand at the hall, the broken tables, the prince, the door where the woman had gone. "Whatever this is going to be."

Felix cleared his throat. He was holding the damp cloak very carefully, as though it might be evidence. "If my lord General will permit," he said, "I find I share his hope. Respectfully. In every particular."

They both looked at Marcus.

The prince of Aeridor stood among the rushes with goose grease on his boots and his dead brother's candles cold around him, and said something too low for anyone but Roderic to hear.

"I deserve this."

*Aye, lad,* Roderic thought. *You do. That's the first useful thing you've said all day.*

---

# Chapter Two, continued: The Good Seat

**ALANA**

She held it together as far as the kitchens.

The servants' passage ran behind the Lesser Hall like a gut behind a belly, low and hot and greasy, and it smelled of tallow and scalded milk and the goose that had gone under the high table. Cooks flattened themselves against the wall to let her by. One of them, a boy with flour to the elbows, actually bowed. She nodded to him the way she would have nodded to a student who had asked a sensible question, and walked on, and did not look back, and counted her steps. Thirty-one to the scullery door. Forty to the yard.

At forty her hands began to shake.

*Well. That is interesting.*

She stopped under the eaves where the rain-barrels stood and looked at them, the ink-stained, traitorous things, as if they belonged to someone else and had been lent to her on poor terms. They had not shaken in the hall. They had not shaken when she said *cruel* and *careless* and *I'd like a good seat*, and they had not shaken when he flinched. She had been watching them particularly. They had waited, like good servants, until there was no one left to see.

The yard was full of snow and the snow was full of footprints. Beyond the wall the Old Hill fell away toward the harbor, and the canals steamed in the cold the way they always did in Aeridor, warm water out of the old fires under the island meeting the winter air and losing the argument. Lantern-light floated on the fog. Somewhere below, down by the quays, men were already hammering. The king's tide did not wait on anyone's grief.

Alana put her hands into her sleeves and held them there until they stopped.

Then she started down the hill.

---

It was a long way to the Fishmarket Steps, and she walked it, because there was no one to send for a chair and no money to pay one. The silk of her robe was good silk still, Corvus silk, the last bolt the academy had ordered before the war, and it was not made for snow. The hem drank the slush. By the second bridge her feet had gone past cold into that clean, distant ache that meant they would hurt properly later. She noted it, as she noted everything. *Toes. Possible frostbite, minor. Of no account against the general prognosis, which is death at Cyrene. Still. Buy boots.*

She had not meant to speak.

That was the part she turned over, crossing the Bridge of Lamps with the black water smoking underneath. She had gone to the funeral because the refugees always went to the funerals. The council liked to see them there, behind the pillars, grateful. She had gone to the feast because the feast had bread. She had stood behind the screen with the cooks because it was warm, and because from behind the screen she could watch the hall without the hall watching her, which was the only way she could bear to be in that room at all.

It was the same room. That was the trouble. The same long tables. The same two fires. Eight years and a war and a burned library, and they had not even moved the benches.

She had been fifteen, the summer the delegation came. Old Master Hesper had brought her as a curiosity, the girl who had read the whole of the astronomical canon at twelve and corrected it at thirteen, and she had been so busy being clever that she had not noticed, until it was too late to do anything about it, that she was also being a fool. The second prince had laughed at something she said in the gardens. Only that. He had laughed, and looked at her properly for a moment, the way no one at the academy ever looked at anything that was not written down, and she had gone home and written fourteen lines about it in the old high meter and recopied them three times for the hand.

She could still remember the sound the high table made. Not the laughter. Before the laughter. The little intake of breath, a whole table of lords drawing in air at once, the way a crowd breathes in when a man steps off a roof.

He had done her voice quite well. That was the worst of it, if she was being precise. He had a good ear.

And then Midwinter. The gown. A year of copying fees for other people's dissertations, and a dressmaker in the Silk Row who had looked at her coin and then at her and said, *Violet, I think, for you,* with a kindness Alana had not understood until much later. The length of the Lesser Hall. Forty-one steps; she had counted those too. He had looked at her face, and through it, as though she were a window onto something more interesting, and then he had turned and smiled at the herald's daughter.

Forty-one steps back. She had made herself walk them at an ordinary pace. That had seemed important at the time.

*And tonight I walked them again,* she thought, *the other direction, and he stood still for it.*

It should have been sweet. She had imagined it, once or twice, in the years when she still had leisure to imagine things. She had not imagined the blood on his chin, or how young he had looked with it there, or the way he had said *I haven't earned it* as though he had been holding the words in his mouth for a long time and was glad to be rid of them.

She did not care for that at all. It was not in the notes.

So she made the notes, there on the bridge, with the fog coming up wet around her ankles. It was what she did with any phenomenon she did not understand. She set out the hypotheses.

*One. The subject has joined a suicide expedition because she wishes to kill Tharsians, and the risen things Tharsis makes, and Lucan if the gods are generous. The prince is incidental. He is the man holding the ship.*

That was the one she had said aloud in the hall. It had the merit of being true. It also meant that the only thing left in her that wanted anything at all was the part that wanted to burn the dead, and she did not think much of a woman whose whole heart could be written on the back of a lamp-oil order.

*Two. The subject is twenty-three years old, has lost her home, her chair, her library and every student she ever had, and is still, after eight years and a sonnet read aloud in somebody else's voice, pathetically attracted to Marcus of Aeridor.*

She stood quite still in the middle of the bridge and made herself look at that one. A scholar does not get to skip the ugly line in the proof.

He had come over the table like a storm coming over a seawall. She had watched it through the gap in the screen, the goose flying, the great red-bearded oaf going down under him, and something in her chest had gone tight and hot that was not, if she was being precise, entirely disapproval. And afterwards, the blood on his chin. *I haven't earned it.* She had wanted, for one appalling breath, to wipe it off for him.

*Three. Both.*

That was the worst of the three, and therefore, by every rule of evidence she had ever been taught, the likeliest.

None of them gave her any comfort. She turned them over one after another like stones on a beach, hoping for something living underneath, and under every one there was only wet sand.

When she was fifteen she had built a whole life for him in her head. She was ashamed of how detailed it had been. A house on the cliffs at Corvus with a library on the ground floor and the sea on three sides. Him reading at the window. Her correcting his grammar. Children with ink on their hands. She had furnished the rooms. She had chosen the curtains, gods help her, in that same violet.

The house was ash now. The cliffs were Tharsian. The library was a black hole in the ground with a spire growing out of it.

And she was going with him after all. Not to the house on the cliffs. To Cyrene, on a prison ship, with a cohort of thieves and an old general and a manservant packing for four, to walk into the dark behind the drowned and not come back.

*I used to dream of a life with him,* she thought. *Now I get to share his nightmare instead. The last one. The one he doesn't wake from.*

She laughed, once, out loud, on the empty bridge. It came out of her like something breaking and it frightened a gull off the rail.

Fifteen-year-old Alana would have taken the bargain. That was the truly unforgivable part. Fifteen-year-old Alana would have said, *At least I'll be beside him,* and meant it.

She walked on before she could find out whether twenty-three-year-old Alana would say it too.

---

The Corvus lodging-house leaned against its neighbors at the foot of the Fishmarket Steps like a drunk man against a wall. Four floors, eleven families, one stove that worked. It smelled of fish oil and boiled cabbage and the particular sourness of too many people who had once been someone sleeping in too few rooms. Three of her old students' mothers lived on the second landing. She went up past their door very quietly.

Her room was under the roof. A cot, a basin, a crate of books, two trunks.

She lit the candle-end and knelt by the small trunk and did not open it.

She never did. That was the discipline. She knelt and put her palm flat on the lid, on the cold iron of the bands, and felt for it the way you feel for a hearthstone in the dark to know whether the fire has gone out. Through the wood and the iron and the oilcloth it lay where it always lay, wrapped and corded and sealed with her own wax: one book, from the forbidden wing, from Livia's shelves, taken off its chain on the night the sky over Corvus went the color of a forge.

It was not warm. It was never warm. It only had the quality, under her hand, of something that was paying attention.

*Livia opened hers.*

She had not seen it. She had been in the boat by then, with the fishermen cursing at the oars and the water full of floating pages, and she had looked back and seen the library roof go in with a sound like the whole sea falling down a stair. But she had heard, afterward, in the refugee camps, the way you hear everything in camps. Livia had stayed. Livia had walked into the wing with the fire coming up the stairs behind her and taken down her book and opened it, and when she walked out again the Tharsian soldiers had stepped aside for her.

People said it to Alana sometimes, carefully, as though she might break. *You were her colleague. You must have known her.*

She had known her. That was why she had never opened the trunk.

Her hands had begun again. She took the right one off the lid and looked at it in the candlelight. Ink in the creases of the knuckles. Ink under the nails. A small white burn across the ball of the thumb from the night she had tested, on a drowned thing in a ditch at Hollowmere, whether lamp oil and a particular rune together would do what lamp oil alone would not. (It had. Mostly. She would want a great deal more oil.)

*Afraid,* she wrote in her head, in the neat hand she used for observations. *Subject is afraid. Not of Cyrene. Cyrene is arithmetic. Of the boat, possibly. Of the long nights on the boat, with nothing to do but think, and the trunk under the bench, and the prince three oars away with his split knuckles and his face like a bill come due.*

*Correction.* She frowned at the candle. *Of being on the boat and being useless. Of all of it, everything she has carried out of the fire, coming to nothing on a beach because she was too careful. That is the fear. Note it, and go on.*

She got up. Her knees cracked like an old man's; the cold had got into her on the hill. Somewhere down the Steps a dog was barking at the fog, and beyond the dog the hammering on the quays went on and on, steady as a heart, the sound of the king building her a boat to die in.

She found her good pen, and the last sheet of the good paper, and sat on the cot with the board across her knees. There were things to set down before the tide. A list of the dead of Corvus, as far as she knew them, so that someone would have it. A note to the mothers on the second landing. A word for whoever got the crate if the crate outlived her.

She wrote the first line, and stopped, and looked at it.

It was in the old high meter. Fourteen syllables. She had not written a line in that meter in eight years, and her hand had simply done it, the way a hand finds a stair in the dark.

Alana of Corvus looked at the line for a long time.

Then she scored it through, once, very neatly, and began again in prose.


---

# Chapter Three: The Clemency

**MARCUS**

They brought the cohort down from the harbor prison an hour before the tide, in the blue dark, chained in tens.

Marcus heard them before he saw them. Iron on stone, a long dragging music coming down the Gaol Stair, and under it the sound of men's feet, too many and too slow, the shuffle of people who had not walked farther than a cell wall in a long time and had forgotten how. Then the fog thinned at the bottom of the stair and there they were: a grey column of shaved heads and bare shins and breath smoking in the cold, with turnkeys walking beside them carrying lanterns and cudgels, like drovers bringing cattle to the boats.

He stood on the quay and watched them come, and his ribs ached where Ursus had found them, and his knuckles had stiffened in the night into two fists of split plum. His mouth tasted of old blood. He had not had a drink since the canal.

That was eleven hours. He had counted them. He had lain on his bed in the dark with his boots on and counted them the way a man in a fever counts the beams of the ceiling, and around the eighth hour his hands had started a small, private tremor that he had watched with interest and then put under his armpits so he would not have to. It was there still. He could feel it in the bones of his wrists, a little shiver like a plucked string that will not stop humming.

*Let it hum.*

The quay smelled of tar and fish-guts and the sulphur rising off the warm harbor water, which steamed in the cold so that the whole of the Lower Harbor seemed to be quietly on fire. Gulls stood on the bollards with their feathers fluffed and their eyes shut. Out past the moles, where the warm water ended, the Grey stood up off the sea like a wall of wet wool, and somewhere inside it the morning was happening to somebody else.

"Two hundred and twelve," said the warden.

He was a small, neat man with a fur collar and a wax tablet and the bright, attentive face of a grocer. He had been waiting on the quay since before Marcus arrived, and he held out the tablet now and a stylus with it, and smiled.

"Two hundred and twelve men of the penal cohort, Highness, released into your charge by order of the king. If you'd make your mark. Here. And here, for the irons. The irons are to come back."

Marcus looked at the tablet. *Received of the Harbor Prison,* it said, in a clerk's tidy hand, *two hundred and twelve (212) men, condition fair.* Underneath was a column of figures for the irons, by the weight.

"Fair," Marcus said.

"Fair, Highness. We gave them bread last night. Not all of them ate it."

There was an oilskin packet inside his tunic, flat against his breastbone. He had carried it so long that he only noticed it when he was cold, and he was cold now, and he noticed it. Ninety-one names in his own hand. Copied by candlelight in a cabin that rolled, the first winter, with the ink freezing in the pot so that he had to hold it in his armpit between lines. No new names. He had promised that much to a sheet of paper, which was the only thing left to promise it to.

And here was a grocer offering him two hundred and twelve more, by the weight.

He took the stylus. His hand shook. He made his mark anyway, a hard bad *M* that tore the wax, and gave it back.

"The irons are to come back," the warden reminded him, kindly.

"Then come and fetch them," said Marcus. "Cyrene. Ask for me at the gallows."

---

Roderic came along the quay with a sea-chest on his shoulder that two dockhands would have carried between them, and set it down by the gangplank, and looked at the cohort, and spat into the harbor.

"Well," he said. "I've seen worse."

"Where?"

The General thought about it honestly, which was one of the things Marcus had always hated about him. "The Long Field," he said at last. "The morning after. We were the ones who won."

They stood side by side and looked at what they had been given.

It was not a cohort. It was what a city sweeps out of its corners. Marcus walked the column with Roderic a pace behind him, the way he had once walked inspection lines with a vine-stick under his arm and Tiberius a pace behind *him*, and he made himself see it. Old men. Boys. A one-eyed giant with the soft belly and the flour-white forearms of a baker. A thin fellow with clever hands who was already, Marcus saw, working at the pin of the man's irons in front of him, quite gently, as if out of habit. Men with brands on their cheeks. Men with nothing on their cheeks at all but cold. Some of them looked at him. Most did not. The ones who did had heard about the funeral; he could see it in their faces. Word went down the Gaol Stair faster than water.

*The drunk prince. Come to lead us to our graves. Gods, we must have been very wicked.*

Halfway along the third ten there was a man who stood differently.

It was a small thing. Nobody else would have marked it. The rest of the ten slumped in their chains the way chained men slump, every one his own shape, but this one had his feet set a shoulder apart and his weight a little forward on them, and his left arm held slightly crooked at the elbow as though it still remembered the weight of a shield-boss. He was perhaps thirty. Grey come early into a short black beard. A soldier's tan, faded to a prisoner's yellow.

Marcus stopped in front of him.

"Legion," he said.

The man looked at the stones of the quay. "Fourteenth, Highness. Was."

The Fourteenth. The one they had given Titus instead of the Ninth.

"Rank."

"Optio. Third century, second cohort." A pause. "Was."

"Your name."

"Decimus Rufo."

"And what is an optio of the Fourteenth doing in the harbor prison, Decimus Rufo?"

The man lifted his head then, and Marcus saw his eyes, and knew the answer before it came. He had seen eyes like that in a shaving-mirror.

"I ran, Highness," said Decimus Rufo. "At Hollowmere."

Somewhere down the quay a gull woke up and screamed. Nobody else made a sound. The whole ten had gone still on either side of him, the way a field goes still when a hawk's shadow crosses it, and behind him Marcus heard Roderic stop breathing.

"Tell me," said Marcus.

"There's not much to it." Rufo's voice was flat, a man reading a list he had read too many times. "We held the ford from first light. Your brother was on the bank behind us, on the grey, where we could see him. Second hour, the water started moving wrong. Third hour, they came up out of it. Our lads. The ones we'd lost on the bridge the week before. We'd buried them ourselves, Highness, up on the hill, with the proper words. And there they come, walking up out of the river with weed in their hair, in the front rank of the Tharsians, so we'd have to cut through our own to get at the enemy." He swallowed. "My sister's husband was in the second rank. Gallus. He had a squint. He still had it."

"And you ran."

"I put down my shield and I ran." Rufo did not look away. He would not, Marcus saw, give himself even that. "I don't know what I thought. That I'd get to the hill and dig him up and he'd be there. That it'd be a lie. I got as far as the wagons before the provosts had me. By then it was over. By then your brother—" He stopped. "I'm sorry, Highness. I am sorry for it. I've been sorry every day. It doesn't do anything, but I am."

The tremor had come back into Marcus's wrists. He let it. He stood there on the quay in the steam and the stink of tar and looked at a man who had left Titus on the bank, and he waited to see what would come up out of the cellar of him.

He had expected the red thing. The hot thing, the thing that had put him over the table at Ursus. It was not there. What was there was colder and much worse, and it took him a long moment to know it, because he had spent three years with his face turned away from it.

*You ran. You put down your shield and you ran. And they caught you, and chained you, and threw you in the dark, and nobody ever told you that you were saving anyone.*

*At least you chose it.*

"Can you still hold a shield?" Marcus said.

Rufo blinked. "Highness?"

"A shield. A big curved board with a boss in the middle. Can you hold one. In a line. With men on either side of you who'll die if you put it down."

The optio's mouth worked. "I— yes. Yes, Highness."

"Then you're the first man in the first line of the first century, and when the dead come walking out of the water at Cyrene, as they will, you'll be standing where I can see you." He turned to the turnkey with the keys. "Strike his irons."

"Highness, the warden said the irons stay on until the ship—"

"*Strike his irons.*"

The turnkey struck them. They fell on the quay with a sound like a dropped bell.

Rufo stood rubbing his wrists, staring at Marcus as if he had grown a second head. Marcus did not wait to be thanked. He walked on down the line, and his ribs hurt, and his hands shook, and Roderic fell in a pace behind him, and after a while said in a low voice, not looking at him:

"That was either the best thing you've done since you were fifteen, or the worst."

"Which?"

"Ask me at Cyrene."

---

The ship was called the *Clemency*.

Someone in the harbor office had a sense of humor, or none at all. She was a prison hulk, an old grain transport of the deep-bellied Vessan kind that had been stripped of her masts a decade ago and moored in the Lower Harbor to hold the overflow when the Gaol Stair was full. They had given her back one mast in the night, which was why the quays had rung with hammering till dawn. It stood up out of her like a new tooth in an old jaw. Her timbers were black with weed below the waterline and grey with gull-lime above it, and when the tide moved under her she groaned all along her length, the deep, complaining groan of an old woman getting out of a chair.

She had thirty oars a side and men for perhaps twenty. She smelled, from forty paces off, of bilge and despair.

Felix stood at the foot of her gangplank with Alana's crate of books in his arms, looking up at her with the expression of a man reading his own epitaph and finding the spelling poor.

"The harbormaster," Felix said, "assures me she is sound, Highness."

"Is she?"

"He assured me very quickly. And then he went away."

The trunks were already aboard. So, Marcus saw, was Alana. She stood at the rail of the afterdeck in a borrowed sea-cloak much too large for her, with the violet showing at the hem, and she was not looking at him. She was looking at the casks being swung up out of a lighter in a cargo net, and counting them, and her lips were moving. Lamp oil. A great deal of lamp oil. He wondered where Felix had found the money and decided he did not want to know.

She looked up once, as he came along the quay. Her eyes went over him from his split lip to his split knuckles in a single, clerkly pass, the way a moneylender's eyes go over a pledge, and then she went back to counting casks.

*A good seat,* he thought, and his face burned in the cold.

The last ten came down the Gaol Stair alone.

It was not a ten. It was one man, between two turnkeys, in heavier irons than the rest, wrist and ankle both, with a length of chain between that made him walk short. He was a long, spare, slow-moving man of perhaps fifty, with a sailor's rolling gait that the chains had not been able to take from him and a sailor's face, all creases, burned dark by years of wind and then left in the dark long enough to fade to the color of old sailcloth. His beard had grown down to his breastbone and gone white at the chin. He did not look at the cohort. He did not look at the ship. He looked, as they brought him along the quay, at the Grey, standing up out of the sea past the moles, the way another man might look at the face of a woman he had once known.

"The master for the *Clemency*," said the warden, consulting his tablet. "There's only the one in the prison, Highness. We looked."

Marcus did not hear him.

He had heard, instead, a voice from a long way down, from under three years and a great deal of wine. *Drink this, Centurion. It's cold out on the water.* A cup in a seaman's hand, held out steady across the pitching deck of a ship running for the harbor mouth, with the stair burning behind it and the sound of the Old Hundred still coming across the water, the shields, the roar, Tiberius's voice in the middle of it like a bronze bell. *Drink it. The flank's a long pull.*

And he had drunk it. Gods help him, he had been thirsty. He had drunk it down in one, and handed back the cup, and turned to look at the stair, and the stair had gone very slowly sideways and then away.

He had woken two days out, in a hammock, with the taste of poppy in his mouth like a rotten tooth, and this face bending over him.

*It's done, Highness. I'm sorry. Those were my orders.*

The quay had gone quiet again. Roderic had seen his face. Felix had seen it. Somewhere above, at the rail, Alana had stopped counting.

Marcus walked to the man in the heavy irons and stood in front of him.

The prisoner turned his eyes from the Grey and looked at him. They were pale eyes, sea-colored, washed out by weather. There was no surprise in them. There was nothing in them, really, but a kind of tired attention, the look of a man watching a squall come up across the water who has already reefed his sails and can do nothing more about it but wait.

"Captain Nerva," said Marcus.

"Highness."

"Aulus Nerva. Master of the *Swift Heron*. Out of the harbor of—" The name stuck. He had not said it aloud in three years. He did not say it now. "Out of the harbor."

"I had that ship. They broke her up for her bronze." Nerva's voice was low and hoarse, the voice of a man who had not used it for much, lately. "They told me at the trial. I think they meant it to hurt."

"Did it?"

"Some."

Marcus's hands had become fists. He had not felt them do it this time either. He looked down at them, split and swollen, shaking very slightly at the wrists, and he thought how easy it would be. One blow. The man was in irons. He could not lift his hands above his chest. Marcus had broken Ursus's nose last night for less than this, for a word, and the hall had cheered, half of it.

*You drugged me.*

"You drugged me," he said.

"I did."

"In the wine."

"Poppy, and something the surgeon had in a blue bottle. He said it was for amputations." Nerva paused. "You slept two days. I didn't know if you'd wake. I sat with you, the second night."

"Ninety-one."

The captain's face moved for the first time. It was very small. A tightening at the corners of the eyes, as of a man looking into a wind.

"I know the number, Highness," he said. "I've had a long time to learn it."

"You should have let me go back."

"Yes. Maybe." Nerva looked at him steadily. "And then it'd be ninety-two. Or worse than ninety-two. They raised *him* inside the week, you'll have heard, and he was only a centurion. What do you suppose they'd have given for the king's son?" He did not say *Tiberius.* He did not need to. "He knew that. He knew it when he sent you down to my boat. He looked at me the way a man looks at a dog he's going to ask a hard thing of, and he said, *Get the boy off this rock, Aulus. However you have to.* And I did. And I've sat in the dark three years with it, Highness, and I'll tell you true: I don't know if it was right. I don't ask you to say it was. I know I'd do it again."

Marcus stood there.

All along the quay the cohort was watching. Two hundred and twelve men in chains, and the turnkeys, and the warden with his wax tablet, and the gulls. Rufo was watching, with his wrists bare. Roderic was watching, with his arms folded and his face like a shut door. Up at the rail, Alana was watching, and he did not have to look up to feel her eyes on the back of his neck like a cold coin.

*Hit him. You want to. You've wanted to for three years.*

*And then who sails the boat?*

It was such a small, mean, practical thought that he almost laughed. It came up through the red thing like a rock coming up through a falling tide, flat and grey and covered in barnacles, and it did not care at all how he felt. It was the kind of thought Titus would have had. It was the kind of thought Tiberius would have had, standing on the stair working out which boy to save and how. It was a ledger thought, and he hated it, and it was right.

He opened his hands. They hurt, doing it.

"Can you sail her?" he said, and jerked his head at the *Clemency*.

Nerva looked past him at the ship for a long moment. His eyes went up the new mast and down along the weeded strakes and the oar-ports, half of them still boarded up, and back to the stern, where the steering oar hung in its yoke at a sullen angle like a broken arm.

"I can sail anything that floats," the captain said at last. "Whether she floats is another question. I'll want caulkers. I'll want pitch. I'll want the starboard steering oar off her and looked at by a man who knows what he's looking at, because I don't like the angle of her." He paused. "And I'll want stones, Highness. You don't take a ship into the Grey on dead reckoning. Not unless you mean to stay there."

"You'll have what the king gives us."

"What has the king given us?"

Marcus turned to the warden.

The warden smiled his bright grocer's smile, and reached into his fur collar, and brought out a small doeskin bag on a cord, and held it out on his palm.

Marcus took it. It was very light. He tipped it into his hand, and out came a single sliver of stone, no longer than his little finger, the color of honey held up to a candle. It lay across his palm and turned, all by itself, a little, the way a sleeping dog turns in its basket, until its blunt end pointed back over his shoulder. Up the hill. Toward the Hall of Kings, and the steaming springs under it, and the Heart-Stone in the rock.

Home.

One stone. A stone of the capital, that would show them the way back, and nothing else at all.

He heard Roderic make a sound behind him, a short ugly breath out through the nose that was almost a laugh.

"Well," said the General. "That's your father."

Nerva looked down at the shard on the prince's palm. Something moved behind his washed-out eyes, and was gone.

"I've been to Cyrene," he said. "Twelve times. I know the way in by the smell of the shipyards and the way the swell sets off the headland. I can get you there without a stone, Highness, if the gods aren't watching too close." He nodded at the honey-colored sliver. "That's for coming back. Somebody thinks you won't need it."

"Somebody," said Marcus, "is going to be disappointed."

He closed his hand on the stone and turned to the turnkeys.

"Strike his irons. And then strike the rest of them. All of them. I won't take men to sea in chains." He raised his voice so the whole quay would hear it. "If any man here can swim back to the Gaol Stair from the harbor mouth, he's welcome to try. The water's warm as far as the moles. After that it isn't."

Nobody laughed. But a few of them looked at him, then, who had not looked before.

---

They cast off on the turn of the tide, with the bells beginning on the Old Hill.

He had forgotten. The second morning of mourning: the bells of the Hall of Kings rang the hours for a dead prince from dawn to dusk, and the other bells of the city answered them one by one, the Fishmarket bell and the harbor bell and the cracked old bell of the Gaol Stair, until the whole hill was ringing like a struck shield. They rang out over the Lower Harbor and the steaming water and the black hulk of the *Clemency* creeping out between the moles on her twenty pairs of untried oars, and the cohort pulled to them, badly, catching crabs and cursing, while the drum-boy beat a stroke that nobody kept.

Marcus stood at the stern beside the steering oar and looked back.

The capital stood up out of its own steam behind him, terraced and lamplit and white with snow, the Old Hill crowned with the dome of the Hall of Kings, and somewhere under the dome his brother lay on a bier with his hair combed the way he hated it. There was no one on the moles to see them go. No herald. No banner. No king. He had not expected a king. He found, all the same, that he had looked.

*Let them ring.*

Nerva had the steering oar. The captain stood with it tucked under his arm and his bare, chafed wrists resting on the loom, feeling the ship through it the way a horseman feels a strange horse through the reins, and he did not look back at the city once. He was looking ahead, at the Grey.

It was close now. The warm water was thinning under them. Marcus could feel the cold coming up off the sea through the soles of his boots, and the steam of the harbor was falling away astern in rags, and ahead of them the fog-wall stood up as high as the hill behind, soft and white and absolutely without end. Things moved in it. Not things. Only fog, moving on fog. But it was the kind of moving that the eye kept snagging on, and the oars faltered as the men looked up and saw it, and the drum-boy missed a stroke, and somebody in the waist began, quietly, to pray.

"Steady," said Nerva, not loudly. And then, louder, in a voice that was suddenly not hoarse at all but rang down the length of the ship like a hammer on an anvil: "*Steady on the oars there! Pull! It's only weather! I've sailed through worse in my bath!*"

The oars caught. The stroke came back.

The captain reached down without looking, with his free hand, and brought up a leather bottle from a cleat beside the oar, and pulled the stopper with his teeth, and drank. Then he held it out to Marcus.

Marcus looked at it.

He knew what was in it before he smelled it. Thin red sailor's wine, sour as a bad debt, the kind the harbor taverns sold by the skin. He could smell it from where he stood, over the tar and the bilge and the cold. His mouth flooded. The tremor came up out of his wrists into his hands, into the whole of his arms, and for one long moment, with the bells ringing behind him and the Grey rising ahead, he wanted it more than he had ever wanted anything, more than he had wanted to hit this man on the quay, more than he had wanted Titus to be alive.

Nerva watched him. The captain's face did not change. He had drunk first, Marcus understood. So the prince would see it was clean.

Marcus took the bottle from him.

He held it a moment. Then he turned and upended it over the stern rail, and watched the thin red stream go down into the white water of their wake and vanish, the way blood vanishes in the sea, and when it was empty he threw the bottle after it.

"Not from you," he said.

Nerva looked at the place where the bottle had gone. Then he turned back to the Grey and set the steering oar a hand's breadth to starboard.

"No, Highness," he said. "I'd not think so."

Behind them the bells of the Old Hill rang on for the dead prince, fainter now, and fainter, until the fog came down over the stern of the *Clemency* like a hand over a mouth, and the capital and its bells were gone.

---

By the afternoon of the first day the fog had turned to sleet, and the sleet had opinions.

It came in sideways off the dark water out of the north, small and hard as thrown salt, and it found every gap in a man's clothing as if it had been told where to look. The swell had got up under the *Clemency* and she wallowed in it, lifting her blunt old bows and setting them down again with a weary boom that went through the whole ship and came up through the soles of the feet. Below, in the waist, two hundred men who had never been to sea were discovering it. The sound of it came up through the hatch-gratings, and the smell came after.

They stood on the afterdeck, the five of them, because there was nowhere else to stand.

Nobody had arranged it. Marcus had come up for air. Roderic had come up because the General could not abide being below decks with anything that groaned, whether it was a ship or a man. Felix had come up because Marcus had. Alana had come up, as far as Marcus could tell, to look at the weather in the manner of someone checking a sum. And Nerva was at the steering oar, where he had been since the moles, as if he had grown there.

So they stood in a rough half-ring by the stern rail, near enough to touch, and nobody touched, and nobody spoke.

The sleet rattled on the deck between them like dice.

---

**RODERIC**

His back had found the weather before he had.

It was the old place, low on the left, where the horse had come down on him at the Long Field thirty years ago. It had begun at noon as a dull complaint and was working itself up now into a proper grievance, a hot iron nail driven in a little further with every wallow of the ship. He stood with his feet wide and his hands behind him and gave it nothing. Fifty-two years old. He had stood a whole night in a frozen ditch once with an arrow through his calf, and laughed about it after.

*You were thirty then. You laughed because you were thirty.*

He watched the boy without seeming to. That was the trick of it, with boys and horses both. You looked past them at the horizon and saw everything they did out of the corner of your eye.

The boy was holding the stone. Had been holding it all day, in his left fist, inside his cloak, as though somebody might take it from him. Every so often the fist moved a little, when the stone turned. Every time it moved, the boy's jaw moved with it.

*Sober.* Roderic had not seen him sober past noon in two years. He looked worse for it. Grey around the mouth. The hands going. He had poured the captain's wine over the side like a priest pouring a libation, and Roderic, watching, had felt something in his chest that he did not care to put a name to, and had turned away to cough.

*He'll take a drink by Cyrene. Three days. Four. The first time the dead come up out of the water at him, he'll want it, and there'll be a jar somewhere, there always is.*

*Or he won't.*

He did not know which he was afraid of more. A drunk he could manage. He had managed this one for years. A sober man would remember everything, and a sober man would ask him, sooner or later, the question that the drunk had never once thought to ask.

*Where were you, General? When the stair fell. When the Old Hundred died. Where was the king's best horse-soldier that day?*

He shifted his weight. The nail in his back went in another finger's breadth.

The ship boomed down into a trough. Spray came over the quarter and struck him across the face, cold enough to stop the heart, and he wiped it off with the flat of his hand and said nothing, and nobody else said anything either.

After a while he cleared his throat.

"I'll see to the men," he said, to no one.

Nobody stopped him. He went forward along the rail, walking very straight, and the sleet closed behind him.

---

**FELIX**

Felix had been sick twice, discreetly, over the lee side, and was fairly sure he had a third in him.

It was not the sea. He wished to be clear on that point, if only to himself. He had grown up on the Fishmarket Steps and been in boats since before he could walk. It was the *smell* of the ship, the old grain-sour, prison-sour, bilge-sour breath of her coming up through the gratings, and the noise from the waist, and the fact that he had not eaten since the feast, and had not eaten much at the feast because he had been occupied in standing behind a screen watching his master throw a knight down a table.

He stood half a pace behind Marcus and a little to the left, which was where he always stood. Close enough to catch a falling prince. Far enough not to be noticed doing it.

The prince was not falling. That was the trouble. Felix did not know what to do with his hands when the prince was not falling. He had folded them. He had unfolded them. He had put them in his sleeves. He had taken them out again and found a loose thread at his cuff, which was now very much looser.

*He might have left me behind.* The thought had been sitting in him all day like a stone in a shoe. *He didn't say "come." He didn't say anything. I packed, and I came, and he let me, and he hasn't looked at me once.*

He did not know what he had expected. Thanks were not a thing that came from princes, and he would not have known where to put them. But there had been a moment on the quay, when Marcus had stood in front of that captain with his fists made, and Felix had thought, *Now. Now he'll do it,* and had already been reaching, in his mind, for the cold water and the cloth and the apology to the turnkeys. And Marcus had opened his hands instead.

Felix had served him six years. He had never once seen him open his hands.

It frightened him more than the sea did.

There was also, he could not help noticing, the matter of the lamp oil. Thirty-one casks. He had told the chandler they were for the king's own lamps, and that the king's steward would settle the account at the quarter. Neither of these things had been entirely untrue, if one counted the prince as the king's, and Cyrene as a sort of quarter. But the chandler had a brother in the harbor watch, and a long memory, and Felix had a feeling, somewhere low in his belly next to the third sickness, that if by some miracle any of them ever came home, the first thing waiting on the quay would not be a herald.

*Technically,* he told himself, *we are not expected to come home.*

It was not as comforting as it should have been.

His stomach turned over. He swallowed hard. He looked at the prince's back, and at the lady's, and at the captain's, and at the place where the General had been.

"I'll just—" Felix said, and gestured vaguely at the hatch. Nobody looked round. "The trunks. They'll want lashing."

He went below, quickly, with his hand over his mouth.

---

**ALANA**

The prince had a scar behind his left ear that she did not remember.

She had not meant to notice it. She had been looking at the weather. The weather was a perfectly good thing to look at: the cloud-base coming down by perhaps forty feet an hour, the wind backing into the north, the sleet thickening by degrees into something whiter and slower that would be snow by dark. She had a mind to write it down. Sea-weather was not a field she knew, and a scholar starting a new field should begin with observation.

But he had turned his head to watch the General go, and there it was. Short and white and puckered, from just under the ear to the hinge of the jaw. An old cut, badly stitched. Someone's knife, or someone's sword-edge, from some place she had not been, in the years when she had not been thinking of him in any useful sense.

*It is not in the notes,* she thought. *Because I stopped taking notes. That was the point.*

She took her eyes off it. She put them on the sea, firmly, as she would have put a book back on a shelf. Her hands were cold in her sleeves, and the borrowed cloak smelled of somebody else's pipe, and the violet hem of her robe had gone black with wet and weighed on her ankles like a pair of fetters.

The trunk was below. In the stern cabin, under the bench, lashed down by the servant with knots she had checked and then retied. She could feel it there, under her feet, through the planking. She knew she could not. It was half a ship away and wrapped in oilcloth and iron. She could feel it all the same, the way one feels a person standing behind one in an empty room.

*Paying attention.*

The ship heeled. Her shoulder touched his.

It was nothing. Wool on wool, through two cloaks. She moved away from it the way one moves a hand off a hot pan, before thinking, and then was furious with herself for moving so fast, because he would have noticed, and he would think it meant something, and it did not.

He had not looked round. His jaw was set. He was holding that wretched stone.

*He did not hit the captain,* she thought. *Make a note of that. Then put it with the scar and the hands and everything else that does not fit the hypothesis, and leave it in the drawer, and shut the drawer.*

"It will be snowing within the hour," she said. Her voice came out drier than she meant. "I am going to write down the weather."

She went down the ladder without waiting for an answer, and if anyone had been watching, which no one was, they would have seen that she did not hold the rail.

---

**NERVA**

When the woman had gone, the captain called a man from the waist to the steering oar.

He was a thief, by his hands, and he had told Nerva at noon in a whisper that he had once rowed for the fishing fleets at Vessa before he took to other men's purses. Nerva had watched him row since and believed about half of it. Half was enough. He put the loom in the man's hands and set them where he wanted them and held them there a moment with his own, the way you'd show a child.

"North by the swell," he said. "Keep it on the left cheek. When it moves, you call me."

"Where'll you be, Captain?"

"Close."

He did not look at the prince.

He had felt the prince's eyes on him all afternoon, on the back of his neck, the way you feel a lee shore before you see it. He did not mind it. A man had a right to look. He had looked, himself, at the faces of the turnkeys for three years, every morning when the bread came, and wondered which of them had a brother on the stair.

There was a flask in his coat. Not wine. Something harder, that the thief had found for him somewhere in the waist and pressed on him with great ceremony, and that he had not opened. He put his hand on it now through the wet wool and left it there.

*Not from you.*

Fair. That was fair. He had drunk his own wine first, on the stern, so the boy would know it was clean, and then stood there and watched the boy understand why he had done it, and pour it into the sea. Three years in the dark he had thought of what he might say if he ever saw that face again, and when it came to it he had said what he had said, and none of it was the thing he meant. He did not know the thing he meant. Perhaps there wasn't one.

*I'd do it again.* He had said that, too. He had not known he was going to say it until it was out. It was true. He wished it were not.

He went down the ladder to the main deck, slowly, with his chafed wrists stiff on the rails. He did not go below. He went forward into the sleet and stood by the mast, alone, with his face to the north and his back to the stern, where the prince was.

It seemed the decent thing. To give a man the whole of the stern.

---

**MARCUS**

He did not notice them go. Not properly. He noticed it the way a man notices that a fire has gone out: all at once, by the cold.

He looked round, and there was only the thief at the steering oar, hunched and muttering to the swell, and the empty deck, and the sleet.

It was snow now. The woman had been right. It came down out of the low dark sky in a thick soft slant and settled on the rail and the coils of rope and on the shoulders of his cloak, and did not melt, because nothing here was warm enough to melt it. The warm water was a day behind them. This was the open sea, the real sea, the one the springs did not reach, and it was the color of a knife.

He opened his left hand.

The stone lay in his palm, honey-gold in all that grey, the only warm color for a mile in any direction. It turned. It pointed back over his shoulder, steady as a hound, at a city he could not see any more.

*Home.*

He thought of Titus on his bier with his hair combed. He thought of Roderic's back, held so straight it must have been hurting him. Of Felix's hand over his mouth. Of the woman's shoulder, and how fast she had taken it away. Of a captain standing alone by the mast with his face to the north, giving him the whole of the stern like a gift he had not asked for and could not give back. Of Decimus Rufo somewhere below in the stink of the waist, with his wrists bare, being sick into a bucket with the rest.

Two hundred and twelve. Ninety-one. One.

The ship boomed down into a trough, and came up, and boomed down again, and the snow came over the rail in a long white breath, and the light was going. Somewhere ahead in the dark, two days or three, Cyrene was burning, or had finished burning, and the dead were waiting for him in the water off its beaches with weed in their hair.

His hands were shaking. He let them.

He closed his fist on the stone, and turned his back on it, and stood at the rail facing the way they were going, alone, while the snow came down on the *Clemency* and covered the deck and his boots and his shoulders, and the night came down behind it.


---

# Chapter Four: The Grey

**ALANA**

On the second morning there was ice in the sea, and nobody had told her it would be beautiful.

She came up the ladder at first light with her weather-book under her arm and stopped on the top rung, the way one stops in the door of a lecture hall that has filled while one was not looking. The snow had passed in the night. The sky had lifted to a hard, low lid of pewter, and the sea under it had gone the color of slate, and on the slate, everywhere, as far as the eye could carry, the ice rode the swell. Not a field. Not a road. Pieces. Plates of it no bigger than a tabletop, and slabs as long as a cart, and here and there a great tilted thing the size of a chapel, green-white and grooved like an old tooth, that came down the swell at them with a slowness that was worse than speed.

The *Clemency* went through it with men at the bows.

There were six of them, with long poles and a boathook, leaning out over the stem and fending the floes off as they came, and the floes rang when the poles struck them, a dull wooden note like a mallet on a cask. Nerva stood among them in his shirtsleeves with his white-tipped beard frozen into a single stiff braid on his chest, calling the ice the way a huntsman calls hounds. *Left. Left. Let that one by. Now. Shove.* His voice was not loud. It did not need to be. On the whole ship there was no other sound but the oars, and the ice, and the long hiss of the hull through water that was very nearly not water at all.

*Note,* she thought. *Sea-ice. Not solid. Moves with the swell, not the wind. Rings when struck. Captain treats it as a living hazard, like rocks that walk.*

She did not write it. Her fingers would not have held the pen. She put the book inside her cloak, against her ribs, and went down the deck to the rail to look, because a scholar starting a new field must begin with observation, and because she found she wanted to.

The waist stank. Two days of two hundred men who had never been to sea had made of it something she would not have kept pigs in, and the night's snow, trodden into the planking, had become a grey slurry that sucked at the soles of her boots. Men lay along the benches in their cloaks, or sat with their heads between their knees, or pulled at the oars with the blank, mechanical faces of the sick. A boy was beating the stroke on a drum by the mast. He was perhaps fourteen. He had the drumsticks wrapped in rag against the cold, and he beat with his eyes shut, and his lips moved with each stroke as if he were counting coins.

At the rail she found she was beside the giant.

She had seen him on the quay. One eye, a puckered seam where the other had been, a belly like a sack of meal and forearms white to the elbow with something that was not snow. He was hanging over the side being quietly, thoroughly sick, and when he had finished he wiped his mouth with the back of one enormous hand and saw her and looked mortified.

"Beg pardon, lady."

"Not a lady," Alana said. "That is flour, on your arms."

He looked down at them as if they belonged to someone else. "It doesn't come off. Twenty years in the ovens. It's in the skin now." He scrubbed at one forearm with a thumb the size of a door-bolt. "Oppius, lady. Baker. Was. Bread Street, by the Fishmarket."

"What did you do?"

"Sold short." He said it without shame and without pride, as a man gives the price of a loaf. "Ten years I put a little chalk in the flour, when the grain was dear. Everyone does. The guild-master caught me in a bad year." His one eye went past her, out over the ice. "Fed a lot of people with that chalk, lady. They'd not have eaten, else."

*Note,* she thought, and then did not finish it, because there was nothing to note. It was only true.

---

The stern cabin was a box of black timber with a bench along each wall and a lamp that swung, and it smelled of tar and her own wet wool and, faintly, of the oil-casks stowed against the bulkhead. She had slept there two nights, if it was sleep. She had lain on the bench with the small trunk under her, and felt the ship go up and come down and go up, and every time it came down the trunk shifted a finger's breadth against its lashings and settled again, with a small creak, like something turning over.

She did not open it. She put her palm on the lid each night before she lay down, and each morning when she rose, the way her mother had touched the doorpost of the house at Corvus. That was all.

*Paying attention.*

It was paying attention to the ice, she thought. Or to the north. Or to nothing at all, and she was a frightened woman in a cold box, assigning intentions to oilcloth.

She had filled nine pages with weather. The pages were very good. She had marked the wind at each turn of the glass, and the set of the swell, and the color of the sky in the absence of any sun to lend it one, and the precise hour, on the first night, at which the sleet had become snow (the third bell of the dog-watch; she had been right, within a quarter-hour, and had told no one). She had drawn the floes. She had drawn the captain's way of laying a hand flat on the rail with the fingers spread, which he did every hour or so, and which she had at first taken for a sailor's superstition before she understood that he was feeling the ship's pulse through the wood.

There was nothing in the nine pages about the prince.

That had taken discipline. She was proud of it, in a small, bleak way. She had come up on deck on the evening of the first day and found him alone at the stern with the snow on his shoulders and his fist closed on that stone, and she had stood at the top of the ladder a full minute without his knowing, and then gone below again and written *wind backing NNW, moderate; snow heavy, settling* and nothing else whatsoever.

---

**MARCUS**

"You're giving iron to thieves," said Roderic.

There was a chest in the waist by the mainmast, an iron-strapped thing with the king's seal on the hasp, and Marcus had broken the seal with the heel of a boathook at first light and was kneeling in the slush beside it now with his sleeves pushed up. The General stood over him with his arms folded and a face like a winter field.

"Forty-two gladii," Roderic went on, low. "And I'll wager there's not one in that chest without rust in the fuller. Sixty spears. Eighty shields, condemned. Some clerk in the arsenal cleared his books with this, Highness, and he smiled to do it."

"Then we'll be the best-armed condemned men on the sea."
