She had looked at the eleven men against the tower wall exactly once, when they came down: a long, level, unhurried look, the look a physician gives a wound, that had made two of them drop their eyes. Then she had turned her back on them and not looked again. Only her folded hands, white at the knuckles, said what it had cost her. "For a month. Perhaps a season."

"And then?"

"And then, if you are very lucky, and you keep doing it, they'll call it wisdom. For a year." She turned her calm, bruised face to him. "After that they'll forget it was ever a choice, and think it was simply how things are done here. That is how all the good things happen, I think. Somebody chooses them at the wrong time, and is hated for it, and then everyone forgets."

"That's a grim sort of comfort."

"It isn't comfort," said Liora. "It's the work." She looked down at the sword-belt still hanging from his hand. "You have blood on your face, Highness. Some of it is yours. Go and let your barber look at it, and eat something. You have orders to sign tomorrow. The grief can have the hours after."

She went away toward the steam-box house before he could answer.

He stood in the courtyard with Merula's sword in his hand, and saw Iason.

The young man of Cyrene was standing by the well with his unwiped sword, quite still, looking at the eleven against the wall. He had heard all of it. He did not say anything. After a moment he turned his back on Marcus, deliberately, the way a man turns his back on a fire he does not trust himself near, and walked out through the gate after the town.

Marcus let him go. There was nothing in him to say that Iason would have wanted to hear.

---

**RODERIC**

He found the boy on the wall at dusk, on the seaward side, where the fort looked out over the bay and the Grey beyond it, standing yellow and endless on the edge of the world.

He had not meant to find him. He had come up because the steps of the wall were the only steps in the fort that a man with a back like his could climb by himself without being seen to need help, and because he wanted to look at the sea. Or he told himself that. He was fifty-two. He had stopped believing most of what he told himself a long while ago.

Marcus had a cup in his hand.

Roderic saw it from the head of the stair and something went cold in him. Then he came closer and saw what was in the cup, and smelled it, and it was water. The spring's water, from the well in the courtyard. It smelled of rotten eggs and iron, faintly, even cold.

"It tastes like a forge," the boy said, without turning round. "I don't know how they drink it."

"They grow up on it. Like the Grey." Roderic came to the parapet beside him and leaned on it, both forearms on the old stone, to take the weight off his back. "Give it a year. You'll be ruined for any other water. That's what the shipwrights say."

"A year." Marcus turned the cup in his hand. "Do you think we'll be here in a year, General?"

"I think we'll be somewhere. Here's as good as anywhere." Roderic looked down into the bay. The tide was coming in over the weed-rocks under the eastern port. There were lamps lit in the town, here and there, along the waterfront. A few. More than last night. "It's a good harbor. Better than I thought. That engineer's down in the yards already with a lantern and his little tablet, measuring things. He tried to measure the *Clemency*. Nerva told him he'd put him over the side."

The boy smiled a little. Then the smile went.

"I killed a man on the stair this morning," he said. "In the eastern tower. He was carrying a bowl of gruel."

Roderic waited.

"My hands were steady. They were steady on the ship, in the night, with the dead coming in through the ports. And they were steady this morning on the stair, and under the wall, and in the courtyard." He held his free hand out over the parapet, palm down, in the last of the light. It shook. A small fine tremor, a plucked string. "And now look. I'm sober for the first time in three years, General, and the only time my hands don't shake is when I'm killing someone." He put the hand down on the stone. "What does that make me?"

Roderic did not answer at once. He had heard that question before, in other words, from other young men, at the edge of other fires. He had asked it himself, once, of his grandfather, after his first field. He remembered the old man's answer, which had been a joke, and how much he had hated it.

"It makes you a soldier who's stopped drinking," he said at last. "That's all. It'll pass. The shaking, I mean. The rest of it doesn't pass, but you stop noticing it, mostly." He was quiet a moment. "My grandfather used to say a thing. *They cheer the charge, lad. Nobody cheers the man who walks home.* I thought it was the saddest thing I ever heard, when I was young. I thought it meant the man who walked home was a coward."

"And it didn't?"

"It meant the man who walked home was the one who'd thought about it." Roderic shifted his weight. His back sent a long red message up into his shoulder blade and he ignored it. "I charged the crossbows this morning. You saw."

"I saw. I told you not to."

"You did. And I went anyway, because I'm an old fool and there was a gap and I've never in my life seen a gap I didn't want to ride through." He looked out at the Grey. "But I didn't shout. You noticed that?"

"I noticed."

"First time in thirty years. My back had the breath. I went up the dune like a thief in the night, not a word, until the witch nearly fried me off the horse, and do you know, boy, it was the best charge I've ever made. They never knew I was there till I was on them. Thirty years I've been telling the enemy I was coming." He gave a short laugh, without much in it. "Fifty-two years old, and I learned something today. Don't tell the songs."

Marcus looked at him sideways.

"That's not what you came up here to say."

"No," said Roderic. "It isn't."

He was silent for a time. Below them, on the black beach to the east, a fire had been lit beside the wreck of the *Clemency*, a big one, and he could see the cohort gathered round it, small dark shapes against the gold, and hear them very faintly over the water: a voice raised, and laughter.

"I watched you at the chapel," he said. "When the witch and the engineer came with the drain. You wanted to go up it. I saw it on you, the way I've seen it on you since you were twelve. You'd have gone first through that grate, and you'd have been first up into whatever was waiting, and you'd have been very brave and very likely dead, and two hundred men would have been standing under that wall in the tortoise with nobody in the middle of it talking." He did not look at the boy. "And you didn't go."

"Rufo asked me not to."

"Rufo asked. You chose." Roderic turned his head at last, and looked at him. "That's the whole of it, Marcus. That's the whole of command, the part nobody puts in the songs. It's not the charge. Any fool can charge; I'm the proof. It's standing still in the place where they can see you, while somebody else goes up the drain. It's letting someone you—" he stopped, and chose another word, "—someone you need, go where you'd rather go yourself. And then carrying it, whatever happens. That's the hardest thing I know. I'm not sure I've ever done it well. You did it today."

The boy looked at him for a long moment. His face had gone very still, and in the dusk, with the scar along his jaw and the soot still in the lines of his forehead, he looked older than Roderic had ever seen him. He looked, for one moment, like his brother.

"General," said Marcus. "One day I'm going to ask you something."

There it was.

Roderic had known it was coming since the quay. Since before. Since the day three years ago when a drugged prince had been carried off a ship in the Lower Harbor on a litter, and Roderic had come down to the quay to see him, and stood looking at him, and thought: *One day he'll be sober. One day he'll think to ask.* His mouth was dry. His back was screaming. He made himself hold the boy's eyes.

"I know," he said.

"Not tonight."

"No."

"But I will."

"Ask me sober," said Roderic. "And ask me on a day we can spare. Not on a wall, with a fort to hold and two hundred thieves on the beach. Ask me on a day when there's nothing else to do afterward but sit with it. Because there won't be, boy. There won't be anything else to do."

Marcus nodded slowly. He did not look away. Then he finished the spring water in his cup, and made a face at the taste of it, and set the cup down on the parapet.

"Go down to the fire," Roderic said. His voice came out rougher than he meant. "Go on. Your men are drinking the Tharsians' wine on the beach, and they need to see you there not drinking it. That's the other half of the job. I'll hold the wall."

"With your back?"

"With my back, my bad knee, my nine wounds and my grandfather's sword," said Roderic of the Long Field. "Go on, Centurion."

The boy went.

Roderic stood on the wall alone and watched him go down the long slope of black rock in the dusk toward the beach and the fire, a dark figure growing smaller. He watched until he could not tell him from the shadows. Then he put his face in his hands, on the cold stone of the parapet, and stayed like that for some time.

*A day we can spare,* he thought. *Sunstone keep me, let there not be one for a while.*

---

**ALANA**

The cohort had found the garrison's wine.

They had found everything else too. The fort's storerooms had been stocked for a Tharsian winter, and Oppius the baker had gone through them that afternoon with the expression of a priest entering a temple. Now there was a whole salt pig turning on a spit made of a broken oar over the fire on the black beach, and the *Clemency*'s after hatch-cover, a great square of black oak planking, set up on two water-casks beside it for a carving-table, and barley bread from the fort's own ovens, and hard yellow Tharsian cheese, and dried figs, and onions, and a barrel of pickled fish that nobody would touch but Atticus and his Vessans, who ate it with their fingers and made noises of deep contentment. And there was wine. Twelve skins of it, and a cask, red and thin and sour, Tharsian soldier's wine, and two hundred thieves who had been dry for four days.

Alana had not meant to come down. She had meant to sit in the cave under the fort with the Heart-Stone and the crates of shards and the quaestor's ledger, and write, and think about Livia, and about the word *stock*, and about the fact that she had been glad, for one terrible moment, when Iason's sword went in. She had sat there for an hour. Then the healer had come down the stair, and looked at her, and said, *Go and eat something, Alana of Corvus, or I'll have the barber carry you,* in a voice that Alana understood was not a joke.

So she came down to the beach in the dark, with her bandaged hand held against her chest, and stood at the edge of the firelight.

They saw her. The noise around the fire faltered and went on again, lower. She saw them nudge each other. Two hundred men who had watched her burn the sea, and lie face-down on the deck while her fire ran round them like water round a stone. She saw what was in their faces. It was not quite fear. It was the thing men feel in a temple, or at the edge of a cliff.

Felix rose from his place by the fire and came to her, with a stool.

He had found it somewhere in the fort, a little three-legged milking stool, and he set it down for her a few paces back from the fire, apart from the men, on a dry patch of sand, and dusted it off with his sleeve, and bowed.

"My lady," he said. "If you'd care to sit. I'll bring you a plate."

Alana looked at the stool.

She looked at the fire, and the men around it on the black sand in their filthy cloaks, packed shoulder to shoulder, eating with their hands. Rufo with his split shield propped behind him like a chair-back. Oppius at the spit with the fat running down his forearms. Hostus, Fingers, Atticus, the drum-boy Kaeso asleep already with his head in the baker's lap. Pell. Two hundred faces lit gold and red, watching her, waiting to see where she would sit.

She had sat apart her whole life. At the long tables of Corvus, at fifteen, the youngest by ten years, with the masters' eyes on her. At the high table of the Lesser Hall, at the bottom of it, with a prince reading her sonnet aloud. In the lodging-house on the Silk Row, under the roof, alone with a trunk. On the *Clemency*, in the stern cabin with the oil. She had been apart so long she had begun to think it was a thing she was, like being tall, or left-handed.

"Thank you, Felix," she said. "No."

She walked past the stool, and past him, into the firelight, and stopped in front of Fingers, who was sitting cross-legged on the sand with a little heap of knucklebones and a heap of Tharsian copper in front of him, and looked down.

"Move over," she said.

Spurius Lento looked up at her with his mouth open. Then he moved over.

She sat down on the black sand beside him in the last bolt of Corvus silk, with the oil still in its hem and the char on it to the knee, and held out her good hand.

"I'll need a stake," she said. "I haven't any money. The king's credit is in the bottom of the bay."

---

The fire went very quiet.

Then Rufo laughed. It came out of him like a bark, the way the prince's had on the beach. And somebody else laughed, and somebody pushed a wineskin along the sand toward her, hand to hand, until it came to Fingers, and Fingers, with an expression of the deepest suspicion, held it out.

She took it. She drank. It was terrible. It tasted of goat and vinegar and the inside of a boot. She drank again, a long swallow, and wiped her mouth on the back of her bandaged hand, and passed it on.

"Gods," she said. "The Tharsians deserve to lose."

The fire roared.

---

She took six coppers off Fingers in the first three throws, and he looked at her, and she looked back at him with perfect innocence, and took four more.

"You're counting," he said at last, accusingly.

"Of course I'm counting. There are five bones and each has four faces and they're not evenly weighted. That one," she pointed, "has a chip off its back and lands belly-up two throws in three. Any first-year student of Corvus could tell you that. It's arithmetic."

"It's *cheating*."

"It's *scholarship*," said Alana. "There's a difference. There is currently a difference." She swept the coppers toward her. "You've been cheating me with that chipped one since the first throw, Master Lento. I simply cheated better."

Fingers stared at her for a long moment. Then a slow grin spread across his ugly face, the first she had ever seen there.

"Eleven years in the harbor prison," he said, "and I never had a cellmate as crooked as you."

She found, to her own astonishment, that it was the nicest thing anyone had said to her in two years.

---

It was Rufo who asked for the song.

The pig was bones by then, and the bread was gone, and the wine had gone round enough times that men were lying back on the warm black sand with their hands behind their heads, looking up at the place where the stars would have been if the Grey had let them through. Someone had been singing, badly. A soldier's song about a girl in Vessa with a wooden leg. Atticus and his Vessans had objected to it on grounds of local pride and sung another, worse. The drum-boy had woken up and kept time on an upturned cooking pot.

And Rufo, propped on his split shield, with the firelight in the deep lines of his face, said: "Your turn, lady."

She looked at him.

"You've had us all singing," the optio said. "Fair's fair. Give us one of yours. One of the Corvus ones. Something high. Something a lady'd sing." He meant it kindly. She could see that he did. "We've not heard a proper song in years, some of us. Not since the prison."

The whole fire turned to look at her. She saw them settle, the way men settle in a hall when the harper is about to play: something softer coming into their faces, something almost shy. They wanted a lady's song. Something sad and fine about a tower and a lost love and the sea. They wanted to sit by the fire and be sad, prettily, and feel for an hour that they were the sort of men a lady would sing to.

*Not a lady,* she thought. *I've told every one of you. On every deck of that ship.*

And then she thought of the quaestor, sitting down on his stool with his hands at his throat and that look of great surprise on his face. And of Captain Merula, with his reading lenses on their cord. And of the white closed hand on the red banner, and the word *stock*, and Livia, writing her letters, asking to be told.

She had not composed a line in the old high meter in eight years. Not since a boy had read one aloud at a high table to make a room of drunk men laugh. She had sworn off it. But her hand still slipped into it when she wrote her notes, and her tongue knew it, the way a sailor's legs know a deck.

She stood up.

She stood up on the black sand in the firelight with the wineskin in her good hand, swaying very slightly, and cleared her throat, and lifted her chin exactly as old Master Hesper had taught her to stand when she read before the full chairs of Corvus, and gave it to them in the high meter. In the grave, rolling, six-beat measure of the great laments, the one the court poets used for the deaths of kings, every stress in its place, every pause exact:

> *Attend, ye hosts, and hear the fall of Sabellius the Wise,*
> *Who counted every stone on Cyrene, and every dead man's eyes;*
> *Who weighed the souls of fishwives, and wrote them down as stock,*
> *And never in his ledger found the measure of his*—

She told them what he had never found the measure of.

It rhymed. It scanned. It was, in the meter of the deaths of kings, the filthiest couplet ever composed on the island of Cyrene, and she went on. There were eleven more. One for each of Captain Merula's men, and what they had been doing in the bell-tower while the gate came open, and what the white closed hand of Tharsis was in the habit of closing on, and a final stately couplet about King Lucan himself and his famous ledgers that made Oppius the baker drop the spit into the fire and Kaeso fall off his cooking pot.

She did not know where any of it came from. It came the way the fire had come off the deck: once it started, it ran.

By the third couplet the fire had stopped being a fire and become a single animal, roaring. By the sixth, men were rolling on the sand. Atticus was weeping. Rufo had his face in his hands and his shoulders were shaking and he was making a noise like a saw going through wet oak. Fingers was on his back with his knees up, kicking. Felix, by the empty milking stool, had turned the color of the pickled fish and had both hands pressed over his mouth.

And when she finished, on the last couplet, on Lucan, in the dead grave fall of the lament's last line, there was a silence for one heartbeat. And then the whole black beach went up like oil.

She sat down. Her face was burning. Her heart was going like the drum on the *Clemency*. She took a long drink from the wineskin, because her hand needed something to do, and passed it to Fingers, who took it without looking because he was still lying on his back on the sand, wheezing.

"*Lady,*" gasped Oppius from the other side of the fire, wiping his one eye with his floury forearm. "Lady, gods forgive me—"

"Not a lady," said Alana.

"*No,*" said Oppius, with deep feeling. "No. By the Sunstone. You are *not*."

And somebody, she never knew who, at the back of the fire in the dark, called out, "*The Chair!*" And raised a wineskin. And the cohort took it up, as they had taken up the prince's word on the deck at dawn, two hundred thieves on a black beach banging their cups and their spoons and their split shields together: *The Chair! The Chair! The Chair!*

She did not cry. She was very nearly sure she did not cry.

---

It was the coppers that did it.

They sat in a little heap on the sand by her knee, sixteen of them, Tharsian, stamped with the closed hand, and every time the fire flared she saw them, and every time she saw them she saw Fingers' face when he had worked out that she was counting. He had laughed. He had called her crooked, and meant it kindly. And she had sat there with her heap of coins like a moneylender, a woman who had been given a seat at the fire and had at once set about taking something from the men who gave it.

*You cheated a thief,* she told herself. *He cheated first. It's even.*

It was not even. She knew it was not. She was the one who had arrived on this beach as a stranger, and they were the ones who had moved over.

"Master Lento," she said. "Again. One throw. All of it." She pushed the sixteen coppers into the middle of the sand between them. "Against whatever you like."

Fingers looked at the heap, and then at her, with the deep caution of a man who has lost to her six times.

"I've nothing left to stake, Chair. You've had it all."

"Then not money." She did not know what she meant until she heard herself say it. "If I lose, I'll pay in something I have."

The men nearest heard it. She saw the word go round the fire the way the word *forward* had gone down the *Clemency*'s waist, from mouth to mouth, and grow as it went. Somebody laughed. Somebody said *a song*, and somebody else said *she's sung*, and then Atticus the Vessan, of all men, who never spoke, took his pickled fish out of his mouth and said in his slow hill-voice:

"A dance. On the table."

There was a short, appalled silence, and then the fire roared again, and two hundred faces turned to see what she would say.

*Say no,* she thought. *Say it in the voice you use on the first-years. Say it as Master Hesper would have said it, and they'll laugh, and the moment will pass.*

"Done," said Alana. "Throw, Master Lento."

He threw. Two up, three down, the chipped one belly-up as it always fell. She took the bones and weighed them in her palm, and she knew exactly how they would land if she threw them flat and low with a little spin from the wrist, as she had thrown them all night. She threw them high instead, and loose, the way a child throws.

They came down wrong. All five. She did not need to count them.

"*Ohhh,*" said the fire, as one man.

Fingers looked at the bones. Then he looked at her, and she saw him understand, slowly, the way the town had understood the banner coming down. He opened his mouth. She gave him a look that she had once given a master of the second chair who had tried to correct her sums in front of the full academy, and he shut it again.

"The Chair loses," he said, rather hoarsely. "Fair throw."

---

She would ask herself why for a long time afterward. She would ask it in the cave under the fort with the shards, and on the decks of ships, and in the back of the weather-book where she wrote the things she could not write anywhere else; and she would never get an answer that she entirely believed.

The cheating, she would tell herself. A debt was a debt. She had taken something from them and she would give something back, and it had to be something that cost her, or it was not payment at all. That was true. It was the first reason, and the neatest, and she distrusted it for exactly that.

The second reason was older. She had been looked at all her life. At Corvus, at fifteen, a prodigy, examined across the long tables as one examines a calf with two heads. At court, at the bottom of the high table, a curiosity from the scholars' island. On the *Clemency*, by two hundred men who had seen her burn the sea and were afraid of her. Looked at, always, as a thing. And she had never once, in her whole life, chosen to be looked at. She had never once stood up in front of a room and said: *here, then. Look at this. I am giving it to you. You did not take it.*

And the third reason she did not let herself finish thinking, because it began with a hall full of lamps on a Midwinter night five years ago, and a girl of eighteen in a new violet gown walking the whole length of the Lesser Hall to stand in front of a prince, and the prince looking through her as if she were a window, and turning to someone else. She had never danced again. Not once. She had not even let herself remember the steps.

*He isn't here,* she thought, as she stood. *He's up on the wall with the General. Good. Good. I'm glad he isn't here.*

And the gladness was such a plain, flat, obvious lie that she nearly sat down again.

She did not sit down. She climbed onto the table.

---

The hatch-cover was black oak, broad as a bed, still sticky with the pig's fat and scarred where Oppius had carved. It rocked a little on its casks under her bare—

Not bare. She looked down. Boots. Of course. She sat down on the edge of the table, quite calmly, with two hundred men watching, and unlaced them, and pulled them off one and then the other, and threw them out into the dark onto the sand, and the fire whooped. Then she stood up in her stockinged feet on the warm oak and found that the stockings would not do either, they slid; so she sat again and peeled those off too, briskly, as a nurse peels a bandage, and threw them after the boots, and the whoop became a roar.

She stood up.

Her cloak was heavy with oil and smoke. She unpinned it at the throat and let it fall off her shoulders onto the planks behind her, and kicked it off the end of the table. She reached up and pulled the cord out of her hair, and the pins after it, one by one, and held out her hand with the pins in it; and Fingers, after a moment's paralysis, came and held out both his hands, cupped, and she let the pins fall into them like coins. Her hair came down. It had not been down in front of anyone since Corvus. Last, she untied the sash at her waist, the narrow violet sash of the academy that she had worn every day for eight years, and wound it once round her good hand, and let the end of it hang.

And that was all. She was a woman in a scorched violet robe and her own hair, barefoot on a ship's hatch, in the firelight, on a beach at the end of the world. It was not very much.

It was more than she had ever given anyone.

"Kaeso," she said. "A slow three. Like a heartbeat with a limp."

The drum-boy stared at her, and then turned his cooking-pot over and struck it, softly. *Dum*, da-da. *Dum*, da-da. And at the back of the fire, one of the Vessans, she never knew which, took out a little bone pipe of the kind the hill-shepherds use for calling ewes, and found the measure, and followed it, thin and high and sad.

She danced the Long Measure of Corvus.

It was the oldest of the court dances, the Midwinter dance, the one the delegation had taught to the capital and the capital had danced every winter since in the Lesser Hall. It was a dance for two. She danced it alone. She danced the woman's figure and left the man's out, so that at every turn there was a space beside her where a partner should have stood, and she turned to the space, and gave it her hand, and took her hand back, and went on.

Her bare feet knew the steps. She had been afraid they would not, and they did; they had kept the steps for five years in the dark like the book in the trunk, without being asked. And the steps, once they had her, would not stay polite.

The Long Measure was danced at court in a heavy gown, slowly, with the skirts held down. There was no one to hold hers. On the first great turn the scorched violet silk flared out from her like a bell, and on the second it went higher, swirling round her in a wide dark wheel, and the firelight went in under it and showed her legs to the thigh, pale and long and bare, the muscles moving in them as she turned. She did not reach down to stop it. She let it go where the turn sent it. The Vessan with the bone pipe heard what was happening and quickened, and the drum-boy quickened after him, and the old slow measure of the Lesser Hall began to change under her feet into something older than the Lesser Hall: the dance under it, the one the fisher-wives of Corvus had danced on the quays on Midwinter night before any court had tamed it, with the hips in it, and the shoulders, and the head flung back. The sash went out from her hand like a ribbon of violet smoke and wrapped round her waist and unwound again. Her hair whipped across her face and away. There was a shine of sweat at her throat and in the hollow of her collarbone, and the silk had begun to cling where it was damp.

Then she did the thing no court had ever seen.

At the top of a turn she drew a small rune in the air with the first finger of her good hand, a little hooked stroke, almost lazy, without the rod. And the fire beside the table answered her. Sparks came up out of it, not in the ordinary way, but in a long slow spiral, hundreds of them, gold and red, and came drifting over the heads of the men and gathered round her, and turned with her. They followed the hem of her skirt as it swung. They went round her in rings as she spun, like moths round a lamp, like the drowned lights of the Kettle Bank come up out of the sea to dance, and when she lifted her arms they rose with them, and when she dropped into the last low sweep of the figure they settled over her hair and shoulders and her bare arms like gold dust, and did not burn her.

Her burned hand ached. She held it to her breast and let it ache. The hatch-cover rocked on its casks with every step, and she let it rock, and the sparks went round her, and the warm wind off the island moved her hair.

She did not look at anyone. She looked at the space.

---

**MARCUS**

He had come down off the wall at a walk, because Roderic had told him to, and he had stopped at the edge of the dunes because of the drum.

He had meant to go down to the fire and sit among them and not drink, as the old man had said. He had meant to be seen. And then he had seen the table, and who was on it, and he had not gone any farther. He stood in the dark at the edge of the firelight with his cloak around him, where no one could see him at all, and watched.

He knew the dance before she had finished the first figure.

He had danced it every Midwinter since he was twelve. He had danced it badly and well, drunk and sober, with the daughters of half the lords of Aeridor. He had danced it at the Midwinter five years ago with the herald's daughter, whose name he could not now remember, while a girl in violet stood alone at the edge of the floor where he had left her. He knew every turn of it. And he knew, as he watched her turn and give her hand to nothing and take it back, exactly what was missing from it, and whose place the space was.

*Mine,* he thought. *That's my figure she's leaving out. That's where I should have been.*

He had thought her many things. A clever, sharp-tongued, inky child at the bottom of the high table. A wraith in violet, out of a burned city, who had come to watch him die. A terror with a glass rod on an afterdeck, with blood on her lip and the sea on fire behind her. A fellow officer, more or less; a mind he could trust with the stones. He had not let himself think of her as anything else. He had held himself a hand's breadth off from her for four days, on purpose, because whatever he wanted from her he had forfeited eight years ago at a high table, and because he was a drunk four days dry and a prince sent to die, and a man like that had no business wanting anything.

He had not known she could do this. He did not think *she* had known.

She was barefoot. Her hair was down. It was darker than he had thought, and longer, and it went out from her on the turns like the sash did, and the firelight went red and gold through it. Her skirts went out with it. Every turn lifted the violet silk in a wide swirling wheel and showed him her legs to the thigh, long and pale and strong, and every turn he told himself to look away and did not. He had watched a great many women dance. He had watched the dancing-girls of the Silk Row, who were paid to be watched, and the daughters of lords, who were paid in husbands; and none of them had ever moved like this, with nothing held back and nothing offered either, as if her body were simply a thing she had decided, tonight, for once, to live in all the way.

And the sparks. He did not know what she had done to the fire. He saw it rise up out of its bed in a slow gold spiral and come over the heads of the men and wrap itself round her, and turn with her, and follow the swing of her hem like hounds at heel. He had seen her burn the sea with that same hand. Now she was dancing in it, as if fire were a cloak she had put on to be looked at in.

Her face was quite grave through all of it, the face she wore over the casks and the weather-book and the dead, and her eyes were on the empty place beside her, and she was beautiful. Not pretty. He had known a hundred pretty women; he had made a career of them. She was beautiful the way the fire on the Kettle Bank had been beautiful, the way a thing is when it is wholly itself and does not care if you are watching. And he wanted her. He stood in the dark and admitted it to himself plainly, the way a man admits a wound once he has stopped pretending he can walk on it. He wanted her so badly that his shaking hands went still at his sides, as they did in a fight.

And they were all watching. That was the next thing he understood, and he did not like it.

Two hundred thieves, sitting round the fire on the black sand, gone utterly silent. Nobody whistled. Nobody called out. Oppius had stopped carving with the knife in mid-air. Rufo had come up off his split shield onto one knee, slowly, as a man kneels in a temple without knowing he has done it. Atticus the Vessan had his hand over his mouth. And Fingers, the little lockpick, the thief, stood at the foot of the table with her hairpins cupped in his two hands as if they were the King's Shard, and looked up at her with a face that Marcus would have struck him for, if he had had any right to strike anyone for anything.

He had no right. He knew it. He stood in the dark and felt it come up in him anyway, hot and low and ugly, the thing he had felt at sixteen when another boy took the horse he wanted: *mine*. He had thrown her away at a high table. He had looked through her at a dance. He had kept a hand's breadth off her on a prison hulk for four days because he was too proud and too ashamed to close it. And now two hundred men who had nothing in the world were sitting on a beach looking at what he had thrown away, and seeing it, and he was standing in the dark, alone, behind them.

*You fool,* said the voice in his head that sounded like Titus. *You poor proud stupid fool. You had eight years.*

The pipe stopped. The drum stopped. She turned, at the last figure, to the empty space, and curtsied to it, very low, the old court curtsy, the sash trailing from her hand onto the planks. And straightened. And stood there, breathing hard, barefoot on the black oak, in the silence.

Nobody moved. Nobody made a sound. There was only the surf on the beach, and the fire, and the long sigh of the island breathing up through the sand.

Then Decimus Rufo got all the way to his feet, and stood, and bowed to her. Not a soldier's bow. A courtier's, from the waist, stiff and clumsy and grave, the way he must have seen lords do it from the back of a hall. And one by one, without anyone saying anything, the men round the fire got to their feet as well, two hundred thieves standing up on a black beach in the dark, and stood there, and did not cheer.

She looked at them. Marcus saw her face change. He saw her not know what to do with it.

"Debt paid, Master Lento," she said at last, to Fingers, in a voice that shook only a little; and climbed down off the table, and took her hairpins back out of his hands, and sat down on the sand by the fire with her hair still down and her bare feet tucked under her robe, and reached for the wineskin.

And then she looked up, across the fire, and saw Marcus.

He did not know how long he had been visible. He had come forward without knowing it, into the edge of the light. She looked at him across the whole width of the fire, and he could not have said what was in her face, and he did not know what was in his. He knew only that he was not laughing, and that every other face around that fire was soft and stunned and shining, and his was not.

He wanted to go to her. He wanted to walk round the fire and sit down on the sand beside her where Fingers was sitting and say—what? *I remember the dance*? *I'm sorry*? He had said sorry to her in his head forty times on the *Clemency*'s deck and never once aloud. He had told himself it would only be for him.

It still would be. He knew that. But he had watched her dance with an empty space for five years' worth of a missing partner, and he thought, standing there in the edge of the firelight with his hands shaking: *Maybe it doesn't matter anymore who it's for. Maybe the only thing worse than saying it for yourself is never saying it at all.*

She looked away first.

---

**ALANA**

After a while she got up and went down to the water's edge, out of the light, because her face was too hot and the wine was too loud in her head. The surf was warm round her ankles, the island's breath, and it steamed in the cold night air. Behind her the fire roared on and someone had started the song about the girl in Vessa again. She stood with her arms wrapped round herself and looked out at the dark where the Grey stood up off the sea.

She heard him come. She knew his step now. She did not turn round.

Something was set down on the wet sand beside her feet. She looked. Her boots. Both of them, side by side, with the stockings folded neatly over their tops like the napkins on a high table. He must have gone hunting for them in the dark beyond the fire, on his knees on the black sand, while two hundred thieves watched him do it.

She did not know what to do with that at all. So she did nothing, and left them there.

"They're going to call you that for the rest of your life," he said behind her. "The Chair. You know that."

"I've been called worse."

"I know." He came and stood beside her at the water's edge. Not close. A hand's breadth off, as she had stood beside him at the mast. "I called you some of it."

She did not answer. The surf came in and went out round their feet.

He did not have a cup. She noticed that. He had been at the fire, and the wine was going round, and he did not have a cup, and his hands were shaking at his sides. She looked at them, and then at the wineskin in her own hand, which she had not realized she was still holding, and without thinking, out of nothing but long habit and the warmth of the fire and the stupid loose kindness that wine puts into a person, she held it out to him.

Then she understood what she was doing, and went cold, and snatched it back.

"I'm sorry," she said. "I'm sorry, I didn't think, I—"

"It's all right."

"It isn't. I'm a fool. I'm—" She threw the skin away from her, up the beach, onto the black sand, where it lay like a dead thing. "I'm sorry."

"Alana," he said.

It was the first time he had said her name. Not *lady*, not *the witch*, not *you*. She stopped talking.

"It was good," said Marcus.

She did not understand him for a moment.

"The sonnet," he said. He was not looking at her. He was looking out at the dark, at the Grey, with his jaw set. "At the high table. Eight years ago. I want you to know that I knew it was good. When I was reading it. I knew it was better than anything the court poets had written all that winter, and I knew it was you who'd written it, and I knew it was you in the violet, at the dance, and I read it out anyway, in that voice, because I was seventeen and a prince and drunk and I wanted the table to laugh more than I wanted anything else in the world." He stopped. "I'm not telling you to make it better. I can't. I decided on the ship I wouldn't say it at all, because it would only be for me. But I heard you just now, at the fire. In the same meter. And I thought, if I don't tell her it was good, she'll go on thinking that I didn't know. And I'd rather you hated me for the truth."

Alana stood very still in the warm surf.

She had imagined this. Of course she had. She had imagined it at fifteen and at nineteen and at twenty-three, in a hundred forms, on the cliffs of Corvus and in the lodging-house and on the deck of the *Clemency*: the prince, humbled, saying sorry. In every one of them she had known exactly what she would say. Something cold and precise and devastating. She had drafted it. She had *revised* it.

She could not now remember a word of it.

"I know," she said at last. Her voice came out small and strange. "That you knew. I always knew that you knew. That's why it—" She stopped. "That was the part that hurt."

"I know," said Marcus. "I worked that out at the rail."

The surf came in round their ankles, warm, and went out.

"You lost that throw on purpose," he said.

She felt her face go hot again in the dark. "You were watching."

"From the dunes. Since the drum." He was quiet a moment. "I've watched you throw bones all night, from the wall. You never threw a bad one. Then you threw like a child."

"I owed them. I'd cheated Fingers."

"Fingers," said Marcus, in a voice that was not quite his centurion's voice and not quite anything she had heard from him before, "held your hairpins as if they were the King's Shard."

She turned her head and looked at him.

He was not looking at her. He was looking at the Grey with his jaw set hard, the scar along it pale in the dark, and she understood, slowly, with a kind of disbelieving astonishment, what she was hearing. The prince of Aeridor. The second son, who had looked through her in the Lesser Hall as if she were glass. Standing in the warm surf on a beach at the end of the world, *jealous*. Of a lockpick.

It pleased her. It pleased her so suddenly and so fiercely that she was ashamed of it, and the shame did nothing at all to make the pleasure go away.

"You left out the man's figure," he said.

"There wasn't a man."

"There was a space." He said it very low. "I know that dance, Alana. I know where the space is. I danced the other half of it at Midwinter five years ago, with someone whose name I can't remember, while you stood at the edge of the floor." He stopped. Started again. "That was my figure. I should have been in it."

"Yes," said Alana. "You should."

"I'd dance it now." He turned to her at last. "If you'd let me. Here, on the sand. Badly. I'm a worse dancer sober, I've found. I'm a worse everything sober. But I'd dance it."

And there it was: the thing she had wanted at eighteen so badly that she had walked the length of the Lesser Hall for it, held out to her in the dark on a beach, five years late, by a man with shaking hands. She thought of the space beside her on the table. She had danced to it all night and never let herself ask who she was putting in it. She had told herself it was nobody. It was the debt. It was a gift to two hundred thieves. It was the first time in her life she had chosen to be looked at.

*Was it for him?* she thought. *Did I climb on that table, and take down my hair, and dance the Long Measure with a hole in it, for the one man on this island who wasn't even there to see?*

*He was there. He was in the dunes. Since the drum.*

*Did I know?*

She did not know if she had known. That frightened her more than the dead on the Kettle Bank.

"No," she said.

He nodded, as if he had expected it. He did not argue.

"Not tonight?" he said.

"Not tonight." She heard her own voice, and it was not steady. "I've been looked at enough for one night, Highness."

Something went across his face that was almost a smile, and was not.

They stood there. The surf came in and went out. Behind them the cohort had finished with the girl from Vessa and moved on, inevitably, to the Chair's couplets, which they were trying to reconstruct from memory and getting badly wrong, and arguing about.

"I'm not going to forgive you," Alana said.

"I didn't ask you to."

"Good. Because I'm not." She drew a breath. "Not yet. Perhaps not ever. I'll let you know. I'll write it down."

"In the weather-book?"

"In the back." She heard herself say it, and could not believe she had. "With the other dead."

He laughed. It was very short, and surprised, and there was something in it that was not laughter at all. And then neither of them said anything, and he did not move away, and she did not move away, and she was aware of the hand's breadth of warm wet night between his arm and hers as she had not been aware of anything since the book had turned under her palm in the dark. She could feel it like heat off a stove. She thought, quite clearly, in the cold analytical voice that came to her at the worst times: *If he moves his hand an inch, I will not move mine.*

He did not move his hand.

She did not know if she was glad.

"I want you to keep the shards," he said abruptly, in a different voice, the centurion's voice. "The twenty. The ones he cut. And the ledger, and whatever's in it. You're the only one of us who can read it the way he wrote it." He paused. "Gaius says there are forty planned in that ledger and twenty cut, which means there are twenty more in the stone that he meant for Lucan's pilots, and somebody in Tharsis is waiting for them. I don't trust the stones with anyone else. I don't trust them with me."

She turned her head and looked at him in the dark.

"You trust them with me," she said. "After the trunk. You know what's in the trunk."

"I know you've never opened it."

"Not yet," said Alana.

"Not yet," Marcus agreed. "I'm sober. Not yet is the only kind of promise I believe in anymore."

She looked at him for a long moment. Then she looked away, out at the Grey, because she found she did not trust her face.

"I'm going back to the fort," she said. "I'm going to go and write down the weather."

"It's dark. There's no weather."

"There is always weather," said Alana of Corvus, with great dignity, and bent, and picked up her boots, and turned, and walked away up the black beach barefoot in the warm surf with the boots in her hand and her robes dragging wet round her ankles, past the fire and the roaring cohort and the abandoned wineskin and the little three-legged milking stool, standing empty on the sand where Felix had set it down.

She did not look back. She was quite sure, almost, that she did not look back.

---

**MARCUS**

At dawn he went down to the slipways.

The engineer was there already. Liora had made him sleep, and he had slept three hours, she said, on the floor of the old sluice-house with the cell door wedged open and his books for a pillow, because it was the only place on the island he could. Then he had got up and come back to the slips. Gaius was standing on the head of the second slip in the grey light with his lenses on his forehead and his wax tablet in one hand and a stylus in the other, looking down the long stone ramp toward the sea. The black skeleton of a half-built hull stood on the slip below him, burned at the stern, sound at the bow. Steam rose white between its ribs from the culvert underneath. On the next slip down, in irons, in the cold, Captain Merula and his ten men were already at work under Rufo's eye, shovelling ash.

On the quay above them, a little way off, three women of the town stood watching with their arms folded. Nobody had asked them to leave.

His hands were wrapped in clean linen now, the healer's work, and he held the stylus awkwardly in the bandages, and used it anyway. The broken link of chain was gone from his ankle. The iron ring was still there; nobody on the island had a tool to cut it but him, and he had not got round to it.

"Four slips," said Gaius, without turning round. "Two steam-boxes, one of them cracked. Seasoned oak under the tarps in the third yard that the garrison never found, because I told them it was rotten. They never checked. Tharsians." He made a mark on the tablet. "Three hulls on the slips, two burned past saving, one I can save. Rope-walk intact. Pitch store intact. Forty-one shipwrights in the steam-box house, if the healer lets them up, and eleven Tharsian prisoners who are going to learn to use an adze if it kills them, which it may." He looked up at last. "Do you understand what you've done, Highness?"

"I took a town."

"Everyone else took a town yesterday," said Gaius. "*You* captured a navy." He turned back to the slip and the black ribs and the steam. "It just hasn't been assembled yet."

Marcus stood beside him in the cold and looked at it.

Then he took the oilskin packet out of his tunic. Not the old sheet. He had asked Alana for a page from the back of her weather-book, before dawn, at the door of the cave under the fort, and she had torn it out for him without a word, and not met his eyes, and given it to him. He unfolded it now. The four names from the Kettle Bank were on it in her hand, copied fair. Below them he wrote three more, in his own, with Gaius's stylus, pressing hard so that the wax-point scored the paper.

*Lucius Bassus, forger. Sextus. Little Gallo.*

Seven. Two hundred and five.

He folded the page and put it in the oilskin with the ninety-one, separately, and put the oilskin back inside his tunic against his ribs. His hands were shaking. He let them.

And up on the headland, behind him, the fort's bell began to ring.

He turned. Gaius turned. On the slip below, in the ash, the eleven Tharsians stopped their shovels and looked up. On the quay the three women of the town looked up. All along the waterfront of Cyrene, in the grey of the morning, doors were opening, and faces were coming out into the street, and turning up toward the fort.

There was a small figure in the open arch at the top of the tower, swinging on the bell-rope with its whole weight. He could not see it clearly at that distance. He did not need to. He knew who climbed anything.

It was not ringing for Titus. It was not ringing an alarm. It went on and on, wild and clumsy and joyful, the bell of Cyrene, rung by a barefoot fisher-boy in the dawn, for nothing, for no one, for the town.

Marcus listened to it. It was a real bell. He could hear it from the right side.

"Well," said Gaius, after a while, and made another mark on his tablet. "That'll need a new clapper."

---

# Chapter Nine: Page Two

**SYLARA**

*Tenth morning on the island. The fisher-boy rang the bell at first light. Nobody ordered it. Nobody stopped him. I count from the bell.*

The bell was still ringing when the boat went out.

She lay along the ridge of a net-loft above Netmakers' Row with frost in the wool of her hood and her belly flat to the slates, and watched it go. The slates were warm. The island breathed up through everything it had, so that a woman could lie all night on a roof in the dead of winter and freeze only on the side that faced the sky. She had stopped thinking it strange. She had not stopped being hungry.

The boat was a fishing smack with one brown sail patched in three colors, and there was a thief at the tiller.

She knew he was a thief because they all were. Two hundred of them had come up the black beach out of the sea yesterday morning, and they said it of themselves in the lanes, loudly, the way hill-men say they are from the hills. This one the others called Pell. He had two men of the town with him, and he threaded her through the gap between the mole towers. On the roofs of the towers, where yesterday there had been Tharsians at the bolt-throwers, there were convicts. They waved at him. He waved back.

Then the boat was out on the open water, and the Grey stood up ahead of it like a wall of dirty fleece, and took it.

She did not know why it was going. Humans could not take so much as a henhouse without sending word to somebody.

She envied it so hard her teeth ached.

---

It was not her town. It was not her war. She had come for the iron.

Every seventh day since the end of summer, five men in grey-green had walked into the western glade of Silverwood with iron flasks on their backs, and knelt at the roots of the old trees the way men kneel to pray, and when they stood up the earth under them was black. She had watched it from under the fern eleven times. She had watched the singers of the western glade, which had sung since before there were maps, go quiet one at a time, the way a wood goes quiet when a hawk is over it. She had watched Lyrielle, who felt the Heart-Grove in her own blood, begin to walk like an old woman.

The elders at the council roots argued for a month and came to it at last. Kill the five, and the iron comes back with fifty. Find where the iron is filled.

So she had found the ship that brought it: a lean, ugly two-master that called at the western shore every few weeks with full flasks, and took the empty ones away, and stank, for no reason anyone could tell her, of horses. She had lain three days in its hold among the empty iron, and come out of the Grey into this bay, and gone over the side in the dark and swum for the rocks in water as warm as a bath. That was ten days ago.

She had not seen the ship since. She had no stone.

A shard pointed home, to the island it was cut from, and nowhere else, and every stone on Cyrene pointed at Cyrene. Without a Silverwood stone a stolen boat would only carry her in circles in the Grey till she died of thirst. The ship would come back. Ships did. She was good at waiting.

*Humans arrive with maps,* the wardens told every child they took in. They had not needed to tell her. She had been six, and lying in the bracken at the edge of the wood, the day a banner came through her village. It was blue, the old blue of Aeridor, and it had not come to burn anything. It was passing through, on its way to somewhere that mattered. The men under it needed bread and roofs and firewood, and her village was a small mark on their maps that nobody had thought to ask about. Afterward there was no village. It was not even a story. It happened every year, somewhere.

For ten years now she had watched human commanders for the wardens, from trees and roofs and ditches. She was good at that too. She wrote them down.

---

*Page one. The landing. A drunk prince. A convict army. A witch. An old man on a borrowed horse. Dead by the second day.*

She had written that at noon yesterday, on this roof, with a lead point in the little book of bark-leaves the wardens used. Three of the ports had fallen by then. The fort had not, and a fort is not a customs-house. It had seemed generous at the time.

She had seen the hulk driven up the black beach, and the witch knee-deep in the surf flinging lightning the way a farm-wife flings out slops, and the old man pull a Tharsian off his horse with a boathook and climb into its saddle stiffly, an old man getting onto a horse that was not his, and then ride as if it were. The drunk she had from a cohort man under her eaves, who had told another, laughing, that the prince went through a tavern window into a canal at his own brother's funeral. It was the kind of thing men said of their commanders. Most of the time it was true.

The fort fell before sunset.

She had been wrong. She minded less than she should have. She was not often wrong about commanders. That was the point of her. But some part of her, under the warden, had been waiting ten years to be wrong about one, and it sat up now and looked about with interest, the way a hound lifts its head at a step on the path.

She told it to lie down.

---

*Page two. He spared them. Weak or clever. Insufficient data.*

She had come down off the roofs for that, hooded, her bow unstrung on her back, and gone up the hill in the crowd behind the netmender and his rope. In the gate they stood so close she could smell the cellars on them: damp, and lamp-smoke, and old fear, which smells of wet rust. Nobody looked at her. A town that has lived fifteen days in the dark does not look at strangers. It looks at the door. She had stood near enough to the prince to put an arrow through his eye, and noted it, as a warden notes the nearest tree.

She had braced for a speech. It had not come. He had talked to the netmender the way one man talks to another across a broken fence, and at the end of it the netmender still had his rope, and the Tharsians still had their necks, and nobody was happy. Then an old woman had walked out of the crowd and struck the youngest prisoner across the face with her stick, once, very hard, and walked back; and nobody stopped her; and the boy did not lift a hand.

Sylara had watched the prince while that happened, not the boy. He had not moved either. He had let it be done. She did not know what that meant, so she wrote the line about weakness and cleverness, which was what she wrote when she did not know.

Now she watched them work.

The eleven were on the slips from first light to dark, in irons, with a stride of chain between each man's ankles. They dragged the burned ribs off the third slip and shovelled the ash into barrows and wheeled it down to the sea. The optio with the split shield sat on a bollard above them all day and said nothing.

And the town came to watch. That was the part she had not expected.

It was never many. Three or four women on the quay with their arms folded, who stood an hour without a word and went home. An old man with a stool, who set it down every morning in the same place and sat with his hands on his knees until the light went, like a man waiting for a ferry. Children, who came in a pack the first day and jeered in the southern speech, and by the fourth were bored and went down to the beach to throw stones at the gulls.

Every morning the healer went along the head of the slips on her way to the steam-box house, the tall gold-haired one with her satchels, and every morning Sylara watched the eleven watch her go by, because she watched everything. Most of them looked at her as men in irons look at anyone who is not. Three did not look at all. A big red-faced one with a bad back. A small bald one who was always making the others laugh, and stopped when she passed. And a heavy grey one with a sergeant's shoulders, who did not stop shovelling and did not lift his head, and smiled at the ash. And a long thin boy, not much older than the one the old woman had struck, looked at her once and then down at the ground, as if the ground had been struck instead.

Sylara did not know what it meant. It did not go in the report either.

Sylara thought that was the most hopeful thing she had seen on the island. It was not the kind of thing that went in a report.

The rope she passed every night, on her way down off the roofs to steal bread. Three doors from her loft, on the netmender's door, it hung coiled on a nail, black with tar. Nobody had touched it. In the mornings she saw the netmender come out and stop and look at it, and go on.

---

*Third day. The prisoners know when the tender comes. The commanders do not.*

The steam from the roof-vents of the steam-box house hid a woman as well as the fern did at home, and from there she could hear most of the slips.

The Tharsians talked at the shovels, low, in their own round speech. It was near enough the isles' speech that a guard could have followed it with his ear bent, but it came round and swallowed at the ends, and the guards had stopped bending their ears the first morning. Sylara had more of it than they did. She had got it under the fern at home, from five men in grey-green complaining about the damp.

So she heard the youngest one ask. He was shovelling beside the captain, the grey stooped one with lenses on a cord, and the old woman's stick had left a yellow-green weal across his cheekbone.

*When does the tender come?*

*At the dark,* said the man on his other side. *Same as always. Shut your mouth.*

*The dark of what?*

*The moon, you calf. Shovel.*

The captain said nothing at all. But the next load he threw went into the barrow a great deal harder than the one before it, and the boy did not ask again.

The captain had told the prince, in the gate, that he did not know when the next ship was due. A liar, then, or a careful man. With clerks it came to the same.

Sylara lay in the steam and counted. She had kept the moon's count on a knotted cord at her wrist since Silverwood, because in the Grey there was no moon to keep it by. Nine nights to the dark. Ten at most.

She found she had closed her hand round the cord.

That afternoon she heard the engineer tell the prince. They stood at the head of the second slip, the engineer with his lenses on his forehead and his wax tablet in his bandaged hands, the prince in his patched cloak with his hands clasped behind him. She had noticed that. Most men forgot their hands. He never did.

"There's a tender," the engineer said. "The Survey's. She called once after the fall, to land their gear. I mended her rudder. She'll call again. I don't know when."

"Merula knows."

"Merula told you on the first day he'd not tell you." The engineer made a mark in the wax. "He let me keep my books. He'll keep that too."

The prince looked out at the gap between the moles.

"Then we watch the gap," he said. "Day and night."

"With two hundred men and four towers."

"Two hundred and five," said the prince, as if it mattered which. They walked on down the slip, and she lost the rest.

She knew the date. They did not. She was not here to help them. The ship was her way home, and if the prince's thieves took it, she would be stranded in worse company than before.

She wrote: *Third day. He keeps his hands behind him, where nobody can see them.* And under it, because it was true: *So do I.*

---

*Fifth night. The shipwright from the drain has not wiped his sword.*

She had marked him on the first night, because he was the most dangerous man on the island and nobody else seemed to have noticed.

The lanes said he was the one who had gone up the drain with the witch and cut the throat of the Tharsians' clerk under the fort. Then he had heard the prince give the eleven their lives, and turned his back, and walked out after the town. He slept now in a burned house on Rope Street. Every night he sat on its step with the sword across his knees and the blood gone brown on the blade, and looked down the hill at the slips.

At the pen.

The eleven slept in the old rope-store at the head of the fourth slip, a long stone shed with a barred door and a ring-bolt in the floor their chains ran through. The cohort called it the pen. One man stood its door at night with a spear and a brazier. From a second loft she had taken for the purpose, thirty paces up the hill from the pen, Sylara could see the door, and the step on Rope Street if she turned her head.

On the third day she had heard two cohort men at the barrows.

"The calf fired the third slip, the netmender says. Iason's slip." The man spat. "He'll do something."

"He'll do nothing. The Centurion said."

"The Centurion said," the first man agreed, and they both laughed, not kindly.

---

It was past midnight. The fog lay on the harbor below the slips, flat and white as a sheet on a bed, and left the slope clear; the brazier at the pen door made a small red room in the dark.

She saw him get up off the step.

He came down Rope Street without hurrying, the way a man walks to his work, the sword low in his hand. He walked into the brazier's light and stopped in front of the sentry, a heavy grey cohort man, and said something she could not hear.

The sentry looked at him a while. Then he leaned his spear against the wall, carefully, as if it might break, and took a ring of keys off his belt and set it on the lid of a water-butt beside the door. And walked away down the slip toward the beach, fumbling at his breeches as he went.

*The sentry has gone to piss,* she thought. *He'll be slow about it.*

She had strung the bow before she knew she meant to, and there was an arrow on the string, one of her twelve, with the green goose-fletching the wardens dye with alder catkins; and she was up on one knee behind the loft's chimney with the head of it laid on the middle of the shipwright's back. Thirty paces. Downhill. No wind. She could have put it through a ring.

*It isn't yours,* she told herself. *Not the town, not the boy, not the word.*

That was true. Under it, colder, was the other thing. If he killed the boy, the prince's word was a coin with nothing behind it. She would know what humans were. She knew already. It would be a relief, in a way, to have it written down.

The shipwright took the keys and opened the door of the pen.

She heard the chains first. Then he came out backward into the light, hauling the boy by the neck of his tunic, the boy on his knees with his chain loose from the ring and rattling on the stones. He put him down at the head of the slip, facing the sea, where the burned ribs of the third slip's hull had stood; and stood over him with the sword.

He wanted it seen. She understood that. It was the quay gallows again, in small.

The boy did not fight. He knelt with his chained hands on the stone, and shook, and made no sound.

In the dark of the pen the grey captain had got to his feet, as far forward as the ring-bolt let him, so that the brazier caught the lenses on his cord. He spoke once, in the isles' speech, flat and dry, a clerk reading out a sum.

"Shipwright. He's fourteen."

"So was I," said the shipwright, "when I swept Philon's yard. So was my sister."

The captain said nothing else. Sylara saw him decide not to.

The shipwright set the edge of the sword against the boy's neck, under the ear, carefully, the way a man sets a chisel in a mark.

Her draw came back to her cheek.

---

"Iason."

She had not seen the prince come. She had watched the door, not the slips, where he walked every night; and he came up out of the dark of the second slip into the edge of the light with no more noise than the fog.

He did not draw. He stopped five paces off with his hands at his sides, and in the red light she saw them.

They were shaking.

Not much. A fine quick tremor, like a bowstring a moment after the loose. She knew what fear looked like in a man's hands. Fear was stiff. This was something else. She put it away for later.

"Go back to your walk, Highness," said the shipwright, without turning his head.

"I've walked it. Four nights. You've watched me do it, from your mother's step." The prince's voice was low and ordinary. "I've watched you watch me."

"Then walk somewhere else."

"I gave my word for him, Iason."

"You gave it. I didn't."

"You did. On the ship, when you took that sword out of the king's arms chest, you gave me your name for it. When I give my word, I give it for you too. That's what the name was for." A pause. "I'm sorry. I should have said that on the deck."

The shipwright laughed. It was a horrible sound.

"He burned my slip. The third. Me and Philon and four others laid her keel last spring, before they took me for the chandler, and this one put a torch to her while my mother was lying on Rope Street with—" He stopped. The sword did not move. "You don't know anything about it."

"No," said the prince.

Sylara had expected him to argue. Commanders argued. He did not. He stood in the cold and let the shipwright be right, and the brazier settled with a soft crunch of coals.

Then he did a thing she had never seen a commander do. He looked past the shipwright, down at the kneeling boy, and said, "What's your name?"

The boy stared up out of his shaking.

"Your name. I had you bound on the roof of the eastern tower, and I never asked it. I'm asking now."

"Nonius." It came out in a whisper, with the Tharsian roundness on it. "Nonius, Highness."

"Nonius." The prince said it back carefully, as if he were fixing it somewhere. "How old?"

"Fourteen. Fifteen at the thaw."

"Don't," said the shipwright. His voice had gone strange. "Don't do that. Don't give him a name."

"He had one already. I just didn't ask." The prince stepped forward, once and again, and Sylara's arrow moved with the shipwright's back as it tensed. Three paces now. Inside the reach of the blade. Commanders had men for that. "Nobody asked your mother's either, Iason. The quaestor didn't. The Chair's read his book. *Rope Street: four, two, six, one.* That's how he wrote them down. I don't know which of those was your house. Liora knows. She has their names, your mother's and your sisters', and she says them every night." He stopped. "Cut that boy's throat and he goes in the same book. *One.* Somebody in Tharsis gets to write him down as one."

The shipwright's shoulders were shaking now too.

"And then? I hang? For a Tharsian?"

"Then I'd have to hang you." The prince said it without any weight on it, the way the engineer gave the length of a beam. "I said in front of the whole town that no man of his dies by my men's hand. I've no ships here, Iason, no gold, no writ. I've a word. That's all I brought. If you spend it tonight, I've nothing to give them next time. Not Orso, not the town, not the next Tharsian deciding whether to open a door." He drew a breath. "So I'd hang you. And then I'd hang you again every night for the rest of my life, on that wall, in my head, the way I go back to the stair. I'd rather not. I've enough names."

Sylara did not breathe.

The edge stayed on the boy's neck for three heartbeats. Four. She counted them in her own drawn arm. On the fifth the shipwright made a sound in his chest like a beam going, and the sword came away from the boy's skin and down, slowly, until its point rested in the ash on the stone, and he stood leaning on it like a man on a crutch, with his head down.

The boy knelt with his eyes shut, saying something over and over in Tharsian. Sylara had enough of the speech for it. It was not a prayer. It was the names of the days of the week.

---

The prince did not take the sword. He did not shout for the optio.

"There's a watch wants standing," he said instead. "This door. Every night until we're done with them. If anybody comes down that street for him, you stop them. Orso. The old woman with the stick. Any of my thieves with a skin of wine and a good idea." He looked at the sword in the ash. "You."

"Me," said the shipwright.

"You're the only man in Cyrene I'd believe. If you can keep him alive, nobody else in this town has an excuse." The prince's hands were still shaking. He put them behind him. "That's the bargain."

"And if it's me who comes?"

"It was you," said the prince. "Tonight. Look what you did."

The shipwright looked down at the sword lying in the ash, a long time, as if it belonged to somebody else.

The prince stooped and lifted the boy onto his feet and walked him back into the pen, past the grey captain at the end of his chain, and Sylara heard the chain go back through the ring and the lock go home. When he came out he had the keys. He held them out. The shipwright took them.

Then the prince went away down the slip toward the beach, where the sentry was still about his business, and she heard his voice down there, low, and then nothing. The sentry did not come back that night. The shipwright stood the door instead, with the keys on his belt and the sword in his fist, and did not sit until nearly dawn.

Sylara let the draw down, and put the arrow back with the other eleven, and sat behind the chimney with the bow across her knees.

Her hands were shaking.

They had not shaken at nine, when the wardens first gave her a bow. They had not shaken at sixteen, the first time she put an arrow into a man. They were shaking now, after a night when she had loosed nothing and nobody had died.

She did not write that down.

What she did, before the dawn came up grey out of the Grey, was open the book at the second page by feel, and find the line under *He spared them*, and draw the lead through it once, hard enough to score the bark.

*He spared them.*

That was all the page said now. It was the shortest line in the book, and it had taken her longest to write.

---

In the morning the heavy grey sentry was on the slips with a barrow, wheeling ash beside the Tharsians, without irons. Nobody said why. Nobody needed to.

That night the shipwright sat by the door of the pen with his back to the wall and the sword laid flat across his thighs.

He had wiped it. The brazier light ran clean along the blade from hilt to point, the way light runs along a new-split plank. He did not sleep, and he did not once look up at the burned house on Rope Street.

Toward the middle watch the pen door opened a crack from inside, and a hand set something down on the stone beside him. A heel of barley bread. The shipwright looked at it for a while. Then he ate it.

*Sixth night,* she wrote. *The shipwright has the watch. Somebody inside shares his bread with him.* And then, after a moment, because the report was the report, she added the name she had not wanted. *Nonius.*

---

*Seventh day. Went looking for the iron.*

The prince and his thieves were a page in a book. The iron was the reason.

She remembered the night of her landing in pieces. The crew swinging the empty flasks out on a whip from the yard, not up the water-gate stair but sideways and down, toward the black rock at the waterline. A voice saying *steady, steady*. The clank of iron going away from her into stone, as if the rock had a mouth.

And there was the smell. Twice now, when the wind came round into the west, she had caught it on the roofs under the island's own stink of eggs and forge: something sweet, there and gone. She knew it. She had knelt where the five knelt in the western glade, after they had gone, with her face to the black earth.

The tides that week fell lowest by day; by night the foot of the stair was under the sea. For ten days the Tharsians had kept a man on the water-gate stair, and for seven more the prince's slingers had, and she had waited for them to grow bored, as men on a post do. On the seventh day she stopped waiting.

So at low water in the afternoon she went along the foot of the headland, over the weed-rocks under the fort's seaward wall, flat on her belly more than not. Beside the foot of the water-gate stair, round a fold of black rock, half under a beard of weed, there was a door.

It was low, and iron, and set in the living rock at the waterline, so that at the top of the tide the sea would wash its sill. The door was old. The hinges were not. Nor was the lock, a heavy Tharsian box-lock, black with oil. She worked at it with her knife as long as she dared, and it did not care in the least.

She put her face to the crack between the iron and the rock.

It came out at her like breath from a sickroom. Sweet and black: a hive gone bad in the heat, honey left to sit on a wound. Her stomach turned over. Whatever the five poured into the roots of Silverwood had been here, behind this door, and was here still. She did not know what it was. She knew where.

A pebble clicked on the rocks above her.

She went flat against the foot of the door, and something caught at her shoulder as she went down, a small sharp tug, and she pulled free without looking, iron or weed, it did not matter which. Then she turned her head a finger's width.

Slingers. Three of them on the rocks above the stair, and their leader with them: the brown silent Vessan. He stood with his sling hanging loose and looked along the whole shore, slowly, the way a hill-shepherd looks along a hillside at dusk for the one ewe that is not where she ought to be. Not searching. Counting. His eyes came along the weed toward her, and went past, and stopped, and came back.

He said something in his slow hill-voice. Two of the others started down.

There was an outfall beside the stair, a square mouth in the rock where the fort's drains came out, half full of warm foul water. She went into it backward like an otter into a bank and lay with the stink of it in her mouth while the two slingers turned the weed over with their feet, and threw a stone into the drain-mouth for luck that struck the wall a hand from her face. The light went. They gave it up.

The leader did not. She could see his shape against the last of the sky, standing on the rock above the stair, quite still, looking down at the door. Then he went.

She lay in the drain an hour, to be sure. When she came out it was full dark and she stank like a tanner's pit, and at her shoulder, when she felt for it, there was a pulled place in the wool of her cloak, a little loop of green drawn out and broken off. She would mend it. She had mended worse.

*Seventh day. Found the door. Could not open it. The iron goes in there; the smell is the smell at the roots. The slinger with the slow voice sees like a shepherd. Do not go back by day.*

---

She went down to the black beach after, east of the fires, and scrubbed the drain off her skin in the warm surf with black sand. Then she lay up in the marram to dry.

The healer came along the beach alone, the golden-haired woman with the high gold collar who ran the hospital. She did not see anything. She walked down into the surf as she was, and sat down in it where the waves ran in and out, so that the warm water came up round her waist and drew back, and she drew her knees up and put her arms round them, and was still.

Sylara knew that stillness. She had seen it in deer, and in a hare that has felt the snare close, and once, long ago, in a girl of six in the bracken at the edge of a wood. It was the stillness of a creature that has gone as far into itself as it can go and pulled the door shut.

Then the prince came along the beach from the fires.

She watched him see the healer, and stop, and waited for the rest of it. Men went to a hurt thing. They put their hands on it and called that kindness, and the hurt thing bit them or died of fright.

He did not go to her. He did not go away either. He went up the beach to a place perhaps forty paces from the water and sat down on the black sand with his back to her, facing the dunes, so that for one bad moment she thought he had seen her. But his eyes were open on nothing. He sat with his back to the sea and the woman in it, and he did not turn his head, and he did not leave.

The wardens taught that with a hurt hawk in a mews. You sit within its sight, and you turn your back, and you stay. So it knows it is not alone. So it knows it is not hunted.

The healer sat in the surf most of an hour. Then she rose with the water running off her and came up the beach, and paused a pace from his back, and Sylara saw her lips move. The prince did not turn round. He only lifted one hand off his knee a little way, and set it down again. The healer went on up toward the town.

*Seventh night,* Sylara wrote in the dunes, by feel. *He knows how to sit near a hurt thing.*

She had not written that of a commander before. She had not often written it of anyone.

---

*Eighth day. A sail.*

The bell woke her. Not the boy's bell, wild and glad: the other kind. Short hard strokes, over and over, the bell of a town that has been taken once and knows the sound of its own fear.

She was in a cleft of the headland rocks, and came up onto one elbow with her heart going like a drum. *The tender.* But it was early; by her cord the dark was still five nights off, counting this one. Down in the bay the cohort were running for the moles, and the people of Cyrene stood in their doorways with their hands at their mouths.

A ship came out of the fog.

It was a galley, long and low, her oars going like a beetle's legs, with a blue banner at her stern. The old blue of Aeridor. And at her masthead a long pennant with a silver dolphin on it.
