Sylara lay on the rock and looked at the blue. She was cold on the side that faced the sky and warm on the side that faced the stone, and for the space of twenty oar-strokes she was six, and in the bracken, and could not have moved if the rock had caught fire.

The bell stopped. In the galley's bow, waving both arms, was the thief, Pell, eight days gone in a fishing smack and back in a king's ship, grinning like a boy who has stolen a pie and been handed a second.

There was a woman beside him.

She stood with one hand on the stem-post, in mail, with a fur cloak over it and a round iron-rimmed shield slung on her back. Her fair hair was braided close to her head, soldier-fashion, and grey at the temples. A scar crossed her face, and she had not troubled to hide it. In her other hand, upright beside her like a standard, she held a long-hafted thing taller than herself, a broad blade with a hook curving off its back: a hedging-bill, the kind hill-farmers use to cut thorn, grown up and gone to war. She was not waving. She was looking at the towers one after another, and Sylara watched her count them.

The galley came on through the gap, and Sylara could read the name on her bow, picked out in old gilt. *Patience.*

*Humans,* she thought, *name their ships for what they haven't got.*

On the quay, the old general came down to meet it. He came slowly, on the grey Tharsian mare, sitting very straight in the way of a man whose back will let him sit no other way, with his beard combed. He stopped at the end of the quay and waited, like a man at the door of his own house.

The woman in the bow turned her head and looked at the horse. Sylara saw it from the headland and knew it, because she had worn that look herself. It was the look she gave an axe in Silverwood. Not hate, exactly. Measure. Where it would go in. How it could be stopped.

She took out the book, and turned past page one, and page two with its single line, to a clean leaf, and wet the lead on her tongue. Then she looked at the galley, and the woman, and the horse, and the old man, and the prince coming down the hill from the fort at a run with his cloak flying behind him.

She suspected, with some irritation, that she was going to need a great many more of them.

---

# Chapter Ten: The Writ

**VALERIA**

The first thing Valeria saw of Cyrene was the bolt-thrower.

It came out of the Grey before the island did: a squat round tower on the end of a stone arm, and on its roof a timber frame with a bow across it like the horns of an ox. The frame came round, slowly, the way a farm dog's head comes round to follow a stranger down a lane, and settled on the *Patience*'s bow, and stayed there. Then a second tower came out of the fog a cable off, and its frame came round too.

Shot crossing over the gap, where any ship that wanted the harbor would have to come. Valeria stood in the bow with her round iron-rimmed shield on her back and did the sum, and did not like it.

"Those are ours?"

"Ours, Captain." The thief beside her was grinning. Pell, his name was. He had sailed a fishing smack home to the king with a letter, and been washed twice by order and kept in a room with a carpet, and had talked about the carpet most of the voyage. "All four ports are ours. The prince took the east one up a sea-stair. Two ran. The General took the last by lying about the Chair."

"The chair," said Valeria.

"You'll see."

She watched the crews instead. Convicts, by the look of them, and one in a woman's knitted shawl. But they had held the *Patience* from the edge of the fog and would hold her, Valeria judged, all the way to the quay, without once looking round to see who was watching. Whoever had set those crews knew his trade. Valeria put that on the list she kept of things that might kill her. She had been keeping it nineteen years.

---

She had been four days on the *Patience*, two of them in the Grey being sick over the side with what she hoped was the dignity of an officer. A ship was a wall that moved, and she did not approve of walls that moved. Her braids had worked loose of their pins in the Grey, fair going to iron at the temples, and she had pinned them back tighter, as one tightens a girth. The cold had got into the old seam on her face, the white line from the corner of her left eye to the hinge of her jaw, so that it pulled when she spoke. Ladies at court had told her, kindly, that a little paint would hide it. She had told them, kindly, that it had taken a great deal of trouble to get.

Behind her along the waist stood the Ford: sixty of them, all that was left of the Fourteenth that could still stand in a line, in mail and old blue cloaks, their bills stacked along the rail like a hedge in winter. A hooked blade on an ash pole taller than a man: the tool a hill village uses to lay a hedge, and the tool it uses when somebody rides through the hedge. Shields in front, bills behind. Nobody had ever made a song about it. That was one of the things she liked about it.

Her own bill, older than the rest, had a hook ground back so many times the steel was thin as a leaf. It had a name, which she did not tell people she had just met.

Against her ribs she carried the writ, with Varro's countersign under the king's name like a boot-print under a signature, and a small packet sealed with the king's private ring for the prince's own hand.

She had known Titus nine years and stood at his left shoulder for most of them, and at Hollowmere she had held the causeway behind him until he sent her back. She had heard about the eulogy from eleven people since, every one telling it as a scandal and every one getting the letters right. Titus had written three. She had watched him write the third.

She had seen the prince once herself, at a Midwinter in the Lesser Hall, asleep under the high table with his head on one of the king's hounds. Someone had put a garland on them both.

---

The fog drew back off the bay like a net hauled in.

A black headland with a fort and one square bell-tower. A town under it with every gutter steaming like a run horse. And on a black beach to the east, the wreck of an old long hull run up the sand on her side, with the surf steaming round her stern like breath round a dead ox.

Up on the headland a bell began to ring, and stopped.

"The *Clemency*," said Pell, and for once he did not grin. "Nerva goes and sits with her of a night."

The *Patience* went in through the gap under the two towers.

The quay was crowded with gaunt townsfolk, and among them the convicts, some carrying shields. A barefoot boy caught the bow-line and made it fast with three turns and a hitch, without looking at his hands. Along the quay a young woman in a scorched violet robe sat on a bollard writing in a book, with a smear of ink along her jaw and her right hand bound in linen. She did not look up when the galley came in. She looked up once, at the fog, frowned at it as if it had got a sum wrong, and wrote something else. Valeria began to suspect she had found the chair.

At the end of the quay sat Roderic of the Long Field, on a tall grey mare with the white closed hand of Tharsis branded on her quarter. He sat her as a man sits his own fireside chair. His beard was combed. Valeria had never in her life seen it combed.

He looked down at her and his whole face cracked open.

"Valeria. By the Sunstone. They sent *you*."

Valeria looked at him. Then at the mare. The mare looked back at her out of one large mild eye, and blew.

"Of course you found a horse," said Valeria.

Then the crowd at the landward end of the quay opened for a man in a black cloak, coming down the fort road at a run. At the crowd's edge he checked, and came on at a walk. Valeria had seen that on drill-fields too: a man who had run, and did not want to be seen to have run.

A mourning cloak gone brown at the hem with salt, patched at the shoulder with a square of Tharsian red, which was either a joke or a necessity and might have been both. Dark hair cut short and badly. A scar along his jaw, still pink at the edges. He was thinner than the boy under the table, and he stopped at the plank's foot and stood very still with his hands clasped behind him, parade-fashion.

He was not drunk.

She looked for it where it always was: the eyes, the color in the face, the care in the feet. It was not there. His eyes were clear and very tired.

"Captain," he said. "Welcome to Cyrene."

"Highness." She came down the plank and did not bow; the Ford did not, as a rule. "Valeria. Shield-captain of the Fourteenth, what there is of it. I've sixty of the Ford, grain, a smith, two hundred new shields, and a writ from your father I'm ordered to read aloud on the quay before anything else is done."

"Then read it," said the prince, and unclasped his hands to take the writ and turn it to see the seal, and give it back.

They shook.

A fine steady tremor, like a rope under strain. She had seen that shake on a hundred garrison mornings and knew what it was asking for. She looked at the hands, and then at his face; and he saw her look, and did not put them behind his back again. He let her see. That was the strangest thing she had yet seen on Cyrene, and she had seen a man in a shawl crewing a bolt-thrower.

---

She read the writ from the plank as she gave orders on a drill-field: flat and slow and loud, each word set down by itself, like stones in a wall.

*"Aurelian, by the grace of the Sunstone King of Aeridor and of the Isles, to all who hear this read. Know that Marcus our son, and Roderic called of the Long Field—"*

She read the name of the field in the same voice as the rest. She had had nineteen years to practise it.

*"—are pardoned of all that was charged against them on the day of our son Titus's burial, and restored. Know that Marcus our son is named Prefect of Cyrene and its waters, to hold them for the crown. Know further that the sentences of the men of the penal cohort sent with him are suspended—"*

It went into them like a charge into a line. A big optio with a split shield and a face like a slab of oak opened his mouth and shut it. A one-eyed giant with flour in the hair of his arms put his hand over his face. A boy with a drum looked round at the men either side of him, because it was on their faces before he understood it.

You did not stop, on a drill-field.

*"—suspended for the length of the war, and only while they serve in Cyrene and its waters, and for no longer, and no farther."*

And it went into them again, differently, like a man finding a stone in his bread. The faces did not fall. They stopped. At the back somebody laughed once, short and ugly, and somebody near him hit him, not hard, and he was quiet.

*"Given under our hand. Aurelian. And by our Lord Marshal, Gnaeus Varro."*

She rolled it up. The steam hissed in the gutters.

Then the optio spoke, trying the word to see if it would bear weight.

"Suspended." He thought about it. "Well. That's more than we had at breakfast."

And the old man on the grey mare made a noise like a bear that has sat on a thistle.

"That," said Roderic of the Long Field, "is *my* line."

The quay laughed. It was not much of a laugh, but when it was done the faces had changed a third time.

---

The new shields came down the plank on the backs of the Ford, lime-wood painted in the blue of Aeridor with the crowned dolphin on the boss, two hundred of them, bought with a day and a night of shouting at quartermasters.

The convicts would not take them. Nobody refused. The optio with the split shield stood beside the blue piles and did not pick one up; and behind him the men who carried shields came and stood, and did not pick one up either. Eighty of them, waiting very politely, like men outside a magistrate's door.

Valeria looked at their shields, and knew them.

They were the Fourteenth's, struck off the stores when the Fourteenth was struck off the army's list after Hollowmere: split and patched and stained, and on every one, stenciled a hand high in faded red, *XIV*. She had mixed that red once, in her first spring, rust and ox-blood and linseed. The Ford had asked the council, in writing, for these shields to be burned on the hill above the capital with the legion's dead, as was decent. The shields had gone instead, under the King's own seal, in a chest to a prison hulk called the *Clemency*; and nobody at court could tell her whether that was a joke, or an insult, or something else.

"Those are ours, Captain." Old Baebius, the Ford's file-closer, with his one ear and his bill on his shoulder. He did not say it loudly. "They've no right."

The optio heard. "Decimus Rufo, Captain. Optio." He lifted the split shield so she could see the notch near the boss, the shape of a lance-head. "We stood under these on the ship when the dead came in through the ports. On the beach. An hour in the tortoise under that wall, with the bolts coming down. Two men died under them. Sextus and Little Gallo. We know what the letters mean, Captain. We'd like to keep them, if it's all the same to you."

A finger from his heart, Valeria thought, looking at the notch.

She thought of the fire she had meant to build on the hill, and the sixty behind her who had wanted to stand round it. She thought of Titus in his tent the night before the ford, saying a legion was not its number or its paint but whoever was still standing under the shields when morning came. She had thought it the sort of thing a good officer says before a bad day. She had not thought he meant it.

"Keep them," she said.

Baebius made a sound. She did not turn round.

"Keep them," she said again, louder, so the Ford would hear it from her and not from each other. "Condemned arms go to condemned men. That's the regulation." It was not. Nobody on the quay knew the regulations better than she did, and nobody contradicted her. "Carry them well, optio. They've been carried well before."

Rufo struck the split shield once with the flat of his sword, and the eighty behind him did the same, together, and the sound came back off the headland.

She would feel it later, in the night, the way she felt the old scar when the weather turned; and Baebius would not speak to her before tomorrow, and would be right not to.

---

She gave the prince Varro's orders at the top of the quay, with Roderic sitting his grey off to one side, pretending to look at the sea. She gave them to his face.

"Four orders, Highness. You hold Cyrene. You don't sail beyond its waters without the council's leave. I report everything to the council, by every ship, and I will." She held his eyes. "And the fourth the Lord Marshal gave me privately, so I'll give it to you in daylight. If you drink, I'm to bring you home."

He looked down at his own hands, shaking at his sides, and back at her.

"Then you'll have a long, dull job, Captain."

"I've had those," said Valeria. "They're the ones I survive."

She held out the little packet with the black cord. "And this. From his own hand. Not the council's."

He weighed it in his palm, once, like a coin he suspected, and she saw him know by its weight what was inside. He put it unopened in his tunic, against his ribs, beside an oilskin of his own.

"Thank you," he said. His voice was not quite what it had been.

---

She saw the Tharsians on the way up through the town.

On the nearest slip, in the stinking steam, eleven men in red cloaks were shovelling ash in irons. One was short and broad and grey, with a clerk's stoop and reading lenses on a cord. Another was hardly more than a boy, with a yellowing bruise across one cheek and raw red rings round his ankles where the irons had rubbed. On a bollard ten paces from the boy a black-haired young man of the town sat with a wiped and oiled sword lying bare in his lap, watching him. Three women stood above on the quay with their arms folded, watching them all.

"Merula's garrison," said the prince. "They gave me the bell-tower on my word. They build ships for us now, where the town can watch."

Valeria thought of the dead coming up out of the river at Hollowmere in the Fourteenth's own blue cloaks. These red cloaks had served the king who sent them.

"You should hang them," she said. "Varro would have."

"Varro wasn't here," said the prince mildly, and went down the slip.

He went alone, down among eleven men with iron shovels, past them as if they were vines in a row, and crouched in the ash by the boy and lifted the iron cuff to see under it.

Valeria was down the slip before she had decided to go, at the long easy walk the Ford used to close a gap; and she was four paces off when a big red-faced Tharsian straightened suddenly from his shovel, the shovel coming up in his chained hands, and the hook of her bill was round the back of his neck with its edge on the big vein under his ear before the shovel had risen a foot.

Nobody moved. The big man rolled his eyes sideways at her and moved nothing else.

"His back," said the prince, from the ash, without getting up. "He straightens like that every quarter of an hour, Captain. Rufo's been watching him do it for eight days."

The big man set the shovel down very slowly and put both hands into the small of his back, and his face twisted. She had seen that face on Roderic.

She took the hook away, and said nothing until they were out of the steam.

"Highness. I'll say this once, so that it's said, and then every time it needs saying, because you will make me. You keep mistaking the edge of a cliff for the front line."

"His ankles are going bad. Nonius, his name is. If the irons aren't padded he'll lose a foot before the month's out. I wanted to see."

"You could have sent the barber. You could have sent me."

He looked at her. "I didn't know you yet," he said.

She found she was angry, and did not know at whom.

---

The healer came up from the long shed at the head of the second slip while they stood there.

Honey-gold hair bound up in linen, a leather apron, a high collar of gold sun-thread with a pale blue stone, a bruise on her cheekbone faded almost to nothing. Valeria knew her before anyone said. The Vigil woman looked at the blue cloaks of the Ford, and knew them too.

"You took my wounded south," Valeria said. It was not what she had meant to say. It came out flat as a charge-sheet.

The healer did not flinch. "Yes. Eighty. I'm Liora." She held out her hand palm up, the southern way, and when Valeria did not take it let it fall without offense. "I knew them all by name. I still do."

"They told me the fort fell. They didn't tell me what came of the wounded."

"They were killed in their beds at the Baths the night the fort fell," said Liora, "and raised, and walked into the sea." Her voice was low and clear and did not bend. "They kept me up in the fort that night, to see to their own. In the morning the beds were empty."

Valeria stood very still. She had held the causeway for them an hour and a half, and been given a medal for it by a man who arrived when the bleeding had stopped. On the nights she could not sleep she had told herself: *at least the wounded got out.* She had held that *at least* since the ford, like a coal in a closed fist.

She looked down at the eleven in the ash.

"And you'd have those spared. The men who did it."

"I asked for it," said Liora. "In the courtyard, before he chose." Her hands were folded in front of her apron, quite still, and the knuckles had gone white. "It was my wish, Captain, not his alone. Lay it at my door, if you need a door to lay it at."

Valeria had no answer.

"You were sick in the Grey," said the healer, more gently. "There's broth in the shed. You'll eat it."

---

She found Roderic at dusk in the fort's courtyard, rubbing the grey mare down himself with a twist of straw, badly, leaning on the mare's shoulder every few strokes, and talking to her in the nonsense men only talk to horses and small children. He straightened too fast when he saw her, and she watched it go up his back into his face, and watched him put it away.

"You've got older since the ford," said Valeria, from a spear's length off the mare. She never went closer.

"It was a long ford." He twisted the straw tighter in his fist. "Last I saw you, you were taking the carts back over the causeway. Varro pinned something on you after, I heard."

"I keep it in a drawer."

"Of course you do." He laughed into the mare's neck. "You still look at a horse the way a cook looks at a rat in the flour."

"I look at a horse the way a horse deserves."

"You know the council blames me for Hollowmere," he said, after a moment. "For not charging."

"I know."

She had thought it every day since the ford, every time some lord said *Roderic* and *Hollowmere* in one breath. *They blame you because you didn't charge. It's the only time I ever saw a cavalryman think.* It was in her mouth. It would have been the kindest thing she had said to anyone in a month.

She did not say it. Not on the first day. Not in front of the horse.

He looked at her over the mare's back, and for a moment the grin went out of his face and she saw behind it something she did not know what to do with: a tired man, glad of an old face, and not hiding it.

"It's good to see you, Valeria."

"You're missing the hock," said Valeria, and went up the stair to the wall to look at the harbor while there was light.

She looked at the gap and the moles a long time, and then down. By the well below her the woman in violet and the engineer sat on the curb with one wax tablet between them, bent over it like two masons over one plan; and as she watched, one began a figure and the other finished it without looking up. The prince came across the yard toward the tower. He stopped on the yard stair and watched them a breath too long. Then he went on.

Valeria put that on the list too. Not under *things that might kill her*. Under the other heading.

---

**ALANA**

Atticus brought her the wool on the morning of the ninth day.

She had the nineteen shards out on the warm rim of the pool (the twentieth was in the royal pilot's binnacle aboard the *Patience*, pointing faithfully at this cave), and they would tell her only one thing. Every one pointed at the Heart-Stone. When she moved one it turned to keep its face toward the great gold crystal, as a sick child keeps its face toward its mother.

Atticus came down the stair slowly and stood at the edge of the gold light with his fist closed, and waited until she looked up, because he was a shepherd and shepherds wait.

"Chair," he said. "There's a door."

He opened his fist. On his palm lay a strand of wool a finger long, fine-spun, the green of moss on the north side of a stone.

"Caught on the iron of it," he said in his slow hill-voice. "Under the water-stair, at the bottom of the tide. Two nights back we near had someone on those rocks. Went down a drain like a stoat. I took it for a fox." He closed his hand over the wool again as gently as over a lamb's ear. "Foxes don't knit."

"It isn't ours."

"We've no green. Nor the town."

"A Tharsian, then. A mole-crew man who never made the fort, living on limpets and going back to a door he knew. Barred?"

"Within."

"Within," said Alana, and looked at the seaward wall of the cave behind the crates. "Then there's a door into this hill that the Tharsians know and we don't. Kaeso! The engineer. And Master Lento, with his picks."

---

Gaius paced it inside, and outside down the water-gate stair in the wind, and came back with his bandaged hands blue and paced it again because he did not believe it.

"Fifteen feet," he said. "Forty-one from the water-stair to the back of this cave by my plan, and my plan is right. Twenty-six from here to that wall. That leaves fifteen feet of hill I can't account for." He pushed his lenses up. "Nobody builds a wall fifteen feet thick on the inside of a hill. The hill's already doing that job."

Alana was looking at the lamps. There were a dozen in their niches, and in the thick still air every flame stood straight up as if its wick were nailed. All but one. The lamp nearest the crates leaned. A hair's breadth. It leaned toward the wall, as a man leans to a keyhole.

"There," she said.

Fingers moved the crates and put his ugly face close to the niche, blowing softly on the soot, and then put two fingers into the back of it under the lamp.

"Tharsian lock," he said with professional contempt. "Three wards where one would do. They build them the way they keep their books."

It took him less time than Gaius took over a sum. A slab of dressed stone as high as a man turned inward on greased pins without a sound.

And the smell came out.

It was sweet. That was the worst of it. It came out of the dark like the breath of a sickroom where somebody has been dying a long time and somebody else has been covering it with honey; and under the sweetness, a tannery smell, of things left too long in water. Fingers stepped back and spat. Gaius stood with his eyes half shut and breathed it, measuring it, because that was what he did with everything.

Alana took the leaning lamp out of its niche and went in.

---

A long low room cut square out of the black rock, with steps at the far end going down to an iron door barred with an iron bar as thick as a man's arm. She could hear the sea sucking at the other side of it.

In the middle stood the vat.

Copper. A dyer's vat, wide as a threshing floor and deeper than a man is tall, gone green at the lip. Inside, a black crust thick as bark, and at the bottom a black sludge with a skin on it that the lamplight did not go into. A lead pipe came through the wall from the side where the pool was, and ended over the lip in a closed bronze cock. Against the wall leaned a long pole with an iron hook on the end, like the poles tanners use to turn hides in the pits.

She did not look at the hook for long.

Runes were cut into the floor in a ring round the vat, and more went up the walls in columns. She knew the script. It was the old high script of Corvus, the script of the oil sign and the fire rune.

But it was turned. She never found a better word. Every stroke went the wrong way, inward and downward, like a glove pulled off inside out. It was like reading her own hand in a mirror, by a bad light, with someone standing behind her. She made herself go round the ring with the lamp, lips moving; and where it closed, the strokes came together into words, and she said them aloud before she could stop herself.

"*Sink. Hold. Drink what lives.*"

"What is that?" said Gaius from the door.

"A sentence." Her voice was steady. She noted that. "You heard the quaestor give one to the dead. *Walk. Hold. Kill what lives.* This is the same sentence." She lowered the lamp. "Somebody has taught it to water."

---

The flasks stood at the top of the sea-door steps in two straw-packed crates of six. Iron, forearm-long and squat, each stoppered with lead and a rune pressed into the lead like a seal into wax. On each shoulder, stamped into the iron, the white closed hand of Tharsis, and beside it a little sprig of three leaves on a stem. On each crate, in square white clerk's letters:

*SILVA.*

She counted them twice, though she could see there were twelve, because counting was what her hands did when the rest of her had stopped.

The day-book lay on a shelf cut in the rock by the steps. Not the ledger; a working book, small and fat and greasy, its boards warped with damp. She knew the narrow patient hand at once. She had read four hundred pages of it.

Most of it was figures. *Third day steeping. Colour of strong tea. Not yet.* And further on: *Ninth day. Black. Drawn off, sealed, twelve.* And under that, as a man notes that he has swept a floor: *Vat scoured. Lees by the sea-door.*

At the front, she found the stock.

*Hospital of the Vigil, by the Baths. Eighty.*

*To the Bank, nineteen.*

*To the vat, sixty-one.*

She had seen it. On the first night, with the quaestor's blood still drifting in the pool: *Hospital of the Vigil, by the Baths: 61.* She had noted the healer's eighty, and set the difference aside for later, as she set everything aside, and thought it a clerk's error.

Nineteen. To the Bank.

She had burned the Kettle Bank. She had set the sea on fire and watched the dead under the water light up like a drowned town, and go out. Nineteen of the eighty had stood on that bank. And Liora had stood in the door of the steam-box house and said, *I'm glad it was fire.*

She turned the pages with the edge of her bandaged hand, so as not to touch them with her skin.

*The Silva crew, five, camped at the western glade. Resupplied by the* Tally.

And on the last written page, under a line ruled very straight:

*Tally at the dark of the moon, for the forty and the twelve.*

"Silva," said Gaius.

She had not heard him come in. He stood over the crates with a lamp in one ruined hand, and his face had gone the color of the Grey.

"It's a lading mark. Where a thing goes, not what it is. I saw it once. Chalked on the empty iron they swung off the *Tally* the day I hung her rudder. I took it for a man's name." He looked up. "Silva. Silverwood."

The elves' island: a green blot at the western edge of the old charts at Corvus, and beside it, in some royal cartographer's hand, only *boundary held*.

"Roots," Alana said. "It's written to go down, the way my fire went down on the bank. Pour it at the foot of a living thing and it sinks, and holds, and drinks. Five men and twelve flasks at every dark of the moon." Her own voice came from very far off, the voice she used for first-years. "And they made it out of the Fourteenth's wounded."

"The *Tally*." Gaius said the name as if it tasted of something. "A lean two-master with horse-stalls in her waist for the Survey's riders. She called once after the fort fell, to land their gear." Something moved behind the pale eyes and was put away. "The forty will be the quaestor's shards."

"He cut twenty."

"Then somebody will be disappointed at the dark of the moon."

"Four nights, counting this one." She did not have to work it out. She had been writing the tides since the beach, and Nerva had been grumbling for two days about the springs coming on.

They looked at each other, and neither said it: that a ship was coming to Cyrene that did not know Cyrene had changed hands.

---

The letter slid out from under the back board when she closed the book, and she knew the hand before she opened it, as you know a voice in the next room. She had seen it once before, on a slip of paper left on her desk at Corvus when she was sixteen, the morning after the cellar wall. *Very good. Now do it again without the rod.* Unsigned. It had not needed signing. There was only one chair at Corvus who wrote like that, and her name was Livia.

*S.*

*You will want a larger vat. Don't. The black wants crowding, as wine does; it learns from its neighbors.*

*Corvus is eleven days from the wood and the iron spoils on the way. That is why you have the vat, and I have only the letters.*

*Draw from the stone's own water and never steep in the pool. I will not have the stone fouled. It is the only honest thing on that island, and we shall want it honest later.*

*Twelve to the wood at every dark until I say. Old trees die slowly, and with very good manners. Let them.*

*If the little chair should come to Cyrene (she will; she always comes to see what has been done, and she always stays to read about it), be kind to her. Show her the vat. She will understand it faster than you did, and it will hurt her more, and both of those things will be good for her.*

*L.*

Alana folded it along its folds, exactly, and put it back under the board, and pressed both palms on the cover.

*She always stays to read about it.*

She was standing in a room under a hill, with the lamp burning down, reading about it.

Above her, through the rock, in the tower room she had taken because it had a door that shut, there was a trunk, and in it a book out of a wing of the Corvus library that only one chair had ever held the key to. She thought of it now, and it seemed to her that she could feel it through the rock, as you feel a stove through a wall: there, and warm, and patient, and heavier than it had been that morning.

"Alana," said Gaius. He had called her *lady* for ten days. "Don't open the flasks."

"I wasn't going to."

"I know." He looked at the vat and the hook, and away. "I wanted someone to have said it out loud."

---

She and Gaius had given it to the prince at noon, on the rim of the pool, figure by figure. But she told Liora herself. That word was not going to be carried to the steam-box house by a boy with a drum, or by a man. She went down through the town in the dusk toward the slips, and did not let herself think on the way.

The healer was at the far end of the shed, by the lamp, rebinding the jaw of Orso Netmender's mother, an old woman with a face like a walnut. She looked up as Alana came in through the steam, and looked at her face for the space of one breath.

"Let me finish this," Liora said.

She finished it without hurry, the linen wound and tucked, the old woman's hair smoothed back off the bandage with two fingers. Then she washed her hands at the basin, slowly, the backs and the palms and between each finger, as a surgeon washes before he cuts. Then she came and stood in front of Alana.

"Tell me."

Alana told her. She did not soften it; every soft way of saying it was some kind of lie, and Liora would know the lie and have to carry it as well as the thing. So she said it as she would have written it. The door, the vat, the book. *To the Bank, nineteen. To the vat, sixty-one.* The black drawn off and sealed in iron. The word on the crates. She did not speak of the hook, or the lees by the sea-door. There was a limit after all, and it was there.

"They walked them into the sea," she said at the end. "The water-gate stair goes down into the water right beside that door. They walked them into the sea, Liora. And then they walked them out of it."

Liora did not move.

For a long moment her face did not change at all, as if she were waiting for the rest. Then the color went. It went from the lips first, so that they were the same color as the rest of her, and then from the rest, so that the faded bruise on her cheekbone stood out yellow again as it had on the first day.

Her hands came together in front of her apron. The right took hold of the left wrist, where the marks had been, and held it hard. Then, deliberately, as one unclenches a hand that has held a rope too long, she let go.

"Show me," she said.

---

Liora went into the still-room alone. Alana stood in the doorway with the lamp.

The healer walked once round the vat, outside the ring of runes, not stepping on them. She did not look at the hook. When she came back to where she had started she put out her hand and laid it flat on the copper rim, as she would lay it on the forehead of a child with a fever, to see how bad it was.

Then she began to say the names.

Low and steady, in the vowels of the capital's old houses, as the priests of the Sun say the litany on Midwinter night: each name, and after it what she had known of the man.

"Manius Varenus, of the Fourteenth, burned across the back. He sang. Tertius, son of Marcia, eighteen, who asked for his mother every night and never once by day. Old Cotta, the standard-bearer's man, both legs. Publius Sura, who would not let me cut his hair. Hanno the Vessan, who taught me to say *thank you* in the hill speech, and laughed at how I said it—"

Alana began to count them. Her mind put a mark beside each name as it came, *one, two, three, four, five*, and she heard herself doing it, and her face burned in the cold.

She stopped.

It was the first time in her life she had ever stopped counting. She let the names go past her into the dark, and did not keep them. They were not hers to keep. After a while she understood that Liora was saying all eighty. She did not know which nineteen had gone to the Bank, so she was saying every one of them here, so that none of the sixty-one would be left out.

When it was done the healer took her hand from the copper and turned round. Her face was white as paper, and calm, and dry.

"Two things, Alana. I ask them of you because you read the book, and because you will be the one who carries those." She looked past Alana at the crates by the sea-door. "They must never touch living ground. Not a drop. Not on a weed between two stones. Wherever they go, they must not go into anything that grows."

"Yes."

"And wherever they go, I go."

Alana opened her mouth to say that it was not hers to promise; that there was a prince, and a writ, and a war, and nobody had ever asked her where anything went.

"Yes," she said.

Liora nodded once, as if that settled it. Then her knees went. She sat down slowly on the flagstones with her back against the copper and her hands open in her lap, and looked at nothing. She did not weep.

Alana set the lamp on the floor and sat down too, on the other side of the doorway, not touching her. They sat there until the lamp burned down.

She did not write any of it in the weather-book. There were things the weather-book was not for.

---

**MARCUS**

He had carried his father's letter for a day and a half without opening it, against his ribs beside the ninety-one and the seven, and it weighed more than both.

He took it out on the seaward wall, in the dark, because it was the one place in Cyrene where nobody wanted anything of him. At the ends of the two moles the towers stood against the faint yellow glow of the Grey, and the gap between them lay open on the black water like a door left on the latch.

He had heard what was in the hill at noon, from Alana and the engineer, side by side on the rim of the pool with the day-book open between them, figure by figure.

Liora had gone down past him at dusk without a word. Hours later she came up again with her face like paper, and went past him at the head of the cave stair, where he had waited, without seeing him; and Alana after her with a dead lamp, who looked at him and shook her head. And he had come up here.

He broke the private seal with his thumb.

The King's Shard lay inside, wrapped in a single sheet: a honey-colored sliver the length of his finger, warm from his ribs. In his palm it stirred, as it had on the *Clemency*'s deck, and turned, and lay still, pointing away over the black water toward the Old Hill and a man sitting alone at a table with one candle.

The letter was in his father's own hand. He had not seen it since he was a boy; the king wrote everything through clerks. This was cramped and slow and old, the letters leaning on one another like men at the end of a long march.

*Marcus.*

*The fisherman brought my stone home on its own lane, and one of theirs beside it, which was well thought of. I send mine back. It points to this house. You are the one who is away from it.*

*The council will say what it says. You have heard it read.*

*Your brother would have*

And then a line struck through, again and again, so many times that the pen had gone through the paper and the ink made a black bar a finger wide. He held it up to the glow of the Grey and tilted it and tried to find the shape of one letter under the black. He could not.

*Keep warm. Eat. The pilot tells me the water of Cyrene is foul, and does a man good.*

*Your father.*

That was all.

*Your brother would have.* Would have what? Taken the harbor by the moles and not the beach? Done it sooner, done it better, not needed to be sent? Or the other thing. The thing a father writes once, by one candle, and looks at, and cannot let stand, because if he lets it stand he will have meant it.

He would never know. The old man had come to the edge of a sentence and looked over and drawn back, and blacked it out so that his son would know there had been one, and never what it was. And then he had written *keep warm*.

Marcus could not see the paper very well. His hands were shaking so that it rattled. He laid it on the parapet under his palm and stood with his head down, breathing, like a man at the top of a long climb. There was a cup by his other hand, brought up from the well. He drank, to have done something, and the spring's water went down foul and cold, tasting of eggs and iron.

He heard her on the stair. Nobody else in Cyrene walked like that, a long even step that set each foot down as if testing the stones. She came along the wall-walk in her mail and fur and stopped two paces off.

Her eyes went to the cup.

He saw her look at what was in it, and her nostrils move very slightly at the smell. Then her eyes went away to the bay, as if there had been nothing there to see.

She said nothing. He knew she had seen. It lay between them on the parapet like the letter under his hand.

---

She leaned a little way off, forearms on the stone, as Roderic leaned, and did not ask about the paper or the shard in his fist. She would never ask him anything she did not need to know, he thought. It was like standing next to a wall.

"He sent me back the stone," Marcus said, after a while. He did not know why.

"I carried it four days. I didn't know."

Below, the surf breathed against the foot of the water-gate stair.

"Titus sent me back with the wounded," she said.

She said it to the bay, flat, a fact set down beside the others.

Marcus turned his head.

"At Hollowmere. He held the ford, and I held the causeway behind him so the carts could get over. When they were over, he sent word for me to go with them. Back to the boats."

"Did you go?"

"It was an order."

The silence came down after it and stood on the wall between them, and Marcus knew what was in it, because it was in him too. A stair in the dark at the bottom of the world, and ninety-one men on it, and one who was not. He had not gone. He had been carried. He had woken on a ship with the stair behind him, and he did not know to this day whose order that had been. He had never asked. He knew whom he would have to ask.

He did not say any of it. She had gone, and he had been taken, and the men who stayed stood between them on the wall.

"I'm sorry," he said at last.

"Don't be. Everyone's sorry. Varro was sorry, pinning a medal on me. They all want me to stand there with my chin up, so they can admire how well I do it. I've been admired for going back with the carts by every man in the capital who never saw the ford. I'd sooner be cursed for something I chose."

---

He told her about the hill, plainly, as Alana had told him: the door, the vat, the sentence on the floor, the twelve flasks with the leaf on them, the five men in a glade on Silverwood. And then the numbers. He watched her while he said them, because he owed her that. They were hers.

Her hands on the parapet did not move. But he saw the scar on her face go white from the eye to the jaw, and her jaw set, as a man sets it in the tooth-drawer's chair.

"The *Tally*," she said at last, perfectly steady. "Tell me about the ship."

He told her: a Survey tender, due at the dark of the moon for forty shards, twenty of them never cut and the rest already in Aeridor's hands, and for twelve flasks that were waiting under the hill.

"She'll tie up at the water-gate as the Survey's ships always do, and load at the sea-door," he said. "Then she'll sail for Silverwood with sixty-one of your Fourteenth in her hold, in iron, and pour them into the roots of a wood that never did Aeridor any harm but stand in its own place." He stopped. "And you'll tell me a ship bound for Silverwood is the council's business, and I'm to write to them by the *Patience*, and wait."

Valeria straightened off the parapet and looked at him full in the face. Her eyes were dry. There was something in them he had seen once before, in Roderic's face on the dune, the moment before the mare went up the sand.

"My orders say hold Cyrene," she said. "They say nothing about Silverwood. They say hold Cyrene." She struck the parapet once with the heel of her hand, as if to show him where it was. "This is Cyrene. That is Cyrene's harbor. A ship that sails into Cyrene's harbor is Cyrene's business, Highness, and if I let her sail out again with my wounded in her hold, I haven't held Cyrene. I've held a wall and watched." Her voice did not rise. It went harder and quieter. "I've left one thing I was told to hold. I'll not leave another."

He looked at her a long moment.

"Then we take her."

"We take her."

"Whole. Her stones are aboard, and her pilot, and her iron. I want every bit of her."

"Then you'll want a plan, not a speech. You'll have one by morning." She was already measuring the gap between the towers as Gaius measured a beam: the width, and the stair, and where sixty bills would stand. "Sixty of mine and two hundred of yours, one writ that says *hold*, and a Tharsian ship in the dark. If this goes wrong, Highness, nobody at the capital will even spell our names right."

He thought of the ninety-one against his ribs, and how many of those names he had guessed the spelling of by candlelight in a tavern on the Lower Canal.

"I'd rather be misspelled," said Marcus.

For a moment he thought she was going to smile. She did not; but something went across the scarred face that was the next thing to it, as a gleam goes along a blade.

"Then stay behind the shields," said Valeria, "long enough to be misspelled later."

She went off along the wall-walk with that long even step, to find Roderic and wake him, because she would need to know, she said over her shoulder, which end of a Tharsian horse came off a ship first.

Marcus folded his father's letter with the black bar inside and put it in the oilskin with the ninety-one and the seven. The King's Shard pointed home. That was all a shard could do. He put it away and turned his back on it, and looked down at the gap between the towers, where at the dark of the moon a ship would come in out of the Grey not knowing who was waiting for her.

His hands had stopped shaking. He knew what that meant, and did not pretend otherwise.

---

# Chapter Eleven: The Tally

**RODERIC**

The dark of the moon was three nights off, and Valeria wanted a horse.

"Not to kill," she said. "To look at. The ones that come off that ship will come off frightened, in the dark, onto wet stone, and I want my front rank to have looked one in the eye before then."

"Then you want an enemy," said Roderic. "A horse is no use without a fool on top to point it at you." He slapped the grey mare's neck, and she swung her long head round and gave him the patient contempt of her kind. "I'm the best fool on the island. She's the only horse."

"If you break your back," said Valeria, "I'll not carry you."

"Nobody's asking you to."

"Somebody always asks," said Valeria, and went to tell the Ford.

---

They did it on the black beach in the morning. There was snow on the marram along the dunes, and the surf came in warm and went out steaming, so that the Ford stood in two ranks with a white fog round their boots. Sixty of them: the front rank's shields grounded in the sand, and behind them the bills going up like bean-poles in a cottage garden.

The points of the bills had been stuck into turnips. Oppius had given up forty from the fort's stores, under protest, and stood on the dunes with his one eye on them, as a man watches his daughters dance with soldiers. The hooks were wrapped in old sailcloth. Along the crest of the dunes sat the cohort, two hundred thieves, and somewhere among them Spurius Lento was making a book. *Three to one on the General. Ten to one on the turnips.*

Roderic walked the mare close to listen while Valeria went down her front rank. She was not shouting. He had known drill-captains in all his years of armies, and they were all shouters, men who had found out early that a loud voice saves thinking. She stopped at each man and said something short, and the men did not stiffen. They settled, the way a ploughed field settles after rain.

"A horse is not a fool," she was telling a boy with a new beard and old eyes. "It won't run onto a hedge. It's the man on top who wants to, and the horse that decides. Your work is to help it decide." She pushed down on the rim of his shield, and he leaned his weight into it. "Like that. Don't look at the man. Look at the horse's eye and let it see the points, and stand still. A horse knows a thing that stands still. It's the thing that runs that gets ridden down." She moved on. "If it refuses, it turns along you, and the man's side is open, and the hooks are for him. Not the horse. It's the only one on that side of the line with any sense."

He had explained the charge himself a hundred times, in the wine-halls of the capital, with the salt-cellars for squadrons. He had never heard anyone explain the other end of it, as a trade: a thing a woman could teach a boy in the morning, quietly, with her hand on his shield.

She turned and saw him.

"You're in my line of sight," said Valeria.

"I'm the enemy. I'm meant to be in it."

"Then be in it from farther off."

---

He went to the far end of the beach and turned the mare, and drew his grandfather's sword, because it seemed proper, and laid it back along his shoulder.

On the dunes Fingers stood up. "*Five coppers on the Long Field!*" And the cohort took it up, laughing. "*The Long Field! The Long Field!*"

Valeria turned her head. She said something to her line, not loudly, and the wind brought it down the sand.

"Then let the Long Field come."

It was nothing. But he had been all his life on horses, and he knew the sound a horse makes at a ditch it has fallen in before: the short stride, the check, the gathering. There had been that in her voice. A short stride, before the name.

He filed it, in the place where an old soldier keeps what he has noticed and does not yet understand, and put his heels to the grey.

He had learned on the dunes that a silent charge was better. He roared anyway, the old cry, *The Long Field and the king!*, like a bull in a burning barn, because there was a hedge and two hundred thieves betting on him and a woman who thought his whole trade a disease, and there were not so many mornings left. The grey flattened her ears and stretched out, and the snow came off the marram in the wind of their going.

The line did not move. Sixty faces under iron caps, and not one of them doing anything at all. They stood the way a wall stands. A stride and a half out he felt the mare ask him the question every horse he had ever ridden had asked at that place: *Are you sure?*

He answered with his heels.

She refused anyway. She did it beautifully. She put her forefeet down a stride from the shields and swung away along the front of the hedge in a long sliding turn, as a ship goes about; and he went on for a moment in the direction he had been going, as riders do; and two sailcloth hooks came over the shields and took him across the shoulders, one high and one low, with the patient strength of men who have laid a great many hedges, and drew him out of the saddle like a cork out of a jar, as he had drawn a Tharsian off a horse on this same sand eleven days since; and he had time to think that it looked better from the other end.

He came down on his back on the black sand.

It was warm. The island breathed up through it, warm as a bed, and above him the sky was white and a single flake of snow was coming down out of it, turning over, toward his face.

He began to laugh. It came up from under the old wound and hurt, and he lay there with his beard full of grit and laughed till the tears ran into his ears. On the dunes the cohort had gone up like a kicked hive, and somewhere in the Ford's second rank Old Baebius, who had not spoken a civil word to anyone in two days, was making a noise like a goose being strangled.

A shadow came over him. Valeria stood looking down, and the white seam on her face pulled at the corner of her mouth as if something under it wanted to move.

"You're dead," she said.

"Gloriously?"

"Wetly. You're lying in the tide."

The surf was going out round his shoulders, warm. He did not care.

A second shadow arrived. Felix, picking his way down the sand with a towel folded over his arm.

"I took the liberty, General," said Felix. "I had a presentiment."

---

He did it twice more, because he was a fool. The second time he tried to be clever and swung round the end of their line, as he had taken the Tharsians in the flank on the Long Field nineteen years ago; and Valeria said, "*Rear. Gate. Now,*" not loudly, and the rear rank turned on its heel like a gate, and there was the hedge again where there had been only backs. Old Baebius hooked him off. He did it with the purest happiness Roderic had ever seen on a human face. The third time the mare looked at the hedge from forty paces, and at the warm patch of sand where her rider had lain twice already, and put her head down and began to graze.

The cohort wept.

---

Afterward he sat with his back against the *Clemency*'s black ribs, with Felix's towel round his neck, and Valeria came and stood a little way off with her weight on her bill.

"The rear rank," he said, wiping his beard. "I've never seen a line turn like that."

"You've never looked."

He thought about it. "No," he said. "I suppose I haven't."

The surf came in and went out.

"A charge can break an army," Roderic said. He did not know why he said it. It was the oldest thing he knew; his grandfather had said it to him at six, in the long gallery where the tourney ribbons hung.

Valeria looked down at him.

"A shield wall," she said, "can make sure someone survives to remember it."

He opened his mouth and found he had nothing to answer with. Nineteen years of answers, all of them about glory, and none would do. She saw it, and something went across her face, quick, like light off a wet stone.

"What do you call it?" he said, to say something, and nodded at the bill in her fist. "Your men have names for theirs."

She turned the haft so the hook came round, and showed him the inner curve of it, honed to a lip like a sickle. "The Farrier."

"Why the Farrier?"

"It unshoes them," said Valeria.

He saw where it would sit, low behind a horse's leg above the hoof, where the great cord runs under the skin like a bowstring; and he felt it in his own heels, and could not keep it off his face. She watched him understand, and did not look away.

"You're a terrible woman," he said.

"I'm a good one with a bill."

"Crowns and crows. Both, then." He shifted against the ribs, and his back put a hot nail in under his shoulder-blade. "What do they cut with those, in your hills? When there's no Tharsians."

"Thorn. Briar. Thistle, in the high pasture. It spreads if you leave it."

He whistled through his teeth. Behind the wreck the grey lifted her head out of the marram with her mouth full.

"Thistle," Roderic told her. "There. You've a name. And it's a thing the captain cuts for a living, so every time she lifts that thing, she'll think of you."

"That's a horse," said Valeria, "not a weed."

"She's both. She's grey, she pricks, and she grows where she's not wanted."

"You've named her to vex me."

"Is it working?"

"No," said Valeria.

It was. He could see it was. It went into him like warm wine.

Then she held out her hand. He took it, and she hauled him up in one pull, weight going back, as a woman hauls a full bucket from a well. Her palm was hard and callused where a haft rubs, and very warm. His knee went as he came up, and for a moment he stood closer to her than he had meant to, and she did not step back.

"Your back's gone," she said.

"It went at the Long Field."

"I know when it went."

She let go of his hand and walked away up the beach without once looking at the horse.

---

He stood with the towel round his neck and watched her go.

*I know when it went.* He turned it over, and did not ask. He had buried two wives, and a man who has buried two knows the beginning of a thing when he sees it, as a farmer knows the first soft day at the end of winter, and knows what comes after. Mud, mostly. Then work. Then, if the weather holds, something green. He had been wary of soft days since the second grave. Standing there in the wet, he found he was done with it. Wariness was for young men, who had springs to spare. A man of fifty-two, with nine wounds and a knee that told the weather, did not get so many soft days that he could stand at the gate squinting at them. If one was offered, he would take it, mud and all.

And nobody had argued with him in nineteen years without wanting a song out of it. She did not want a song. She did not even want to be right. She wanted sixty men alive in three nights' time, and had put him on his back twice to get it, and had not looked pleased.

He found he was grinning into the towel like a boy.

"General," said Felix, "you are standing in the sea."

He was. He did not mind that either.

---

**MARCUS**

"No," said Captain Merula, without looking up from his shovel.

The tide was out, and the eleven were clearing the last of the ash from the foot of the third slip where the stone went under the weed. Iason sat on his bollard ten paces off with both hands on the hilt of the wiped sword.

"You don't know what I was going to ask."

"I know what you've been asking since the first day, Highness." Merula leaned on the shovel and looked at him through the lenses on their cord. "There's a ship coming, and you've found out when, and you want to know how she's let in. I said in your courtyard I'd not tell you. I keep my word too, Highness. I thought you set some store by that."

"I do."

"Then you'll not ask me to break mine."

"You told me you didn't know when."

"I didn't. Then." He bent to the ash again.

Marcus had expected it. It did not help.

"Three lamps," said someone.

It was the boy. Nonius stood at the next barrow with his shovel held across him in his chained hands, white to the lips, but looking at Marcus.

"Three lamps in the bell-arch, lit at full dark, so the pilot can come in on them through the Grey. Two strokes of the bell at dusk, to say the harbor's ours. Two again when she's seen in the gap. And a man on the bottom step of the water-gate stair with a lamp." He swallowed. "She calls from the water, *Tally, for the count.* The stair answers. *Count's kept. Come in to the door.*"

Merula had stopped shovelling. He straightened slowly and looked at the boy with the look of a clerk who has checked a column twice and found it right, and still does not like the sum.

"Why?" Marcus said.

Nonius looked up the slip at Iason.

"You didn't hang me. And he didn't." He drew a breath. "And he sat by the door. Every night. He could have stood it and slept on his feet like the old one did. He sat, where I could hear him." He stopped. "I don't know why that's a reason. It is one."

Iason's face did not move. His hands closed on the sword.

"How do you know the words?"

"I stood the stair the night she came, after the fort fell, with the Survey's gear. The sergeant gave me the lamp because I was youngest." The chained hands tightened. "Highness, they'll want a Tharsian voice. If your man calls it, even with the words right, they'll hear the isles in it, the way I hear them in yours."

Marcus saw where it was going a breath before it went there.

"I'll stand it," said Nonius. "I'll hold the lamp."

"No," said Marcus, and heard it come out with no weight behind it, because the boy was right, and they both knew it, and so did Merula.

Iason had got up off the bollard, and was looking at the boy as he had on the fifth night, as if trying to work out whom he belonged to.

"I'll be under the stair," said Iason. "In the dark of the water-gate. Three paces off him."

Marcus's hands were shaking. He put them behind his back.

"Very well." It was the worst thing he had said since the landing, and he knew it as he said it. "You'll hold the lamp, Nonius. And when the words are said and her lines are on the stair, you'll put it down and walk up into the water-gate, and not run, and Iason will take you through. You walk. You don't stay to watch."

"Yes, Highness."

Merula took off his lenses, wiped them on his cloak, and put them on again, and went back to his shovel.

---

They made the plan in the cave, where nobody could overhear them but the Heart-Stone, breathing its slow gold over their faces. Valeria had drawn the harbor with a knife-point in the soot on a flagstone, and Gaius had looked at it and said nothing, which from Gaius was a medal.

"She'll come in on the lamps under sweeps and lay alongside the stair," said Nerva, squatting by the drawing with his white braid over his shoulder, dripping. "And when her master finds he's been sold, he'll cut his lines and go out the way he came, with sail on her before your men are down the steps."

"Then we make sure he can't," said Marcus.

He laid it out. Nonius at the lamp, Iason in the dark of the water-gate. Rufo and forty shields inside the gate. The Ford along the rock at the stair head, to come down when her lines were fast and her marines started up the steps. Atticus's slingers flat in the weed on the headland either side.

The *Patience* Nerva would lay along the fourth slip with her mast down, where the culvert steam would cover her better than a tarpaulin. "I did it once," he said, "for a man who owed money in Vessa."

"And if the *Tally* runs?" said Valeria.

"Then she goes out through the gap," said Marcus, "under the west tower. Gaius has asked for the west tower."

"With the Chair," said Gaius, not looking up from his tablet, "and the bolt-thrower, and the crew on it. The man in the shawl's the best layer I've seen."

"Why the west?" said Roderic.

"Because I hung her rudder." Gaius made a mark in the wax, and said nothing else.

"And the horses," said Roderic. He sat with his back against a pillar and his legs out in front of him, lowered there like a cask, with sand still in his beard. "She'll have four or six in stalls under the main hatch. If there's fighting on her deck, they'll come up. A groom brings a horse off a ship rump-first, slow, with a cloth on its eyes. A frightened horse comes off teeth-first, and goes through whatever's in front of it."

"Then it'll be my front rank in front of it," said Valeria, "and they've looked one in the eye now. Three times."

"Four, if you count the grazing."

"I don't."

Rufo looked at the two of them, and then at the Heart-Stone, with the face of a man very carefully saying nothing in a temple.

"One more thing," said Marcus. He made himself look at Alana. "No fire on the ship."

He watched it go into her. Her hands did not move in her lap; but her face did what it had done on the *Clemency*'s afterdeck when she was told to wait. It closed, like a book.

"We need her whole," he said, to all of them, but to her. "Her pilot's aboard, and his stones, and whatever will find Silverwood through the Grey. Her iron's aboard, the empties for those flasks." He nodded at the slab-door in the wall behind the crates, shut on the vat and the twelve. "If she burns, it all goes to the bottom of the bay. The *Patience* is the king's, and she goes home. We've no ship of our own that will sail. Swords. Stones. Bills. Not fire."

"And if she gets out through the gap," said Alana, perfectly level, "with all of it aboard, and sails for the wood?"

"Then I'll have been wrong, and you can write it down."

She looked at him a moment longer, and nodded once.

"Then I'll have to be precise," said Alana of Corvus.

---

The chair came up the fort road that evening on the shoulders of four shipwrights, with the cohort behind it and Kaeso's drum going at a slow limping three that Marcus had last heard on the black beach, beaten on a cooking pot, while a woman danced on a ship's hatch-cover. He felt the blood go into his face.

It was a real chair. That was the first terrible thing. Black oak, seasoned, high-backed, made by men who had built ships all their lives and never been asked to build anything so useless, and had put everything they knew into it out of pure astonishment. Every joint was pegged. The arms ended in dolphins. And up the high back, carved deep, a spiral of little sparks went turning from the seat to the top rail; and at the top of the spiral was a small carved woman with her arms raised and her skirts flying out round her in a wheel.

"That," said Gaius, behind him, "was keel stock."

They set it down by the well. Alana, on the well-curb with the weather-book, looked up and saw it, and saw the top rail, and went slowly and completely scarlet from her collar to her hair.

"Centurion," said Rufo, at Marcus's elbow. "It wants presenting proper. By an officer. The lads asked particular."

He had stood in this courtyard while the whole town watched him decide whether eleven lived, and known what to say. He walked to the well now and every word he had ever known went out of him like water from a cracked jar.

"Lady—" he said.

"*Not a lady,*" said two hundred voices, helpfully.

"Alana. Of Corvus. On behalf of the shipwrights of Cyrene, who made it. And the cohort. Who are mostly thieves. Who—" He had had a sentence. It had something in it about the Kettle Bank and the drowned of Cyrene going out like lamps, and it had been rather good, and it was gone. "Who wish the Chair," said Marcus, "to have a chair."

Silence.

"So that the Chair," said Marcus, who could not stop, "can sit. In it."

The cohort made a sound like a dam going.

Alana looked at him as at a sum come out wrong in front of the full chairs of Corvus. Then at the shipwrights with their caps in their hands, anxious, as men stand by a hull at its launching. She got up and walked to the chair with her chin up and sat down very straight, with her hands on the two dolphins.

"Thank you," she said to the shipwrights. "It is very well made. The joints are beautiful." She drew a breath. "The top rail is a libel."

The courtyard roared.

When the cohort had gone singing down the hill at last, she was still in the chair, looking at him.

"That was the worst speech I have ever heard you give," said Alana.

"I've given worse."

"Not sober."

"No," Marcus agreed. "Not sober."

"I'll be precise tomorrow night," she said. "I wanted you to know I heard you, in the cave. I didn't like it. I heard it."

"I know."

"Good." She stood. "Somebody will have to carry this up to my room, and I am not going to ask any of them." She went in to the tower with the book under her arm, and left him beside the well with a chair.

He carried it up himself. It was very heavy. It was keel stock.

---

**ALANA**

At dusk Tam rang the bell twice, and the bay of Cyrene went quiet, as a room goes quiet when someone says a name.

She was on the roof of the west tower at the end of the mole, with the gap on her left hand and the dark harbor on her right. Then, high on the headland, three lamps came alight in the bell-arch one after another and hung there like three nail-heads of gold driven into the dark.

Gaius sat against the bolt-thrower's frame with his tablet on his knees. Round the engine sat its crew: four convicts, and the man in the shawl, whose name was Pinarius, and who had been a weaver before he was a debtor. The shawl was his wife's. He did not say where his wife was.

No moon. No star. Only the three gold lamps and the black water breathing.

*No fire on the ship.* It was a good order. She would have given it herself. And she could feel the oil sign in her all the same, behind her tongue like a word nearly remembered. Somewhere in the Grey a ship was coming with the empties for the twelve, to carry the rest of Liora's eighty to a wood, and she wanted to burn it so badly that her burned hand ached in the rag, as a tooth aches.

*Then I'll have to be precise.* She held on to that like a rail.

---

The *Tally* came through the gap three hours after dark, like a knife out of a sleeve.

She made no sound. The Grey moved, and then the shape was under the tower, close enough to throw a stone onto her deck. Two bare masts. Sweeps dipping without a splash, as a heron walks. A hooded glow at her stern over the binnacle. And the smell of her on the warm air: tar, and bilge, and horses.

On the headland Tam rang twice more. *She's seen.*

She went on across the bay toward the one small lamp on the lowest step of the water-gate stair. Alana watched her sweeps come in together, like a beetle folding its legs, and heard faint across three hundred paces of still water a voice from the ship.

"*Tally, for the count.*"

And a second voice from the stair, young, with the round roll of Tharsis on it, and only a little shake.

"*Count's kept. Come in to the door.*"

A line went over and fell across the steps. Another. A boy on the bottom step bending to make them fast, the lamp at his feet lighting his legs and hands. She had seen him every day on the slips and never once spoken to him.

At the *Tally*'s stern rail, beside the binnacle's glow, a figure stood looking down at the stair.

Only a shape. Grey. It looked down at the boy a long time; longer than a man takes to see his lines are fast. Alana found she was holding her breath.

The figure lifted one hand.

The sound came across the water a moment after, flat and small, like a door shut in another part of a house. On the bottom step the boy sat down. Then he lay down. The lamp at his feet went over, and rolled off the step into the sea, and went out.

Then the night came apart.

---

Shouting; torches; the Ford coming down out of the water-gate. Stones rattled on the *Tally*'s deck like hail on a barn roof. Axes went into her lines, her sweeps dug, a grey sail went up her foremast and filled, and the long black ship came away from the stair and back across the bay toward the gap, faster than anything that size had a right to move.

"*Lay her!*" said Alana. "*Pinarius!*"

But the *Tally* had an engine on her foredeck, and it spoke first. The bolt struck the parapet a yard from Alana's head and the stone burst, and something hit her across the cheekbone like the flat of a hand, and she was sitting down, and Crispus, who had cut purses on the Silk Row and laughed at her accent all evening, was screaming over what was left of his leg, and nobody was at the windlass. On the *Tally*'s foredeck three shapes were spanning their engine again, quick and practiced.

*Precise.*

She got onto her knees and put the rod over the broken parapet. Not the great stroke. The great stroke would have come down like the sky falling and run along every wet rope in her and lit her sails. Alana drew the small one: the long jagged line, short; the two barbs, close. She laid it on the engine's iron frame as a cooper lays a hoop on a cask, because lightning loves iron above everything and goes to it as water goes downhill.

She spoke the name.

It was a thin crack, a whip. A white thread from the rod to the engine on the *Tally*'s foredeck, and the iron frame lit blue along every edge, and the three men at it threw up their arms and fell away as if pushed. The engine smoked. Nothing else did. No sail. No rope.

Her nose began to bleed. She let it.

Gaius was up. He had his ruined hands on the crank beside the weaver's, and he was shouting, and she had never heard him shout. It was a high cracked voice, like a man calling a dog off a cliff.

"*The rudder! The stock, at the waterline—the lower pintle's soft iron, I set it soft—one bolt—not yet—wait for her quarter—*"

The *Tally*'s bow went past below them, turning for the gap. Her long side went by, and then her stern came round toward the tower: the high carved quarter, and the dark blade of the rudder hanging off it into the water, its iron straps wet in her stern lantern.

"*Now,*" said Gaius.

Pinarius loosed. The bolt went into the *Tally*'s quarter at the waterline like an axe into green oak, and under it came a smaller sound, a dull crack, like a bell struck with a stone. The rudder jumped. Then it hung, swinging out from the stern on its one good pintle and back, idly, like a gate in the wind.

The *Tally* stopped turning.

She went straight on instead, foresail full, toward the inner face of the mole; and Alana watched her come with the terrible slowness of a dropped cup. She struck broadside a stone's throw from the tower's foot. A long groan came up through the masonry into Alana's knees, and the ship heeled against the mole until her rail lay level with the stone. Under her main hatch the horses began to scream.

"Every hull I touched," said Gaius, holding on to the windlass, very quietly. "I just never thought I'd see it."

Crispus had stopped screaming. She did not look to see why.

---

She went down the tower stair at a run with blood on her mouth.

The Ford came along the mole with torches, two abreast, their bills like a hedge on the move, and Rufo's shields behind, and among the shields a black cloak she knew. The *Tally*'s marines had run a long cleated plank from her rail onto the mole, the brow they used to walk horses ashore, and were coming down it to take the tower and get their ship off before the tide went.

They met at the foot of the brow.

"*Anchor on me!*" Valeria's voice went down the mole like a bell. "*ANCHOR ON ME!*"

The front rank went down on one knee with shields grounded, and the bills came down over their heads, and the marines ran onto them and stopped as if they had run onto a wall, because they had. The bills went in and out with a sound like threshing, and the hooks went round necks and behind knees and drew, and men went off the brow into the black gap between ship and mole and did not come up. Iason fought in a gap between two shields as he had killed the quaestor, without art, unhurried, as if the marines were planks to be dressed before dark. Nobody came past him.

A cohort man with a spear went down at the brow's foot under an axe from the heeled deck. She did not know his name. She would by morning.

Then she saw the marine on the mole's edge. He had gone in off the brow and swum and climbed the seaward face, and now he stood up behind Rufo's shields with an axe. Marcus had his back to him. The axe was going up.

There was time only for the small stroke and the distance: twenty paces, a hand's breadth left of the back of Marcus's head, and the axe-head, iron, in the torchlight.

She spoke the name.

The white thread went past Marcus's ear so close she saw the hair lift on the back of his head. It took the axe. The marine went backward off the mole without a sound, and there was a hiss in the dark water and a smell of burning.

Marcus turned round.

---

Then the horses came up.

The hatch-cover burst upward like a lid off a pot, and a big bay came up teeth-first, exactly as the General had said, eyes white all round, and found the brow, the only level thing it could see, and came down it into the torches.

The front rank had looked a horse in the eye three times. It did not help. Alana saw the line bend.

Valeria threw her shield down flat on the stone, as a woman throws down a cloak. She put her hand back without looking, and Old Baebius put the Farrier into it, and she stepped out alone in front of her line into the path of the horse.

It was quick. That was what Alana could not afterward forgive it for. It was done as a master's sum is done on a slate, without a stroke wasted. The hook went in low behind the near foreleg, and Valeria drew once with her whole weight going back, and the leg went from under it; and she was already round the falling chest, and the blade went in under the jaw where the great vein runs. The bay was dead before it finished falling.

Valeria stood over it with the blood running down the haft over her fingers. Her face did not change at all.

Alana looked at the General, because her eyes went to the thing that mattered. Roderic stood behind the line with his grandfather's sword hanging from his hand, looking at the dead horse as a man looks at the place where his house used to stand. Then he looked up at the woman with the bill, and something else came into his face on top of the first thing, without driving it out, so that he wore both together.

*Note,* thought Alana, in the cold small voice that came at the worst times. *The General has fallen in love. Subject does not appear to have been told.*

The second horse came up out of the hatch: a young chestnut mare with a white blaze, rearing at the head of the brow, her forefeet striking at nothing. Valeria set the Farrier. The mare came down onto the brow.

Roderic walked past the bill.

He did not hurry. He walked out between the hook and the horse with his sword gone and his empty hands held a little from his sides, palm down, talking: a low rough grumble like gravel in a barrel, the noise a man makes to a horse or a child in the night. The mare came down the brow at him and reared again, and her hooves went up past his face. He did not move. He went on talking. She came down, shaking, and he laid one hand flat on her nose, and she put her head into his chest and stood there.

Valeria came past the dead bay and stopped a spear's length off, as close as she would go to any horse, and her face in the torchlight was white with fury.

"For a *horse*. You walked in front of a horse, on a plank, in the dark, for a *horse*, you—you old—"

"She's four years old," said Roderic, without turning. His hand did not stop moving on the mare's nose.

"I don't care if she's four *days*!"

"I know you don't." He turned then, and looked at her over the mare's neck. "That's why I went."

Valeria turned on her heel and went back to her line for her shield, and did not look round again. Her hands had not shaken on the haft over the dead bay. Alana watched them shake now, on the shield-strap, and miss it twice.

*Amended,* said the cold small voice. *Two subjects. Neither has been told.*

---

It was then Alana saw the flask.

When the ship struck, a rack by her mainmast had spilled its iron onto the mole: squat flasks, lead-stoppered, rolling lightly, as empty iron rolls. *The empties. For the twelve.* One had not rolled. It was heavy: one the wood had not needed yet, come back on the *Tally* to spoil. It lay split on the edge of a stone like a burst seed-pod, and something black was coming out of it, slow as pitch in winter, into the joint between two stones where a fringe of green weed grew: the only living thing on the whole arm of the mole.

The black touched it, and the weed died. Not as a plant dies in frost, from the edges. It went black from the root up in the space of a breath, as a page goes black in a fire; and the black went on down into the crack between the stones, and she could see it going, down, seeking, as her own fire had gone down through the sea on the Kettle Bank.

*Not on a weed between two stones.*

"*Don't touch it!*" she heard herself shout. "*Nobody touch it!*"

A marine came off the brow on his hands and knees, wounded, crawling away from the bills. He did not understand her. He put his hand down to push himself up, and it came down on the split flask, and closed on it.

He screamed. She had heard men scream at Hollowmere. She had not heard that. The black was going into his hand like ink into a blotter, and the skin went grey round it and then dark, and the dark ran up his wrist along the veins in fine branching lines, like roots. Hostus had a strap round his arm before Alana could move, and the barber's face was as white as the marine's.

She had said *yes*, on the floor of the still-room with the lamp going out. She took off her cloak and laid it over the flask and the dead weed, and weighted the corners with stones, so that nobody else would put a hand there in the dark. It was all she could think of. It was not enough.

---

"*The stern!*"
