He looked back at her. She watched him not understand, and then begin to, and not let himself.

The song ended below in a roar. In the gap after it Kaeso's drum began, soft and slow and a little lame. *Dum*, da-da. *Dum*, da-da. A heartbeat with a limp. After a moment the bone pipe found it, thin and high, and followed.

"I asked him," said Alana. "This afternoon. After the song about the girl in Vessa, I said, and nobody to know who'd asked. It cost me two figs. I didn't ask the pipe. I counted on the pipe."

"You counted on the pipe."

"There is a difference between hoping and counting, Highness. I only ever do one of them."

Then she gave him the signal.

Her body had kept it five years in the dark, and did not need asking. The quarter-turn on the heel. Her good hand lifting, palm down, to the height of her shoulder, and held out into the place where the man should stand: the opening of the woman's figure of the Long Measure of Corvus. On the hatch-cover she had given it to the empty air and taken it back. She held it out to him, and kept it there.

"You offered," she said. "On the beach."

"I remember."

"I said not tonight. I said I'd been looked at enough." Below them two hundred men and half a town sat round a fire with its light in their eyes, and not one of them looked up. "Nobody's looking, Marcus. Finish it with me."

She had shouted his name once, on the Kettle Bank, with the dead coming over the rail. She had never said it. She heard him hear it.

He looked down at his own hand as if it belonged to someone he did not trust. Then he lifted it, the one without the stitches, and laid the back of his wrist under her palm, as the court does it: not palm to palm, only the man's wrist under the woman's fingers. It shook against her, a little. She held on.

"Yes," said Marcus. "Badly. I warned you."

"So you did."

"I'll tread on you."

"Then I'll take my boots off," said Alana, "and we'll know for certain."

She sat down on the cold crust of the dune and unlaced them, and pulled them off, and the stockings after them. Under the crust the sand was warm, the island's breath coming up through it. After a moment he sat down beside her and pulled off his own, a prince of Aeridor on a sandhill in the dark, swearing quietly at a knot. She set them in a row, his and hers, as he had once set hers down on the wet sand. Then she drew the cord out of her hair, and the pins after it, and held them out to him.

"Fingers held them," she said. "Last time."

He held out both hands, cupped. She let the pins fall into them, and he put them away inside his tunic against his ribs, with the arrow and the oilskin, with enormous care. Her hair came down.

She gave him her hand again, and the drum came round to the strong beat, and they danced.

The sand took every step and gave it back slow. And he was not bad. She had not expected that. He had danced it every Midwinter since he was twelve, and his feet had kept it as hers had. Only he was counting. She could hear him under his breath on every turn, *one*, two-three, *one*, two-three, steady as Rufo at a drill. She had never in her life heard a man of the court count.

*Note,* she thought. *Subject misrepresented his dancing.*

The walk round, her fingers on his wrist. The first great turn, under his lifted arm, and her skirts went out and the cold got in under them, and she let it. The passing, back to back, her shoulder blades a finger's width from his, the two of them circling on the slope with the fire going round below. And each time she came round to the place where for five years there had been nothing, there was something now: warm, and solid, and smelling of tar and woodsmoke, and counting.

On the second great turn he lost the count where the crust gave under him and nearly went down the dune, and caught himself, and laughed, short and surprised. He did not let go of her hand.

Then the last figure.

At court the Long Measure ended at arm's length, with a yard of polished floor kept between the man and the woman for the bow and the curtsy. The fisher-wives of Corvus had never troubled about the yard.

He drew her in on the last turn, and checked at arm's length. She did not check. She came the whole way in, against him, and laid her bandaged hand flat on his chest, and felt his heart going under it harder than the drum.

"That's not how it ends," said Marcus. His voice was not steady.

"It is on the quays."

Then she kissed him.

She had counted on the pipe. She had not counted past this, and found there was nothing past it to count. He made a sound against her mouth as if she had struck him, and his hands came up, one into her loose hair, closing there, and the other flat in the small of her back, holding her to him. She went back a step in the soft sand, and another, and he came with her, and there was something behind her: a shoulder of black rock that came up through the dune, the island's own bone, warm through the silk where the air was cold. He put her back against it. She let him. She had the breath out of her and did not want it back. His mouth tasted of cold and smoke and nothing else, no wine in it anywhere, and her good hand had closed in his cloak and would not open.

He found the ink under her ear. She knew he had, because he laughed against it, very low; and then he stopped laughing.

Below them the fire cracked and let go a long column of sparks into the dark.

They did not go up. They came up the dune instead, slow, in a long turning spiral, hundreds of them, gold and red, and gathered round the two of them against the rock, and went round them. They caught in her hair. They settled on his shoulders and his black cloak like gold dust, and did not burn.

She had drawn no rune. Her good hand was in his cloak and her burned one was at the back of his neck, and neither of them had drawn anything at all.

*Note,* said the cold small voice, from a long way off. *No rune.*

She told it to be quiet. For once in her life it was.

She took his face in both her hands, the burned and the whole, and pulled him down to her again; and the sparks went round and round them in the dark, and did not burn, and she would not have stopped if they had.

---

Before dawn she sat on the top step of the cave stair, at the door, with the Heart-Stone breathing gold up the long throat of the stair behind her and the courtyard still black in front. There was sand in her hem. She had not slept, and had not wanted to.

She turned to the back of the weather-book. Past the harbor, past the cohort's dead, to the name at the bottom of the last page.

She drew a line through it. One stroke, level: the stroke for a column that has come out.

Then she sat with the pen over the open book, and turned the pages front to back and back to front, the weather and the loading and the wind that had not come, and could not find the place to write him again.

The courtyard went from black to grey. Out in it, Felix began to call his list.

---

The *Patience* went out first on the morning tide, under her blue banner and her dolphin, with Valeria's report in her master's strongbox and the twentieth of Cyrene's shards in her binnacle, pointing back at the cave she was leaving. She went out under oars, thirty a side, rising and falling together like the legs of some great patient beetle, and the Grey took her at the gap. One moment the long low galley; the next a darker smudge in the white; then nothing.

Then it was their turn, and it was not.

Nerva stood at the *Forward*'s tiller with one finger wet and held up into the air, and he held it there a long time, as a priest holds up the bread at Midwinter, while the whole ship and half the quay watched it. Then he wiped it on his tunic.

"No," he said.

"Nerva," said Marcus.

"Wind, none." Alana had written the same two words in the weather-book the night before, by lamplight, and stopped there. Nerva had never read a page of it, and never would. "And the set out of the west, against us, three days of it. She's no galley, Highness. She's a two-master with horse-stalls, and she's down by the head with poison. I'll not put forty thieves on sweeps and row her to the elves. I'll not sail a pig, and I'll not tow one." He looked out at the Grey beyond the gap, flat and pale as milk in a pan. "Wind'll come. It always comes. Tomorrow. Day after."

So they did not go.

---

It was, Alana thought afterward, the most foolish morning of the war, and she had been present for the drill.

Near ninety souls who had said their farewells came back off the ship with their packs on their shoulders and stood about on the quay not knowing where to look. The town, which had come down in its shawls to watch them go, stayed where it was to watch them not go, which was plainly better. Felix stood in the water-gate with his list and read it backward, item by item, with no expression whatever, as casks came up the stair that had gone down it the night before.

Valeria and the General had crossed the courtyard together at first light, the grey mare on one side of him and the captain of the Ford on the other, as far from the mare as the courtyard allowed, at exactly the General's speed; and the Ford, mustered at the water-gate, had watched them come with faces so carefully empty that the emptiness was louder than a cheer. Old Baebius, it was said, had had a word with the front rank at the gate. Now the mare was led back off the ship with the deepest reproach, and the General went after her to apologize, and the Ford's faces did not change at all. And Fingers, who was staying, went down the line of the cohort on the quay with his tablet, collecting on a book he had opened the morning of the drill. When it was put to him that nobody had sailed, he said that the book had been on *whether*, and not on *when*, and that a man who could not tell the difference had no business betting; and went on collecting.

Alana stood at the rail with nothing to do. Her mouth was tender. She had put her hair up that morning with four pins, because the rest were inside a prince's tunic, and she knew exactly where he was on the deck without looking, and she did not look.

Marcus had gone down to the prisoners while Nerva's finger was still in the air. Alana heard him ask it, from the rail. "The man last night. Statius." Rufo had sent him word of it at the time. He asked anyway.

Merula put on his lenses to answer. "I was on the step above him, Highness. No man laid a hand on him. He slipped on the weed under the lead, and his chain took him over. That's the whole of it."

Marcus looked at him as he looked at a column he meant to trust. Then he nodded, and something went out of his shoulders that Alana had not known was in them. Not grief. It was nearer the look of a man told that the fire in the next street did not start at his hearth. *Not by any hand of mine,* she thought he was thinking. He came back up the brow and did not ask again.

The wind did not come that day. At noon she wrote it in the book: *Wind, none.* At dusk she wrote it again.

---

**LIORA**

The wind did not come, and so she had a day she had not counted on.

She spent the morning of it unpacking. The satchels came back off the *Forward* on Felix's list and went back onto Hostus's shelves in the steam-box house, jar by jar, in the order she had taken them down; and Hostus watched her do it with his clean hands folded and said nothing, because he had been a barber twenty years and knew when a woman did not want to be helped. The pots of liniment she had made for the hurts she was leaving behind stood in a row on the end of the bench, each with its name scratched on the lid. One for the cooper's widow on Rope Street with the burns. One for Opiter's shoulder. One for the netmender's mother, and her jaw. None for Statius. His was in her own satchel, and it would keep.

There had been none, either, for the man they called Pansa.

She made it at noon, while the steam came down through the roof-vents and Hostus was at his dinner.

He was the one who laughed. A small quick man, bald as an egg, with a gap in his teeth he could whistle through, and the habit of making the other prisoners laugh at the shovels when the town was watching, which the town did not forgive. The slips had split his hands. Men who work wet timber in the frost get it: the skin cracks across the knuckles and in the webs of the thumbs, and the cracks go down to the red and will not close, and every grip opens them again. On the eleventh day Rufo had brought him into the steam-box house in his chains, with two others, and he had held his hands out to her across the bench, palms up, grinning, the way he had held them out once before, in another light.

*Sister,* he had said. *They tell me you've the touch.*

She had given him goose-grease then. It was all the hands deserved, and she had noted that her own did not shake, and sent him back to the pen.

Today she gave him something better.

Wolfsbane: the blue hood, that grows in the cold places of every island that has a cold place. Every healer of the Vigil carries it dried, in a twist of paper, because there is nothing better made for the pain that lives in a nerve. A little of it in lard, rubbed into a bad tooth or a burned hand, and the pain goes, and the flesh goes quiet and far away, like a room heard through a wall. More, and the flesh goes away altogether: no heat, no cold, no edges, the hand at the end of the arm a thing you must look at to know where it is. The sisters taught the measure to the grain, and they taught it as a warning. She had been an apt pupil. She ground the root fine with the camphor, so that it would smell like every other pot on the bench, and worked it into the lard with her thumb, and scratched *Pansa* on the lid in her neat hand, and set it at the end of the row.

Then she washed her hands three times, in the hot water from the culvert, up to the elbows, as you wash after a fever-bed.

---

She went down to the slips in the early afternoon with Hostus and the basket, to show him the hurts he would have when she was gone.

The sky had cleared over the harbor, which it seldom did, and there was a hard white frost on everything the sun had not reached. Gaius had the nine on the first slip, where the one hull he could save stood in her cradle of shores with staging up her side: planks on trestles, a man's height and a half above the stone, and a ladder to them. They were taking off her burned strakes and carrying up new ones. The ladder was rimed white on its north face, and the men going up it in their irons, with a stride of chain between their ankles, went slowly, one rung and both feet and the next, as old women go up a stair.

She gave out the pots. Merula took his for his knees without a word, and put it inside his cloak. The others took theirs as men take things from a woman of the Vigil, which is to say not looking at her. Pansa took his and grinned, and opened it, and sniffed. Camphor.

"Twice a day," said Liora. "Rub it well in. Into the cracks."

He rubbed it in there and then, sitting on a timber end with the pot between his knees, working it into his split knuckles with a great show of care for the benefit of the men around him. They laughed. He was good at that. She stood and watched him do it, and explained to Hostus about the cracks, and how they must be kept soft, and how the cold opens them, in exactly the voice she would have used for any man's hands.

When the next strake was called for he went to the foot of the ladder and flexed his fingers, and looked at them, and flexed them again.

"Gone quiet," he said, wondering. "Sister, they've gone right quiet. Not a thing." He showed them round. "Best they've been since the snow."

"Good," said Liora.

Two of the others lifted the strake onto his left shoulder, and he took the ladder.

She did not watch him go up. She watched Hostus's face, which was turned up to watch him, because Hostus was a careful man and would always look up at a man on a ladder. So she saw it go across the old barber's face before she heard it: the reaching, and the hand closing round a rung it could not feel, on the iced side, and closing round nothing at all. She heard the strake come off his shoulder and boom on the staging. She heard the chain. She heard him, once, not a word; and then the stone.

He had gone backward, as a man goes who has been holding on with his eyes and not his hands. A man's height and a half is not very high. It was high enough. He lay at the ladder's foot in the frost with his head at an angle no head takes on its own, and his hands open at his sides, palms up, the cracks in them shining with her lard.

She went to him because it was what she did. She knelt on the frost and put two fingers under the angle of his jaw, and waited the proper time, and a little longer. Behind her the slip had gone quiet the way a slip goes quiet, all at once, adzes held up in the air.

"His neck," she said.

"Ah," said Rufo, from his bollard.

Old Baebius had come down from the harbor-head at the noise, with his bill on his shoulder. He looked at the ladder, and at the man, and at the frost.

"Man off a ladder," he observed.

"So there is," said Rufo, and took out his tally-stick. He looked at it a while, as if it had said something to him. Then he made the mark.

Gaius had come down off the staging, white to the lips. He stood at the ladder's foot with his ruined hands hanging and looked at its rungs, at the white bloom of rime on the north face of every one.

"Battens," he said. "I've asked for battens on that ladder since the fifth day. Nailed across. Every engineer in the isles knows a rung goes like glass in the frost. Since the fifth day, Centurion."

"So you have," said Rufo. "I've a note of it." He had too: it was on the stick. "Battens."

Merula had not moved. He stood among the others with his hands folded on the haft of his shovel and the lenses on his face, and looked at the man on the stone, and at the open pot standing on the timber end where it had been set down. He did not look at her. He looked at the pot the way he looked at a column of figures that had come out right, and that he did not like.

Two of the others carried Pansa up to the old ice-house under the fort, where Statius was, by his arms and his chain. Nobody said anything at all.

---

That night she lay on her pallet in the room behind the steam-box house and waited, as she had waited on the stair, for the horror.

It did not come this time either. She had not expected it to. What came instead was quieter, and worse: a kind of tidiness. The sense, which she knew from the end of a long night on the wards, of a bench cleared and a basin emptied and one more thing put away in its place. *Two,* said the part of her that counted, without heat, as it would have said *two dressings left.*

She lay in the dark with her hands folded on her breast, as the dead are laid, and found she was afraid.

Not of the prince, or of the rope on Orso Netmender's door. Of the tidiness. She had washed the dead since she was sixteen. She had seen what men became who killed and felt nothing, and then killed again because they wanted to feel it, and then again because by then it was what they were. She had sewn up what they did. The Vigil had a word for such men, and did not use it in front of novices.

Somewhere above the town the wind did not come. The Grey lay beyond the moles without a breath on it, and in the morning, Nerva said, it would lie there still.

---

They burned the two of them at noon on the black beach, Statius and Pansa together, on Cyrene's third-yard pine.

Merula had asked that someone of theirs should stand at it. *At home we don't burn them,* he had said again, to the prince, flatly, as a clerk repeats a figure for a man who has not written it down. *But they were ours. Someone of ours should be by.* So the eight came down from the slips in their chains, between the Ford, and stood in a line at the windward side of the pyre with the heat on their faces: Merula at one end with his lenses, and at the other end the boy.

He was not truly a boy. He was nineteen, a long thin youth with big red hands and a neck like a heron's, the youngest of them now that Nonius was ash, and he had not lifted his eyes from the ground since the first day against the tower wall. They called him Sennius. She did not let herself think more about him than that.

Between Merula and the boy stood the sergeant.

Atilius. A big man, grey at the temples and thick through the shoulder, who stood even in irons with his feet apart and his weight even, as a sergeant stands at the back of a line, as if the beach were his to keep and the rest of them on it by his leave. It was he who had given Nonius the lamp, on the night the *Tally* first called, because Nonius was youngest. He stood with his big hands loose at his sides and watched the fire take his men, and nothing at all moved in his face. Then his eyes came across the fire to her, through the shaking air, and rested on her a while, as they had rested on her before.

She had not decided anything about him. She had made sure of it. She had kept him in the dark of the room where she did not look, with the door shut, since the gate.

She said the litany.

She said it as she always said it, low, in the old vowels, at the pace of the names: the eighty first, all of them, and the town's after them, street by street, and the *Tally*'s, and Nonius, who held the lamp. Then the two on the fire.

"Statius, of Tharsis," she said. "Who had a bad back.

"Pansa, of Tharsis. Who laughed."

The pine roared and spat. Across the fire Atilius's eyes had not left her. He knew. She saw it come into his face as the names were said, as she had seen it go into Statius's face on the weed: not suspicion, nothing so careful. Only knowledge, all at once, the way an animal knows. And with it, very slowly, because he was a slow and certain man, something that might in another man have been a smile. *You,* it said. *You, Sister?*

She opened the door.

---

The Vigil had a word for it too, though not one the sisters were ashamed of. The Seeing. With both her hands laid on a man, and her eyes shut, and enough of herself poured out into her palms, a healer of the Vigil could see into him: not with the eyes, but as you see your own hand in the dark, knowing where it is. Most of the sisters needed an hour on their knees to do it once. Liora had done it on the wards a hundred times, quickly, because it was quicker than asking a man where it hurt when he could no longer tell you. She had never once done it without her hands on him.

She had never done it across ten paces of fire.

She did not shut her eyes. She kept them on his, and reached.

It was like walking into the sea in winter. It was like putting her forehead to a laden cart and pushing it up the fort road. The heat of the pyre came between them and she went through it; the shaking air, and the smoke, and the fat smell of what was burning, and the rattle of the chains when the line shifted, and she went through all of it. Something warm ran out of her nose and over her lip. She let it run. Her knees wanted to go and she told them no, in the voice for the farthest pallet, and they did not go.

Then she was in.

It was dark there, and red, and it was loud: the great slow fist of him, clenching and opening, clenching and opening, as it had clenched every moment of every day of his life, without his leave, in the dark. She had seen a hundred. She knew her way about a heart as she knew her way about her father's house. Four rooms, and four doors between the rooms, and the doors made of something thinner than the skin of a drum, opening one way only. And the doors held on cords. The old anatomists of the Vigil called them the heartstrings, and the novices laughed the first time, until they had seen them: white, fine, tough as gut, a hundred of them, like the shrouds of a ship, holding each door so that it shut and did not blow through.

His had a flaw. Most hearts that had carried a man fifty years did. On the left side, low, one of the doors did not close true; it fluttered at the end of every beat, like a sail luffing, and the strings that held its edge had worn thin on one side, thin as thread. He had felt it, perhaps, on stairs. He had put it down to the wine.

She took two of the strings, the thin one and its neighbor, as she would have taken two ends of a suture. Not with her hands. With the part of her that had been reaching since she opened the door.

She tied a knot.

A surgeon's knot, the first one the sisters teach: a double throw, then a single, so that it holds of itself and will not slip. She drew it down until the door was bound to the wall and could not open.

The fist clenched on nothing.

---

Across the fire Atilius put his hand to his chest. It was an odd small gesture, almost polite, the hand of a man who has found something missing from an inside pocket. Then his face changed. He went down on one knee on the black sand, and the chain between his ankles went taut and threw the man beside him, and he went the rest of the way down onto his side and lay there with his heels working in the sand.

The line broke up shouting. The Ford came in with their bills. Somebody shouted for water, and somebody else for the healer, and Merula went down on his knees in his chains beside his sergeant and took hold of his collar, as if that would help.

Liora stood where she was.

She stood with the blood running off her chin and her hands folded at her waist and waited to be called. It was what she would have done for any man. You did not run to a man on a beach till you knew which man, and whether he was yours. The Vigil had taught her that before it taught her anything else.

"Healer," said Rufo.

She went round the fire.

She knelt in the hot black sand beside the sergeant, and put two fingers under the angle of his jaw, and under them his pulse went like a bird in a box, everywhere at once and nowhere. His lips were going blue. His eyes were open, and they found her face, and held it.

They were struggling. That was the word for them. Not afraid yet, not quite; struggling, the way a man struggles who has gone through ice, with all his great strength, against something he cannot see and cannot hold. They asked her for help. They knew there was no help in her, and they asked anyway. They knew whose face it was. They asked anyway.

She could undo it.

She knew that, kneeling there, quite exactly. A single pull on the right end, and the first throw would come free, and the door would open, and the fist would close on blood again and go on closing. He would live. He would be grey and short of breath for a month, and Hostus would bleed him and bless her for her quickness, and in the morning she would sail.

She looked into his eyes and let him see her.

And it came to her, then, out of the room where she did not look, with the door standing open now and the light going in: not a picture. She had never let it be a picture, and she did not now. Only the order of it. The others had held. He had not held. He had not needed to. He had been first, and he had been the worst, and he had been every time; and afterward he had stood over her and looked down at her, as he was looking up at her now, and smiled the slow certain smile of a man for whom it is all one, the beach and the room and the woman, all his to keep.

She tightened the knot.

It went down hard under the part of her that held it, and she felt the heart buck against it like a horse against a hobble, and buck again, weaker.

His back arched off the sand. The struggling went out of his eyes and the fear came into them at last, all of it, and the pain after it, enormous, a pain like a fist the size of the sky closing on the middle of him. His mouth opened. Nothing came out of it. His hand found her sleeve and clutched there, and she did not take it away.

She had watched a great many men die. She watched this one. She watched the light go out of him the way it goes out of all of them, from the edges in, slowly, like a lamp in a room where somebody has shut the door; and in the middle of it, at the very end, he was not the sergeant any more. He was only a man on a beach, a long way from home, dying in pain, holding on to a stranger's sleeve, as the boy of the Fourteenth had held on to hers on the hospital ship, and asked whether she had come for him.

And she was sorry.

It came up from under her breastbone like the warm water coming up through the floor of the cave, slow and from very deep, and it filled her, and her eyes stung, and her hands, at last, began to shake. Not sorry that he was dying. She would have tied the knot again, tomorrow, and the day after, and every day. Sorry for all of it: for him, for her, for the room, for what it had made of both of them, for this beach and this fire and this blue mouth gasping in the sand. Sorry the way she had been sorry for every man she had ever knelt beside, the way she had been sorry since she was sixteen.

She could have wept with the relief of it.

She had been afraid since the stair. Since the smile she had caught with her fingers, and the tidy bench last night, and the word the Vigil did not say in front of novices. She had been afraid it had gone out of her at the vat for good, with whatever else had gone, and that what was left was only the counting. It had not gone. It was here, it hurt, it was hers. A monster would not have felt it. She held on to it as she would have held on to a pulse she had thought was gone.

She did not loosen the knot.

She laid her free hand flat on his chest, over the place, and let the other thing go out of her into him, the warm thing, the thing the Vigil had taught her and the thing it had not: and she took the pain out of him. Not the death. The pain. She drew it out of him into her own palm, as she had drawn it out of a hundred men on a hundred nights, and held it, and let it burn there, and did not let go. Under her hand the great fist fluttered, and fluttered, and was quiet. His face eased. The clutch on her sleeve went slack. His eyes stayed on hers to the end, and at the end there was nothing in them but a kind of surprise, and then nothing at all.

She waited the proper time, and a little longer, because that was what she did.

Then she closed his eyes. With two fingers, gently, one lid and then the other, as she would have closed her own father's; and she let her hand rest a moment on his brow, because there was nobody else on that beach who would.

"His heart," said Rufo, behind her, quietly. He had said the same once of an old debtor under a bench on the *Clemency*. He said it the same way.

"Yes," said Liora.

She got up off the hot sand with her hands shaking and the blood drying on her lip, and stood, and found that she could stand.

*Not a monster,* she thought.

*Not an angel.*

*Liora.*

---

**MARCUS**

Merula found him, which was a thing that had not happened before.

The fire was down to its red bones. The Ford had taken the seven back up to the slips, and Atilius up to the ice-house where the other two had lain, to wait for tomorrow's pine, if tomorrow had no wind either. Marcus had stayed on the black beach because he did not want to be anywhere else, and he was standing at the edge of the heat when the chain came clinking down the sand behind him, and Rufo's voice said, "He asked, sir. I said I'd ask you." And there was the grey captain at the end of it, with his lenses on their cord and his hands folded in front of him on the links.

"Walk with me, Captain," said Marcus.

They walked along the tideline, slowly, at the pace of the chain. Rufo came after at ten paces, with his split shield, looking at the sea.

"Three, Highness," said Merula.

Marcus said nothing.

"Of eleven. In three days." The flat dry voice did not change. "I don't count the boy. Nonius was ours to lose, and we lost him. I count the others. Statius off the stair, in the evening, under the lead. Pansa off the ladder, in the afternoon, in the frost. Atilius on this beach at noon, of his heart. And there was nothing the matter with Atilius's heart. He could carry a keel-block up the slip on his own. I've seen him."

"Men die, Captain. We're at war."

"Men do. Columns don't." Merula stopped, and the chain stopped with him. "I've added columns for the Survey four years. One man slips, that's weed. Two, that's frost. Three, that's a hand." He put his hand inside his cloak and brought something out, and held it out on his palm. A little clay pot, the lid scratched with a name in a neat hand. *Pansa.* "He had it on his hands at noon yesterday. He said they'd gone quiet. He was pleased." The colorless eyes held his. "She gave me one for my knees. I've not opened it. I'll not, I think."

Marcus took the pot. He did not open it either.

"Why bring me this?" he said. "You could have given it to Valeria's clerk. Or the Ford. Or kept it."

"Because you'll ask, Highness, the way you asked about Statius. You'll stand on the stair and look at me through your father's face and ask, and I'll have to answer you." Merula took off the lenses, and wiped them on the brown cloak, and did not put them back on. Without them his face looked naked, and older, and very tired. "And because I'll say a thing once, and then I'll have said it, and you can believe it or not. Those nights, after the fort fell, I was at the slips. The *Tally* was in. I had the Survey's gear to see down the stair and their pilot to keep sober and their lists to sign. I learned what was done in the steam-box house after it was done, from the look of her in the morning. I'd not have allowed it." He put the lenses on again. "I didn't stop it afterward, either. I had no men to stop it with but those men. That's mine. I'll carry it."

The surf ran up the black sand and hissed and went back.

Marcus stood with the pot in his hand and the fire at his back, and saw it.

He saw it all at once, as Merula said a sum came: the whole of it, already written, waiting for him to read it. The courtyard, on the first day. The eleven against the tower wall, and the healer coming through the gate behind him with her bloody wrists, and two of the eleven dropping their eyes as she passed, quick, as men drop them in front of a magistrate. He had seen that. He had seen it and not known what he saw, and gone on to Orso and the rope. *Lay it at my door,* she had said to Valeria on the slip. *If you need a door to lay it at.* She had asked him for their lives. She had been the one to ask.

And he had asked her to see to the big man's back.

He had stood in the door of the steam-box house and asked it, in the voice he kept for being refused, and she had said yes, and he had thought her a saint for it.

"Who knows?" he said.

"Of mine? The six. They'll say nothing; they're afraid of her now. And me." Merula considered. "Of yours, I couldn't say. Your centurion makes a great many marks on that stick."

Ten paces back, Rufo looked at the sea with enormous interest.

"There's one more of mine," said Merula. "Who knows what was done in that room." He did not look away. "Sennius. The long lad at the end of the line. Nineteen. They put him on the door with a lamp, those nights, because he was youngest after Nonius, and the sergeant said so. He held it. He's never lifted his eyes since. He wasn't one of the three."

"You're sure."

"I asked him. Last night, in the pen, when Pansa didn't come back. He wept." Merula said it as he said everything, as a figure. "He thinks he's next. He didn't sleep. I don't think he'll sleep tonight. I'm not asking anything of you, Highness. I've no right to. I'm only telling you the sum, because you'll sail when the wind comes, and I'll be here, with six. And him among them."

Marcus looked down at the pot in his hand. It was a good pot, plain brown clay, the kind the potters of Cyrene made by the hundred for the hospital at the Baths. The name on the lid was very neatly done.

He had said in front of the whole town that no man of the eleven would die by his men's hand. He had believed, yesterday on the stair, that he had kept it. He had kept it. That was the worst of it. It had been kept, to the letter, three times.

"Thank you, Captain," he said.

"Don't." Merula turned for the slip, and the chain turned with him. "I've done nothing. I'm a prisoner. I only add."

---

Felix knew where she was. Felix always knew.

"The Baths, Highness," he said, not looking up from his list. "She went up after the fire. She's not been up there once before. Not once since we came. I asked Hostus." He made a mark, and then, unusually, looked up. "I'd go gently, Highness. If you're going. She's not been eating."

---

**LIORA**

The Baths had a window at the end of the long ward that looked down over the whole harbor: the slips, the moles, the two towers at the gap, and beyond them the Grey, lying on the sea without a breath on it, faintly gold now with the sun going down behind it somewhere in the west where the wood was.

The beds were still there. The garrison had not troubled to take them away. Eighty frames of ash-wood in two long rows, with the straw pulled out of them and the blankets gone, and on the wall above each, still, the little nail where she had hung the slate with the man's name.

She stood at the window with her back to them and looked at the sea.

She had not meant to come up. She had found herself on the hill road after the fire with the blood dry on her lip and her hands still shaking, and her feet had known the way, as feet do; and the door had been on the latch, and she had come in, and walked down between the two rows without looking left or right, and stood here. She had stood here a great many evenings, once, at the end of the day-watch, with the ward behind her settling for the night. The gap looked just the same. That seemed to her very strange.

She heard him on the stair. She knew his step; everyone on the island did. She did not turn round.

He came down the ward between the beds, not quickly. She heard him stop once, halfway, and she knew without looking that he had stopped by one of the nails, and was reading what was not written there. Then he came on, all the way to the window.

He stood behind her, and a little to her side. Too close.

She noticed it the way she noticed a pulse that has changed. In fifteen days he had never once stood this close to her. Not on the *Clemency*'s deck, nor in the courtyard, nor on the black sand the night she had sat down in the surf; not at her table with his arm under her needle. He kept a distance from her as he kept it from Alana, exactly, because he knew to the inch what a man's nearness could be to a woman. And now he had closed it, without asking, and was standing in it, and was saying nothing.

So he knew.

It went through her cold, from the scalp down. Not surprise. She had known since the beach that he would know; a man who could read a town's face in a courtyard could read three bodies. But there is knowing a thing will come and there is the thing, standing at your back, and she found she was afraid. Simply afraid, the way the body is, with its stomach. She thought of the rope coiled on its nail on Orso Netmender's door. She thought of the prince in the courtyard, telling the whole town in that quiet voice that no man of the eleven would die by his men's hand: and she was not his man, and never had been, and she had made his word into a thing that could be kept to the letter three times over. He would not forgive that. A king's son could not. And she thought, quite clearly, as she had thought on the landing with the count of three in her mouth: *Very well.*

If it was to be him, then let it be him. She had done what she had done. She would not run, and she would not lie to him. And she would not, whatever he asked, tell him why.

She felt herself go still all through, the way a man goes still on the table when he has decided to let you do it.

He took her by the shoulder and turned her round.

It was quick, and his grip was hard, harder than he knew; the fingers went into the joint above the bone. She came round to face him with her back against the cold stone of the window-ledge, and he was there, a hand from her, and his eyes went into hers and held. Grey eyes. She had stitched his arm and never troubled to notice their color. They were grey as the sea under the Grey, and as cold, and they went into her a long way, looking for something; and she let them look, and did not lower hers, and waited for what he would say.

"The boy lives," he said.

That was all.

She heard it, and for a moment did not understand it, and then she did.

The boy. The long one at the end of the line, with the big red hands and the neck like a heron's, who had stood at a door because a sergeant told him to, and held up a lamp because a sergeant told him to, and looked at the floor; and had been looking at it ever since. He had not slept last night. She knew that too. She had seen his face at the fire when the sergeant went down.

The prince had not said *stop*. He had not said *murderer*, or *why*, or *how*, or anything at all about the other three. He had not asked her a single thing. He had come all the way up the hill and down the long ward between the empty beds and closed the distance he had kept for fifteen days to say one thing, and it was the only thing that was still his to say.

Her fear went out of her all at once, like water out of a split cask, and left her light-headed.

"This war may take him," said Liora. "But I will not."

She turned away from him, back to the window and the sea.

She felt him stand there a moment longer, behind her. Then his hand left her shoulder. She heard him go back up the ward between the beds, not quickly, and stop again halfway, by the same nail, and go on; and the door, and his step on the stair.

The Grey lay on the sea, gold going to grey. Somewhere out on it, far off, a breath of air touched the surface and made a dark patch the size of a field, and went away again.

She stood at the window until it was too dark to see the gap, and then a while longer.

---

**ALANA**

She made the list on the hill road, coming down from the fort in the dark, because it would not stop making itself.

*Item.* Statius, the evening before last, off the foot of the water-gate stair under the lead. A big man with a bad back, which the healer had laid her hands on, at the prince's asking; the whole cohort knew it, because the whole cohort knew everything. Slipped, on the weed. Everyone very quick.

*Item.* Pansa, yesterday afternoon, off the staging of the first slip, in the frost. Hostus had told her about it at the well, unasked, with his clean hands shaking a little. A pot of liniment for the cracks in his hands; the man had said they'd gone quiet. *Never seen a salve take like it, my lady. Not in twenty years. Not a thing could he feel.* Alana had read the *Book of Simples* in the long reading room at Corvus in her second winter, cover to cover, because it was there. There were four herbs in the isles that would take the feeling out of a hand so that a man could not find a ladder-rung with it. Three of them stank. The fourth was the blue hood, and it smelled of nothing at all, and every healer of the Vigil carried it.

*Item.* Atilius, at noon, on the black beach.

She had been at the fire. She had stood at the back of it, among the cohort, with her burned hand in her sleeve, because the prince had wanted the whole cohort there for the Tharsians' burning, as for their own. And partway through the litany, while the healer said the names, something had drawn on the air.

There was no other way to put it. She had felt it as she felt her own stroke gather, before it is let go: the tightening, the pull from every side toward one place, the air gone thick and strange and the hair rising along her arms. A working, and a great one, close by, being done by somebody with no rune and no rod and no right to be doing it. She had turned her head looking for the hand that drew it, the way a dog turns its head at a whistle. She had not found it. Then the sergeant had gone down on the sand across the fire, with his hand on his chest like a man who has lost his purse.

And the healer had stood quite still, with blood running out of her nose, and waited to be called.

*Note,* said the cold small voice. *No rune.*

Alana told it, for the second time in two days, to be quiet. This time it did not obey her.

---

The steam-box house was empty but for Liora, and the eight wounded marines asleep on their pallets, and a lamp.

She was packing again. Nerva had come up from the harbor at dusk and stood in the courtyard and sniffed the air like a hound, and said, *Morning,* and gone back down; and the satchels were coming down off Hostus's shelves for the second time in two days, jar by jar, in the same order. Liora did not look up when the door opened. She went on wrapping a jar of something in linen, and setting it in its place, and taking up the next.

Alana stood inside the door with the snow on her shoulders.

She had drafted it on the hill road. She had drafted it a great many times, as she drafted everything, and it was still not right, and she began it anyway, because she did not know how not to.

"At Corvus," she said, "in my third year, Master Hesper set us a course of reading on appetites."

Liora wrapped the jar.

"The academy kept a whole bay of the long room on it. The appetites of the body and of the mind, and how they take hold. Wine, mostly. Poppy. Dice. There's a great deal written on dice; the men who write treatises are very often men who dice." She heard herself, and could not stop herself. "I read it because I'd decided, at fourteen, that I would never in my life be at the mercy of anything I couldn't name. There was a shelf on the drink. I read every book on it. I'd met a prince at a Midwinter dance by then, and I suppose I wanted to know." She kept her eyes on the lamp. "And there was one book at the end of the bay on the other appetite. The one that grows on killing. It was by a magistrate of the old fens who had hanged a great many men, and talked with every one of them the night before. He said it is the same as the drink, exactly. The first is hard, and costs a great deal, and a man thinks he will never pay so much again. The second costs less. By the third it costs nothing at all, and that is when it begins to take its price, because a thing that costs nothing to do—"

"Stop," said Liora.

She had not raised her voice. Nobody in Cyrene raised their voice. She set down the jar in its linen, in its place, and turned round.

"I'm not in the mood for a lecture, my lady. Not tonight. Not from you." Her face in the lamplight was quite calm, and white, and the dried blood was still at the edge of one nostril where she had not troubled to wash it. "Tell me something instead. You've a great deal of learning. Tell me how it is that you can walk about this island without your stockings, in the middle of two hundred thieves and murderers and men who have not seen a woman's ankle in a year, without the least concern in the world for your safety."

Alana opened her mouth.

"I'll tell you," said Liora. "Because you are death in a skirt. Every man of the two hundred knows it, and so does every Tharsian left alive on this island. In fifteen days you have made more dead than I could heal in a lifetime of trying. Limbs in the air. Men cooked inside their mail. Bellies burst like wineskins in a fire. You set the sea alight on the Kettle Bank with the dead standing under it. The black beach. The towers. The mole, three nights since. I had what the dead had done on my table, and I had what you had done, and I could tell the one from the other by the smell. You came down off the west tower with your face cut open and your hair full of other men's blood and asked Hostus to hurry, because you were wanted." She did not move. "And you come in here and stand at my door and talk to me about murder."

"I didn't say—"

"You were working round to it. By way of Corvus." Liora's mouth moved, very slightly. "You're kinder than you think you are, my lady. You'd have got there by the end of the bay."

The lamp burned. On the nearest pallet a marine of the *Tally* stirred in his sleep and said something in his round Tharsian speech, and was quiet.

"You're a warrior," said Liora. "The General calls you a witch, to your face and behind your back, and he means it fondly; but it's the wrong word, and he knows it. You're a warrior. You do what warriors do, and you do it better than any of them, and they sing about it. And I am a warrior too. Who finished what those men started."

She said it in the voice for the farthest pallet. It carried without being raised.

"Men are pardoned for the crimes of war. There's a writ for it. We all watched one read on the quay, with a seal on it, in front of the town. Every soldier on the isles is pardoned the day the war ends, for whatever he did in it, and the songs see to the rest. Women aren't. Women don't get the writ. Women live with the crimes of war for the rest of their lives." She did not blink. "Those three were warriors too. That's what they'd have told you. They committed the wrong crime, on the wrong cunt. And they got the bad luck they deserved, and no more than they deserved. Bad luck. A stair. A ladder. A heart." She took a breath. "Can you say as much, my lady? For every one of the mountain of bodies on your conscience? That every one of them got only what he deserved?"

Alana said nothing.

She stood inside the door with the snow melting on her shoulders and found, for once in her life, that she had nothing drafted. Not a word. She had read every book in the bay, and none of them had this in it.

The Kettle Bank. She had not counted. She had told herself they were dead already, the sea-dead, the Fourteenth's own; that she had only put them down. Nineteen of them had been the eighty. She had not counted the towers either. She had not counted the mole. She had a page in the back of the weather-book for the dead she owed, and not one of those was on it.

Liora looked at her a moment longer. Then she turned back to the bench, and took up the next jar, and began to wrap it.

"Shut the door when you go, my lady," she said. "It lets the cold in on them."

Alana went out, and shut the door.

The snow had stopped. Overhead, for the first time in three days, the steam from the roof-vents was not going straight up into the dark. It leaned. It leaned south, in a long grey feather, and frayed, and blew away.

She stood in the slush in the yard of the steam-box house and looked back at the yellow crack of light under the door, and thought of what the Fourteenth's wounded had called her, singing it to a marching tune on the wards, which Hostus had told half the cohort by now: *the Angel of the Baths*. And of the angel's face over the sergeant on the beach, with the blood running down to her chin, closing his eyes so gently that half the cohort had looked away.

*Note,* she thought, quite clearly, in her own voice and not the cold one. *Subject is not an angel.*

And then, because she was thorough, and because it was true, she underlined it.

*Definitely* not an angel.

---

The wind came in the small hours, out of the north, cold off the ice, steady as a hand on the back. By first light Nerva had his finger in it and did not trouble to say anything at all.

Then it was their turn.

Alana made the list in her head as they warped off the stair, because lists were what her mind did when the rest of her was doing something it did not understand. This morning the rest of her was doing a great deal it did not understand, and the list would not hold still.

Marcus at the stern, in the patched black cloak, with his stitched arm in a sling he kept taking it out of. She had not meant to begin with him. She did not look at him. She knew exactly where he was without looking, which was worse. She began again from the bow.

Iason alone at the bow, looking west. Atticus and his eleven on the forehatch, eating pickled fish at an hour when nobody should eat pickled fish. Rufo and forty shields, every one stenciled *XIV*. Valeria and twenty of the Ford along the waist with their bills lashed to the rail, Valeria already a faint and dignified green.

Roderic at the main hatch with one hand on the coaming, looking down into the dark. Gaius half over the taffrail, glaring at his new pintle. Liora at the mainmast with her satchels, her hands folded on them. Her face was quiet, and paler than it had been, and she looked, for the first time since Alana had known her, as if she had slept. Alana looked at her once, and went on with the list, and did not look at her again until the list was done. Felix everywhere. Kaeso with the drum. Nerva at the tiller with Pell beside him, and the cohort men who knew a rope's end from its middle at the sheets. Marcus at the stern.

She had done Marcus. She began again.

In the hold, packed in straw in their two crates, the twelve. She knew where they were the way one knows where a sleeping dog is in a dark room. Twelve, and not one more. The morning after the fight she had burned the joint on the mole where the split flask had lain, weed and black and her own cloak together, with the small stroke laid on again and again until the mortar ran like glass and nothing in the crack would ever drink again; and Gaius had soldered the split flask into a lead box with his ruined hands, and shut it in the still-room with the *Tally*'s empties, behind the three-ward lock.

And in the *Tally*'s own stall under the main hatch, the grey mare, who had walked up the brow for the second time because Roderic asked her to, and then stood looking at the planking all round her with an expression of the deepest betrayal. The chestnut stayed in the fort stable, with Tam to see to her.

On the stair they left seven in irons. There had been eleven against the tower wall on the first day. Rufo had a mark on his stick for each of the last three, and if anyone in the fort had a view on it, nobody said so in Alana's hearing.

Merula stood among the seven with his lenses in his hand, looking at the ship like a debt he had decided not to collect, and Old Baebius and his forty behind them. At the end of the line the long lad, Sennius, had lifted his eyes from the ground for once. He was looking at the mainmast. Hirtius was in the pen, with the fourteen of the *Tally*'s seamen and marines who had thrown down their axes on her deck, chained to the same ring-bolt; and the eight of her wounded who had lived, Fannius with his one arm among them, lay on the pallets of the steam-box house under guard. The grey man was in the old sluice-house, and its door was not wedged open now. Hostus stood in the door of the steam-box house with his clean hands folded, keeping the hospital. And at the end of the quay, barefoot on the cold stone, with his father's big hand on his shoulder, Tam.

He did not wave. Nobody in Cyrene waved. He watched, as the town had watched the eleven on the slips; and as the *Forward* went out through the gap under the towers Alana saw him turn and run full tilt up the fort road toward the headland.

The bell began as the Grey closed over them. Not the alarm. The other one. She listened until the fog swallowed it.

---

By night the Grey had them entirely.

At dusk, before the watch changed, Liora had stood at the mainmast with her hands folded on her satchels and said the names, low, in the old vowels, as she said them every night: the eighty, and the town's, street by street, and the *Tally*'s, and the boy who held the lamp. Then, at the very end, three more. *Statius, of Tharsis. Pansa, of Tharsis. Atilius, of Tharsis.* In the same voice as all the rest. Alana heard it from the rail. Nobody on deck looked round. It was only the litany, and they had all heard it before. Alana did not look round either. She had no need to.

She took the middle watch at the binnacle, because nobody else could read the stones as fast, and because she could not have slept. It was the *Tally*'s binnacle, a brass-bound box on its post by the tiller, with a new lid of Gaius's making, oiled horn instead of glass. Under the horn, in their straw, lay the stones. The Cyrene shard, aft. Corvus to the right hand, pulling north. She looked at Corvus once, at the change of the watch, and looked away; and then, because it was owed, she looked again and let it hurt. It did. She was glad of that, a little, and did not examine the gladness.

The Silverwood stone she held in her palm.

She did not need to. It would point as well from its bed. But it was warm as a hand, as the others were not, and it pulled against her fingers toward the bow, steadily, like a child at a sleeve; and her burned hand ached less with it there. She had not written that down. There was beginning to be a great deal she had not written down. Last night was at the head of it.

They had not spoken all day. A ship has no corners, and there had been near ninety souls on this one since dawn, and a horse, and Valeria being sick over the lee rail in good order.

Nerva was at the tiller three paces off. He had gone deaf as a post, she noticed, at the precise moment the prince came aft.

Marcus stood beside her at the binnacle. Not close. A hand's breadth off.

He had the green-fletched arrow in his fingers and turned it end over end, slowly, as he had turned it whenever his hands were idle since the mole.

"Still nobody?" she said.

"Still nobody."

"There was wool on the sea-door. The same green."

"I know." He put the arrow away inside his tunic, with the oilskin and the ninety-one and the nine and the boy at the bottom of the page, and, unless he had found somewhere else for them, her hairpins. The hand's breadth stayed where it was. The Grey went past them, soft and yellow and endless, and the ship talked to herself in the dark.

She had meant to say it at Cyrene. On the stair, in the cave, at the rail when the *Patience* went out, and on either of the two windless days after, when there had been nothing to do but say it. It was a very small thing. She had drafted it, and revised it, and it was still not right, and she said it anyway.

"I moved you," she said.

He turned his head.

"In the weather-book." She kept her eyes on the shard in her palm. "Out of the back."

He did not ask where to. She had been afraid he would, because she did not know. The line was drawn and the place was not, so tonight he was nowhere in the book at all. She could not have told him that. He did not ask her to.

He only stood there. After a while she heard him let out a breath, very slowly, the whole length of it: a breath he might have been holding since the beach.

---

She was aware of his hand.

It was on the brass rim of the binnacle beside hers. Not touching. The old distance: the distance they had kept on the *Clemency*, and at the mast, and in the warm surf on the black beach. She could feel it as she had felt it on the beach, like heat off a stove.

And the thought came back to her in the cold small voice it had come in then, word for word, as if it had been waiting under the horn with the stones.

*If he moves his hand an inch, I will not move mine.*

He did not move it.

He would not. She knew that now as surely as she knew the set of the Grey. He had not moved it at the water's edge, or at the mast, or by the well; on the dune last night he had not moved at all until she turned and gave him her hand. One night on a dune had not made it his to take. He would wait to be given it, every time, for as long as she chose.

*If he moves his hand an inch,* said the voice again, patiently, as though she had not heard it the first time.

*He won't,* she told it. *Be quiet.*

She moved hers.

More than an inch. She took her hand off the brass and laid it over the back of his, and felt him go still under it; and then, because that was not enough either, she turned his hand over on the rim and put her own into it, palm to palm, and closed her fingers through his, and held on.

Neither of them said anything. The Silverwood stone lay warm in her other palm and pulled toward the bow. The sea washed along the ship's side, and the lamp burned under the horn, and his hand shook in hers, a little, and then, after a while, it stopped.

She did not know if that was hers, or his.

---

**SYLARA**

*Sixteenth day. Going home.*

She lay in the dark of the hold, behind the straw, with the twelve.

She had come aboard on the last night, when the wind began, while the Ford slept in the fort and the slingers and their slow-eyed leader sat round a fire on the beach saying goodbye to the town for the second time, with less ceremony than the first. Up the bow cable, while the engineer cursed his pintle at the other end of the ship. Down the open hatch. Into the straw behind the two crates, which nobody would move, because nobody in their right mind would put their hands near those crates again. She had been right. In the morning men came down the hatch with the last of the water-casks and never once looked.

The smell came through the lead seals, faint. Sweet and black. The smell of the door. She breathed through her mouth.

The second time. She had come to Cyrene in this hold among the empty iron, with five men's poison on its way to be filled. She was going home in it among the full iron, with a prince of thieves taking it west for reasons no roof in Cyrene had let her hear. She had watched all of it from the roofs. She still did not understand how it had happened.

At the far end of the hold, in her stall, the grey mare shifted and blew. She had known Sylara was there from the first hour. She had looked round over her shoulder at the straw with one large mild eye, and then looked away, and told no one. Sylara had decided she liked her.

She counted her arrows by feel. Eleven.

She knew where the twelfth was. She had seen him turn it in his fingers on the stair at dawn, and ask every man on the mole, and put it away against his ribs when nobody would claim it.

She took out the book and opened it by feel at the second page, and ran her thumb along the one line where the lead had scored the bark. *He spared them.* She wet the lead, and held it over the page a while, and added nothing.

Above her, aft, through the planking, there had been two voices at the binnacle, the witch's low and precise, and the prince's. She had not caught the words. She had heard them stop. Now there was only the creak of the tiller and the wash under the counter, and somewhere over her head two people standing very still, saying nothing at all.

She did not write that down either.

Toward the end of the watch she went along the hold in the dark to the mare's stall, and the mare let her by, and she slid back the little mucking-port in the ship's side and put her face to the cold air.

The Grey went by close enough to touch. The sea ran along the hull, and the set of the Grey ran against them out of the west.

And on it, turning slowly over and over, a leaf went past. A single leaf, long and narrow, silver on both sides, catching what little light there was as it turned, the way a trout shows its flank in a stream. She watched it go by an arm's length away, and go aft, and go out. It had come a long way. So had she.

Then the smell came, so faint she could not have sworn to it, and did not need to: under the tar and the horse and the sweetness of the iron, pine resin, and leaf-mould, and the cold green breath under old trees at night.

It came out of the Grey ahead of them, from the west. She put her face into it and shut her eyes.

---

# Chapter Thirteen: The Boundary

**SYLARA**

*Seventeenth day. Second night in the hold. Somebody has been counting the water-casks.*

The healer came down the hatch at the change of the middle watch with a horn lantern shuttered to a slit, and Sylara knew it was the healer before the light did, because no one else aboard came down that ladder slowly.

The men came down it as men come down into a cellar where something has died. Fast, with their eyes on the rungs, and up again faster, with whatever they had been sent for clutched to their chests. They did not look at the crates. They did not look aft at the straw. Twice in two days Sylara had lain with her cheek on the boards and watched a pair of boots come within a long stride of her face and turn round and go up again, and both times she had heard the man let his breath out at the top of the ladder as if he had been holding it the whole way down.

The healer did not hurry. She set her feet on each rung, and stood at the bottom with the lantern held low, and let her eyes go round the hold as a woman's eyes go round a ward at night: the mare in her stall, the casks in their chocks, the two crates with SILVA painted on their lids in Tharsian red. Then she knelt on the boards beside the crates and set the lantern down and folded her hands, and began.

Sylara had heard it every dusk through the planking from the mainmast. She had not heard it from an arm's length.

The voice was low and did not rise. It used the old vowels of the capital, the ones they taught in the great houses and nowhere else, so that every name came out a little longer than the man who had worn it would have said it himself. *Manius Varenus, of the Fourteenth. Who sang. Tertius, son of Marcia, of the Fourteenth. Eighteen. Old Cotta, of the Fourteenth, the standard-bearer's man.* Name after name, at the pace of a slow walk. Sylara lay in the straw behind the crates with her bow under her and counted them, because counting was what she did with anything she could not stop. At sixty-one the healer laid her palm flat on the nearer lid. At eighty she took it away.

Then the town, street by street. Then a ship's dead. Then a boy who held a lamp.

Then the three.

*Statius, of Tharsis. Pansa, of Tharsis. Atilius, of Tharsis.*

The same voice. The same pace. The lamp did not so much as flicker.

Sylara found she had stopped breathing, and started again, slowly, through her mouth, because of the smell.

"You may as well come out," said the healer, without turning her head. "The mare has been looking at that straw for two days. I am not the only one on this ship who can read a horse. I am only the only one who comes down here."

---

She came out. There was nothing else to do in a hold, short of going through the hull.

The healer looked at her in the slit of light from the lantern: at the green hood, and the bow, and the knotted cord at her wrist, and her face, which Sylara knew was filthy, and thin, because she had been eating what she could steal from the water-casks' chocks, which was nothing. The healer looked at all of it in the order a healer looks at things. Sylara watched her do it. Bleeding first. Then breathing. Then the rest.

"You're the arrow," said the healer.

Sylara said nothing.

"The green one. On the stern rail. Through the grey man's hand." The healer's eyes went to the quiver. "Eleven. He has the twelfth inside his shirt. He turns it in his fingers when he thinks. He has asked every man on the mole who could make that shot in the dark, and every man said no, and he has not stopped asking." She considered. "He will be glad. In his way. He does not show it well."

"Are you going to tell him?"

"I haven't decided." The healer sat back on her heels. "You were on Cyrene."

"Ten days before the bell. Sixteen after."

"Where?"

"On the roofs, mostly." Sylara heard her own voice, rough with two days' disuse, and disliked it. "Above Netmakers' Row. Behind the steam from the vents. In the drain under the fort, once, for an hour."

"Above the water-gate stair?"

Sylara looked at her.

It was a long look. The lantern was between them, and its slit of light lay across the healer's hands, which were folded in her lap, quite still, long-fingered, scrubbed so clean the skin at the knuckles had gone thin and shiny. They were the steadiest hands Sylara had ever seen on a woman or a man. She had seen them on the stair, from the rocks under the fort's seaward wall, at the top of the tide, in the last light of the thirteenth day. She had seen the halt, and the big man with the keg on his shoulder at the railless lip, and the healer at the turn of the stair with her satchels. She had heard the name called down the stair, not loudly. She had seen the man turn his head.

And the next day she had seen a small bald man go backward off a ladder at the slips, from a roof three hundred paces off, and the day after that a big grey man go down on the black beach at the edge of a fire, with the healer kneeling by him and her two fingers on his eyes.

Three men who had never once looked at her as she went by in the mornings.

"Yes," said Sylara. "Above the stair."

The healer did not look away. She did not look frightened either. She looked, Sylara thought, like a woman being told the weather.

"And?"

"It's not in the report."

"Why not?"

Sylara thought about it, because it deserved thinking about. Down here in the dark it was the only true question anybody had asked her in ten years.

"Because it isn't about him," she said at last. "The report is about him. What he is. Whether he's worth the wood's trouble." She paused. "He didn't do it. He doesn't know."

"He knows," said the healer.

"Then it's his to write. Not mine."

The healer looked at her a while longer. Then she reached into the satchel at her hip and took out a heel of barley bread wrapped in a cloth, and a piece of hard cheese, and a small stoppered flask, and set them on the lid of the nearer crate between them, beside the red word.

"You'll want to drink that slowly," she said. "It's only water. Everything in this hold is not only water but that."

Sylara did not move.

"I could have poisoned it," the healer agreed. "I could have poisoned the cask you've been stealing from, too, and saved myself the bread." She got up off her knees, a little stiffly, and lifted the lantern. "I am not going to tell him. Not tonight. In two days we'll be in your wood and you can tell him yourself, in whatever way your people tell a man they've been lying on his roof for a month. I'd only spoil it." At the foot of the ladder she stopped. "The mare is called Thistle. The General named her to vex the captain. It is working."

Then she went up the ladder, slowly, as she had come down, and the hatch closed, and the dark came back.

Sylara ate the bread. She drank the water, slowly. She did not trust it, and drank it anyway, and lived; and she lay in the straw a long time afterward with her hand on the bow and her eyes open.

*Liora,* she thought. The name had been in the lanes of Cyrene for sixteen days. She had refused it, as she refused all their names, because names were how a watcher stopped watching. She tried it now in the dark, under her breath.

It fitted the woman about as well as *healer* did.

---

*Eighteenth day. They know.*

She did not need the planking to tell her. The planking told her anyway.

A ship has no corners, the old wardens said of the human navies, and that was why humans who went to sea came home either friends or murderers. Sylara had thought it a saying. Now she lay under the main hatch with her ear to a crack between two boards and found it was a law of nature, like the set of the Grey. Ninety people and a horse on a deck forty paces long. Nothing said on it went unheard by somebody. Nothing heard went unrepeated.

It began with the water.

The convict who kept the water-casks, a long sour man the others called Sallow, had been counting what came up the hatch against what went down it, and found it did not tally, and said so at the mainmast with a great deal of bitterness, as a man does who has been made to keep a thing and is blamed when it goes missing. Sylara had lain very still and waited for someone to come down with a lantern. Nobody did. A heavier voice told Sallow, after a pause, that it was the horse.

"The horse don't drink from a cask, Rufo. The horse drinks from a bucket."

"Then it's a bucket short. Mark it down."

"It's not a bucket short. It's the cask that's short. Three days and the forward cask's down a hand more than it should be. Somebody's at it. In the night."

There was a silence on the deck above.

"Who goes down that hatch in the night?" said somebody else, low.

Another silence. Sylara knew what was in it before the next voice filled it, because she had listened to men's silences from under the fern since she was nine, and this was the silence of men looking at one another and then not.

"She does," said the low voice. "For the flasks. Says them names over them."

"Then it's her," said Sallow. "Mark that down, then. *The healer's thirsty.*"

Nobody laughed.

"Leave it," said Rufo.

"I'm only saying—"

"I heard what you're only saying. Leave it, I said. Or go down and ask her yourself." The heavy voice did not rise. "In the night. In the dark. With them crates. You go and ask her about the water, Sallow, and I'll mark it on my stick after."

The deck went quiet over Sylara's head, very quiet, the way the wood goes quiet when the hawk is over it.

And then, in the quiet, very softly, as men say a thing they have said already among themselves and not yet aloud: "Three."

"Shut your mouth."

"I'm only counting. You count. You've marks for them on your stick. Statius off the stair. That bald one off the ladder. The sergeant at the fire with his heart gone." A pause. "Weed. Frost. Heart. She was standing there for every one, Rufo."

"She was standing there for the whole of the Fourteenth as well, and the town, and you when you had that bolt in your arse on the Kettle Bank. She stands there for everybody. It's her trade."

"Aye. That's what I mean."

The heavy voice said nothing at all. Sylara lay with her cheek on the boards and pictured him, the optio with the split shield, sitting on his bollard on the slips day after day with his stick in his hand, making his marks.

"She dressed my hand Tuesday," said a third voice, young, uncertain.

"Then keep it clean," said Rufo. "That's all I'll say. And not to her face. Any man who says one word of this to her face, I'll mark *him*."

After that, it was not said again where Sylara could hear it. That was how she knew it was being said everywhere else.

She watched it happen through the cracks in the deck, as she had watched the town of Cyrene learn to look at the eleven on the slips. Men moved. Nobody told them to. When the healer came along the deck in the mornings to see to Valeria, who was still being sick over the lee rail in good order and would accept no help from anyone, the men at the sheets found a rope that wanted coiling at the other end of the ship. When she sat at the mainmast to sort her satchels, the space round her widened, a pace, two paces, as water widens round a stone. Nobody looked at her. Everybody knew where she was. She was the stone in the middle of the stream, and the ship parted round her and closed again behind.

Sylara knew that space. She had lived her whole life inside it, among the elves.

It was not hatred. It was what grows up around a thing you cannot measure. Fear, and under the fear, a kind of awful respect, like the respect a village keeps for a deep pool where a child once drowned.

The healer did not seem to notice. She sat in the middle of her space and rolled bandages, and if her hands paused once in an hour, Sylara did not see it.

That night she came down the ladder at the middle watch as before, and knelt by the crates and said the names, and set down bread and water on the lid beside the red word, and went up again without a word. The space did not follow her into the hold. There was no one in the hold to make it, except Sylara, and Sylara did not move away.

She ate the bread. She thought about that for a long time.

*Eighteenth day,* she wrote, by feel, on a fresh leaf. *The thieves have found out what she is. They keep a pace off her and do not look. She has not noticed. She has noticed.*

And under it, slower: *She brings me bread.*

---

*Nineteenth day. Silverwood.*

She felt it before the stones did.

All morning the Grey had been thinning. She knew it by the light through the mucking-port, which had gone from the yellow of old fleece to the white of new, and by the sound of the water along the hull, which had a bottom to it now, a shallow, talking note. The smell came stronger with every glass that turned on deck: pine, and leaf-mould, and the cold sweet rot of a forest floor in winter, and under it the green iron smell of the spring that breathed up through the whole island, as Cyrene's did, but softer, the way a sleeper breathes. She lay with her face to the port and drank it like water.

Above her the witch's voice said, "*There*," as a woman says it when the last figure of a sum comes right.

And the mare, who had stood two days looking at her planking in betrayal, lifted her head and whickered once, very softly, at the hull.

Sylara slid the port wider. The Grey drew aside like a curtain, and there it was.

She had seen it come up out of the fog a thousand times. She had never seen it from the sea before. She had always been inside it.

It came up dark at first, a long shadow on the water, low at the ends and lifting in the middle like the back of a beast asleep. Then the shadow had a colour, and the colour was not green but silver: the silver of the leaves, which did not fall in winter but turned on their stems in the least wind so that the whole island shimmered like the flank of a running fish. The trees stood up out of the shore-sand on their great grey trunks, a hundred feet without a branch, as straight and as close as the pillars of a hall; and above the pillars the silver roof went up and up toward the middle of the island, where the oldest trees stood, until it seemed to Sylara, lying at the port with her face wet and the salt on her lips, that the whole island was one great tree with the sea round its roots.

Up on deck the thieves had gone quiet.

She heard one of them say, "*Gods*," in a small voice, the way a boy says it in a temple. Nobody laughed at him.

Then the engineer, flat and dry, from the stern: "I'd want a hundred and ten feet out of that one. Without a knot."

"No," said the prince.

"I'm only measuring."

"Measure with your eyes, then. And keep your hands in your belt."

The ship came round into the bay on the western shore, where the *Tally* had always called, with the pine on one side and a long spit of black rock on the other, and Sylara watched the anchor go down through the clear green water in a shiver of bubbles and take the sand.

The western shore. Where the five came ashore with their iron every seventh day, and walked up through the stumps to the western glade.

She had not forgotten the stumps. She had not been able to forget them since she was nine years old. But she had never thought of them from the sea, the way a human would see them from the deck of a ship coming in, and she saw them now as the prince must be seeing them from the stern, because she watched his face through a crack in the planking at the after end of the hold, and saw it change.

All along the shore behind the beach, under the first of the standing trees, the ground was covered with them. Hundreds. Grey and silver, weathered to the color of old bone, each as wide across as a farmhouse table, cut level at the height of a man's waist with a two-man saw. Sixty winters had not rotted them. The pine of Silverwood was too full of its own resin to rot.
